Clinician Did: Pearl #7: Substance Hierarchy-"We cannot treat depression you cannot feel while you're using cannabis daily." Harm reduction approach: cannabis reduction as first goal (not abstinence immediately, but reduction from daily to 3x/week initially).

Clinician Did Not: Negotiate job hunting or educational planning while sleep was inverted and cannabis use was daily.

2. Regulation

Clinician Did: Pearl #3: Circadian Architecture. Parents stopped enabling the inverted schedule. No food delivery to room after 10 PM. Wifi turned off at midnight (parents' house, parents' rules). Fixed wake time (10 AM initially, then gradually shifted to 8 AM over 8 weeks).

Clinician Did Not: Try to "inspire" him or explore "why" he was stuck. Motivation follows structure; structure does not follow motivation.

3. Developmental Task (Autonomy + Responsibility)

Clinician Did: Pearl #29: The Two-Choices Rule. "You can live here under these conditions [wake time, contribute to household chores, reduce cannabis to 3x/week], or you can find another living situation. Both are valid. You choose."

Clinician Did Not: Offer a third choice (live here and do nothing). The enabling ended. We treated this as a structural intervention, not an ultimatum.

4. Family Role

Clinician Did: Pearl #31: Manager-to-Consultant Shift. Parents stopped reminding, nagging, and rescuing. They provided the structure (wake time, wifi limits) but did not emotionally manage his response. Script to parents: "Your anxiety about his future is real. Acting on that anxiety by doing things for him keeps him stuck. You provide the structure; he provides the effort. If he doesn't, the discomfort is his to manage."

Clinician Did Not: Ask parents to "motivate" him, or rescue him from the natural discomfort of boredom and structure.

5. Intensity

Clinician Did: Tier 2 (Standard outpatient). Weekly sessions (patient + parents separately every other week).

Clinician Did Not: Escalate to hospitalization (no acute safety risk) or intensive outpatient (patient would not have engaged).

Phase 3: Family Positioning

Parents struggled with Pearl #26: Parent Panic Syndrome-"If we set limits, he'll become suicidal or end up homeless."

We reframed: "He is currently 'homeless' in your basement-disconnected from life, purpose, and meaning. The structure you're providing now enables that. You're not abandoning him by setting limits. You're refusing to participate in his stagnation."

Pearl #58: The Autonomy Firewall-we positioned the parents as holders of house rules, not therapists. Script: "You're not trying to fix him. You're setting the conditions under which living at home is sustainable for everyone. He gets to decide whether those conditions work for him."

Phase 4: Setbacks or Stressors

Event: Patient expressed suicidal ideation when wifi was restricted.

Setback Type: Compensation Fatigue / Extinction Burst (patient testing whether limits would hold).

Response:

Clinician intervention: Assessed acuity. Determined risk was low (no intent, no plan, no means, statement was reactive to frustration, not despair). Script to patient: "I'm hearing that you're really angry about the wifi restriction. That makes sense-structure feels controlling when you're used to none. I need to know: are you actually considering ending your life, or are you expressing how frustrating this is?"

Patient acknowledged it was frustration, not suicidal intent.

Pearl #6: Dignity Over Compliance-we validated his frustration ("This is hard. I get it") without removing the structure.

Parents held the line (Pearl #11: Clinician as Container-we held their anxiety so they could hold the structure). Script to parents: "He's testing whether you mean it. If you back down now, he learns that distress threats override house rules. Hold the limit. We're monitoring safety."

Phase 5: Outcome & Teaching Points

Outcome: Patient moved into a sober living house (subsidized by parents for first 3 months, then transitioned to part-time work to cover rent). Reduced cannabis use to occasional (weekends only). Enrolled in community college (one class per semester initially). Sleep normalized. Depression symptoms improved without medication changes (environmental structure was the primary intervention). Parents transitioned to Consultant role.

Teaching Points:

Biology came first. Sleep and cannabis use were addressed before any discussion of "life goals" or "career."

Enabling was reframed as maintaining the problem, not preventing catastrophe. Parents needed explicit permission to stop rescuing.

Autonomy required structure. The patient did not "find motivation" and then engage. Structure was imposed; motivation followed.

The extinction burst (suicidal statement) was anticipated and managed without removing limits. Testing boundaries is developmentally normal; backing down reinforces that distress prevents accountability.

Environmental change (sober living) was more therapeutic than additional therapy sessions. The milieu was the intervention. Setting biological limits (sleep, cannabis) created the friction necessary for developmental movement.

Chapter Synthesis: The Invariant Logic Across Cases

These four cases-first-episode psychosis, bipolar mania, complex trauma, and developmental stagnation-span different diagnoses, settings, and family structures. Yet the sequencing logic was identical in every case.

The Five Non-Negotiable Principles

1. Biology First

In every case, we addressed the biological floor before attempting relational or insight work. Sleep, substance use, and medication stability were prerequisites, not optional variables. Psychosis, mania, trauma, and cannabis dependence all required biological stabilization before the brain could engage in therapy, family work, or planning.

2. Regulation Before Negotiation

In every case, we assessed and addressed the family's emotional temperature (system state) before attempting collaborative interventions. Red and Yellow states require containment and structure, not insight or problem-solving. Family panic was treated as a clinical phenomenon, not dismissed as "overreaction."

3. Safety Before Autonomy

In Cases 1 and 3, safety concerns (psychosis-driven fear, self-harm) required temporarily restricting autonomy. We did not apologize for this or frame it as "taking away rights." Safety is the foundation upon which all other developmental work rests. Autonomy comes later, when the brain can manage it.

4. Structure Before Motivation

In every case, structure was imposed before the patient "felt ready." Motivation does not precede action in psychiatry; it follows it. We created the environmental conditions (sleep architecture, cannabis reduction, house rules) and allowed motivation to emerge from competence and rhythm.

5. Family Positioning as Clinical Intervention

In every case, how we positioned the family-Manager, Consultant, Observer, Environmental Architect-was as important as medication or therapy. Families in the wrong role destabilize treatment. Families in the right role become the treatment structure. We spent as much time managing family arousal and role confusion as we did managing patient symptoms.

What Mis-Sequencing Would Have Caused

If we had attempted insight work with the psychotic patient before sleep stabilized, we would have increased paranoia and treatment refusal.

If we had included high-conflict parents in the trauma patient's treatment prematurely, we would have re-traumatized the patient and prolonged the crisis.

If we had explored "motivation" with the stalled-launch patient before addressing cannabis and sleep, we would have enabled another year of stagnation.

If we had negotiated discharge planning with the manic patient during acute episode, we would have prolonged the mania and endangered the patient.

Sequence is not a suggestion. It is the difference between stabilization and prolonged crisis.

The Clinician's Task

The clinician's task in family-informed psychiatry is not to do everything. It is to determine what to do first, and what to deliberately not do until the foundation is stable.

This requires:

Tolerating family distress without acting prematurely (holding the container)

Withholding interventions the family is demanding (saying "not yet" to insight work, discharge, autonomy expansion)

Imposing structure the patient resists (sleep architecture, substance limits, means restriction)

Positioning families in roles they may not want (moving from Manager to Observer, from Co-Clinician to Environmental Architect)

The cases in this chapter demonstrate that good outcomes do not require perfect families, perfect patients, or ideal resources. They require disciplined sequencing, clear role definition, and the clinical courage to hold the structure until the system can hold itself.

CHAPTER 17: TEACHING & SCALING THE MODEL

Fidelity Under Pressure

The critical challenge for any clinical model is not when it is rejected, but when it is adopted as a checklist.

When a residency program, an inpatient unit, or a group practice adopts the Predictable Milieu Model (PMM), the temptation is to strip it down to its most visible components: the Scripts and the Pearls. We hand trainees a list of "what to say" and assume they are practicing the model.

This is a category error. The PMM is not a collection of scripts; it is a discipline of sequencing. If a resident uses Script S.12 (The Two-Choices Rule) to force a decision on a patient who has not slept in three days, they are not practicing the model-they are practicing control without containment. They have the words right, but the sequence wrong.

This final chapter addresses the meta-task of the senior clinician: how to transmit this operating system to others without losing the structural logic that ensures safety. It is a guide to preventing the "drift" that turns clinical architecture into bureaucratic rigidity.

17.1 Why Good Models Fail at Scale

Clinical models degrade at scale because nuance is inefficient. Large systems crave standardization. They want a protocol that can be applied by a rotating staff of nurses, social workers, and residents with perfect uniformity.

However, the PMM relies on the assessment of capacity, which varies moment to moment.

Checklist Creep

When we teach this model, we often see "Checklist Creep." The team creates a checkbox for "Family Meeting" or "Sleep Assessment." The clinician checks the box ("I asked about sleep") but fails to act on the data ("He isn't sleeping, but I started family therapy anyway because it was on the checklist"). The form is complete, but the sequence is violated.

Loss of Timing

The "magic" of the PMM is not the intervention itself, but the timing of the intervention. A "Respect Line" set at the right moment is containment; set at the wrong moment, it is provocation. Scaling often preserves the what but loses the when.

Over-Simplification

To make the model teachable, we simplify. We teach "Biology First" as a slogan. In practice, biology is complex. If a team interprets "Biology First" to mean "We never talk to the patient until they are discharged," they have drifted into therapeutic neglect.

17.2 What Must Be Taught First

When training residents or staff, the instinct is to teach the "active" tools first-how to run a family meeting, how to set a boundary. We must resist this.

We must teach Sequencing Before Scripts.

Before a trainee is allowed to use a script to negotiate a behavioral contract, they must demonstrate the ability to assess the biological floor.

The Test: "Tell me why you are not doing family therapy today."

The Correct Answer: "Because Pearl #1: Sleep Supremacy is not met. The patient slept 4 hours. We are holding the frame until sleep is restored."

We teach Restraint Before Action.

In a high-acuity system, the most powerful intervention is often the refusal to intervene until the conditions are right. We teach trainees to stand in the storm and do nothing but hold the frame (Pearl #11: Clinician as Container). This is harder than doing therapy.

17.3 Teaching Judgment

How do we teach the judgment to know when to break a rule or when to hold it? We teach through the framework of "Not Yet."

Teaching "Not Yet"

When a resident wants to discharge a patient who has just agreed to take meds (but hasn't taken them yet), we do not say "No." We ask: "Where are we in the sequence?"

Trainee: "He agreed to the plan."

Supervisor: "Agreement is a Task (Level 3). Adherence is a Behavior (Level 1). Do we have Level 1 evidence yet?"

Trainee: "Not yet."

Teaching Stop-Rules

We explicitly train the "Stop-Rules" from Chapter 8.

"If the parent begins to scream, what do you do?"

Incorrect Answer: "I try to de-escalate them."

Correct Answer: "I pause the meeting because the container has breached."

We grade the trainee not on their ability to solve the crisis, but on their ability to recognize when the crisis has rendered the current intervention unworkable.

17.4 Supervision as Protection

In this model, supervision is not just education; it is a containment structure. The supervisor functions for the trainee exactly as the trainee functions for the patient.

Slowing Urgency

Trainees absorb the urgency of the system. They come to supervision vibrating with Pearl #26: Parent Panic Syndrome, passed down from the family.

The Supervisor's Job: Apply Pearl #24: Bidirectional Regulation. We slow the cadence. We lower the volume. "You are speaking very fast. Let's pause. What is the actual risk right now?"

Re-Anchoring Sequence

When a trainee is stuck ("I don't know what to do with this family"), it is almost always because they are mis-sequenced.

The Intervention: "You are trying to solve a Layer 3 problem (Family Role) with a Layer 2 deficit (Regulation). Drop down a level. What does the regulation need?"

Supervision protects the trainee from the shame of "not fixing it" by reframing the goal from "cure" to "fidelity to the sequence."

17.5 Team-Based Containment

The PMM fails if the doctor practices it but the nursing staff does not. If the doctor sets a "Respect Line" and the nurse negotiates it, the patient will split the team to seek safety (Pearl #28: Splitting is Safety-Seeking).

Shared Language

The entire team must share the Pearl language. "Biological Floor," "Manager-to-Consultant," and "Respect Lines" must be the vernacular of the shift report.

Shift Report: "Patient is in a Level 1 Biological hold (sleep). No Level 3 interventions (meetings) today."

Authority Clarity

We must be explicit about who holds the frame. If the social worker promises a discharge date that the doctor has not cleared, the predictability of the milieu collapses.

Rule: "We do not promise outcomes (Pearl #53: Outcome Realism); we promise processes. 'The team will discuss it' is the only promise we make alone."

Preventing Splitting

When a patient tells the nurse, "The doctor doesn't care about me," the team must recognize this not as a complaint, but as a structural stress test.

Response: "The doctor and I speak the same language. We are both focused on your sleep right now."

17.6 Institutional Translation

Institutions (hospitals, insurance companies) value throughput and speed. The PMM values sequencing and stability. These goals often conflict.

Scaling Under Throughput Pressure

When the administration demands a 3-day length of stay, we cannot abandon the sequence. We accelerate the assessment, not the intervention.

Translation: "We cannot force insight in 3 days. We can, however, stabilize the Biological Floor and set up an outpatient PMM."

We sell the model to administration not as "better therapy," but as "readmission prevention."

"If we discharge this patient before the sequence is stable, they will bounce back in 48 hours. Holding them for 24 more hours to secure the handoff saves 7 days of readmission costs."

Protecting Timing

The senior clinician must act as the shield for the sequencing. We translate the "pressure to fix" into "structural requirements."

"I understand the bed is needed. However, discharging a sleepless patient violates safety protocols. We will discharge when Pearl #1 is satisfied."

17.7 Common Failure Modes

As the model scales, watch for these specific corruptions:

Pearl Misapplication

Staff begins to use Pearls to dismiss patients or families.

Corruption: "That mother is just in Parent Panic Syndrome, so I ignored her."

Correction: Pearl #6: Dignity as Strategy. PPS is a sign of suffering, not a license to dismiss. We address the fear, we don't diminish it.

Script Misuse (Roboticism)

Trainees read scripts verbatim without reading the room.

Corruption: Delivering the "Two-Choices Rule" to a patient who is actively psychotic and terrified.

Correction: "Scripts require a Green-State system (Chapter 8). You cannot run a script on a Red-State brain."

Moralization

The team starts to view the "structure" as a moral good and "resistance" as a moral failing.

Corruption: "He didn't earn his phone privileges, so he gets nothing."

Correction: Structure is for safety, not punishment. If the structure feels punitive, it is no longer therapeutic.

17.8 Ethical Protection

The PMM is a powerful tool for behavior shaping. Like any powerful tool, it can be misapplied.

Dignity as Guardrail

The ultimate check on the model is Pearl #6: Dignity as Strategy. If an intervention creates shame, it is wrong, even if it follows the sequence.

Test: "Would I tolerate this structure being applied to my own family member in a crisis?" If the answer is no, the application is too rigid.

Outcome Realism

We must teach Pearl #53: Outcome Realism to protect against the hubris of "fixing." The goal of the model is not to create a perfect citizen; it is to create a survivable environment for a struggling human. If we scale the model expecting it to cure schizophrenia or erase trauma, we will harm when it fails. We scale it to provide traction, not miracles.

17.9 Chapter Synthesis

The Predictable Milieu Model is not a rigid cage; it is a trellis. It provides the structure upon which the organic growth of the patient can occur.

To teach it, you must embody it. You must be the container for your trainees. You must respect the sequence of their learning. You must prioritize their regulation over their throughput.

The model endures not because it is written in a manual, but because it is lived in the nervous system of the clinician. When you stand in the storm of a psychiatric crisis and refuse to panic, refuse to shame, and refuse to rush-you are the intervention. The manual is just the map; you are the territory.

End of the Clinical Core

APPENDIX B: THE CLINICAL PEARLS HANDBOOK (MASTER INDEX)

Status: Finalized for Publication

This index organizes the core heuristics of the Relational Continuum Manual. These Pearls function as the "Clinical Decision Support Layer"1, designed for rapid recall during complex clinical moments.

DOMAIN I: BIOLOGICAL & FOUNDATIONAL (Pearls #1-10)

The non-negotiables of the Predictable Milieu Model.

Pearl #1: Sleep Supremacy
"Two nights of <4 hours sleep is a psychiatric emergency." 2

Pearl #2: The Biological Floor
"Meaningful therapy is severely limited with a starving or exhausted brain." 3

Pearl #3: Circadian Architecture
"Wake time anchors the day; sleep time follows wake time." 4

Pearl #4: The Substance Hierarchy
"Address chemical volatility before relational volatility." 5

Pearl #5: The 72-Hour Rule
"In acute phases, survival takes precedence over insight." 6

Pearl #6: Dignity as Strategy
"Shame fuels resistance; dignity lowers arousal." 7

Pearl #7-10: [Reserved].

DOMAIN II: THE RELATIONAL FRAME (Pearls #11-20)

How we hold the room and the relationship.

Pearl #11: The Clinician as Container
"You function as a stabilizing external regulator until their system settles." 8

Pearl #12: Respect Lines vs. Boundaries
"Respect Lines define what you will tolerate; Boundaries define what you will do." 9

Pearl #13: Unsolicited Advice Often Lands as Intrusion
"Advice given without permission often triggers defensiveness and resistance. Asking permission preserves autonomy and engagement." 10

Pearl #14: Observation Before Interpretation
"Describe the behavior before assigning meaning." 11

Pearl #15: Grief as the Hidden Variable
"Rage and resistance are often grief in disguise." 12

Pearl #16: The Autonomy Gate
"You cannot force growth; you can only water the soil." 13

Pearl #17-20: [Reserved].

DOMAIN III: FAMILY SYSTEMS DYNAMICS (Pearls #21-30)

Reading the system mechanics.

Pearl #21: The Identified Patient
"The symptom bearer is the signal, not the source." 14

Pearl #22: Chosen Family
"For LGBTQ+ and trauma survivors, biology does not equal bond." 15

Pearl #23: The Developmental Mismatch
"Parents see the child that was; the system sees the adult that is, within the family's cultural and developmental expectations." 16

Pearl #24: Bidirectional Regulation
"Regulation flows bidirectionally between patient and family." 17

Pearl #25: The Protest Polka
"Demand leads to withdrawal; withdrawal leads to demand." 18

Pearl #26: Parent Panic Syndrome (PPS)
"Fear looks like control; address the fear to lower the control." 19

Pearl #27: The Reassurance Trap
"Reassurance feeds anxiety; prediction starves it." 20

Pearl #28: Splitting is Safety-Seeking
"When the system divides staff, it is seeking stability, not trouble." 21

Pearl #29-30: [Reserved].

DOMAIN IV: INTERVENTION & CHANGE (Pearls #31-50)

Doing the work.

Pearl #31: The Manager-to-Consultant Shift
"Move from directing the life to consulting on the life." 22

Pearl #32: Permission-Based Influence
"Would you be open to hearing a concern?" 23

Pearl #33: The Two-Choices Rule
"Autonomy requires options; rarely offer just one path." 24

Pearl #34: The 5-Step Setback Protocol
"Treat regression as data, not failure." 25

Pearl #35: Micro-Success Tracking
"One meal, one shower, one hour. Track the atoms of change." 26

Pearl #36: The "Runway" Metaphor
"They are circling because the system has not yet built a safe place to land." 27

Pearl #37: Compassionate Disengagement
"When helping hurts, stepping back is the intervention." 28

Pearl #38: The Medication Triangle
"Medication is a relationship between Patient, Prescriber, and Family." 29

Pearl #39-50: [Reserved].

DOMAIN V: SUSTAINABILITY (Pearls #51-60)

Protecting the clinician.

Pearl #51: The 3-Minute Reset
"Regulate yourself before you regulate the room." 30

Pearl #52: Countertransference as Data
"Your boredom, anger, or fear can be information about the system." 31

Pearl #53: Outcome Realism
"We plant trees we may never see shade from." 32

Pearl #54-60: [Reserved].

This is the correct move. Locking in the Master Index for the Pearls and Scripts now prevents "blind writing" later. It ensures that when you draft Chapter 5, you can confidently write "See Pearl #1" or "Use Script S.3" knowing exactly what those refer to.

DOMAIN III: FAMILY SYSTEMS DYNAMICS (Pearls #21-30)

Reading the system.

Pearl #21: The Identified Patient - The symptom bearer is the signal, not the source13.

Pearl #22: Chosen Family - For LGBTQ+ and trauma survivors, biology does not equal bond1414.

Pearl #23: The Developmental Mismatch - Parents see the child that was; the system sees the adult that is15151515.

Pearl #24: Bidirectional Regulation - The family regulates the patient; the patient regulates the family16.

Pearl #25: The Protest Polka - Demand leads to withdrawal; withdrawal leads to demand17.

Pearl #26: Parent Panic Syndrome (PPS) - Fear looks like control; address the fear to lower the control181818.

Pearl #27: The Reassurance Trap - Reassurance feeds anxiety; prediction starves it19191919.

Pearl #28: Splitting is Safety-Seeking - When the system divides staff, it is seeking stability, not trouble20.

Pearl #29-30: [Reserved].

DOMAIN IV: INTERVENTION & CHANGE (Pearls #31-50)

Doing the work.

Pearl #31: The Manager-to-Consultant Shift - Move from directing the life to consulting on the life21212121.

Pearl #32: Permission-Based Influence - "Would you be open to hearing a concern?"22222222.

Pearl #33: The Two-Choices Rule - Autonomy requires options; never offer just one path23.

Pearl #34: The 5-Step Setback Protocol - Treat regression as data, not failure24242424.

Pearl #35: Micro-Success Tracking - One meal, one shower, one hour. Track the atoms of change25252525.

Pearl #36: The "Runway" Metaphor - They are circling because there is nowhere to land26262626.

Pearl #37: Compassionate Disengagement - When helping hurts, stepping back is the intervention27.

Pearl #38: The Medication Triangle - Medication is a relationship between Patient, Prescriber, and Family28282828.

Pearl #46: (Reassigned to #35 for grouping) [Note: Source listed #46 as Micro-successes; moved to #35 to keep domains contiguous].

DOMAIN V: SUSTAINABILITY (Pearls #51-60)

Protecting the clinician.

Pearl #51: The 3-Minute Reset - Regulate yourself before you regulate the room29292929.

Pearl #52: Countertransference as Data - Your boredom, anger, or fear is information about the system30303030.

Pearl #53: Outcome Realism - We plant trees we may never see shade from31.

Pearl #54-55: [Reserved for supervision/scaling questions mentioned in source].

APPENDIX C: SCRIPTS & MICRO-SCRIPTS LIBRARY (MASTER INDEX)

Status: Locked for Drafting

These scripts will be referenced in the chapters as [Script S.X].

CATEGORY 1: THE FRAME (Opening, Closing, Boundaries)

S.1: Opening the Family Meeting - Sets agenda, lowers urgency, establishes the "No Blame" rule.3232.

S.2: Closing the Meeting (The Summary) - Synthesizes themes, assigns homework, confirms next steps.3333.

S.3: Setting Respect Lines - "I want to hear you, but I cannot hear you when the volume is this high."34343434.

S.4: The "Time-Out" Call - Stopping a session that has become unsafe or unproductive.35.

S.5: Contracting for Safety - Establishing the rules of engagement for the inpatient stay.36.

CATEGORY 2: THE WORK (Translation, Meaning, Grief)

S.6: The "Manager to Consultant" Proposal - Teaching parents how to resign from the job of General Manager.37373737.

S.7: Grief-Informed Inquiry - "What have you lost since this illness began?"38383838.

S.8: Translating Symptom to Meaning - "When he stops talking, what is the story you tell yourself about why?"39393939.

S.9: The "Runway" Explanation - Explaining the Holding Pattern concept to frustrated parents.40404040.

S.10: Micro-Success Validation - Pointing out the small win the family missed.41414141.

CATEGORY 3: FRICTION (Conflict, Interrupting, Redirection)

S.11: Interrupting the Blame Cycle - "Hold on a moment. I want to understand that, but I need to slow us down."42424242.

S.12: The Two-Choices Offer - Navigating an impasse by offering two acceptable paths.43.

S.13: Redirecting Parent Panic - "I see how scared you are. Right now, your fear is driving the car, and we need to put logic back in the driver's seat."444444.

S.14: Compassionate Disengagement - "We are working harder than you are right now. We need to step back so you can step up."45.

S.15: Addressing Splitting - "It seems you're hearing different things from different team members. Let's get everyone in one room to clarify."46.

CATEGORY 4: DECISIONS (Medication, Risk, Discharge)

S.16: The Medication Recommendation - Presenting meds as a tool for traction, not a cure.47474747.

S.17: The Refusal Pivot - When a patient refuses meds: "Okay, we respect that. What is your plan for sleep/safety without them?"48484848.

Red | De-escalation, safety planning | Insight, problem-solving | Containment, time-out, reschedule

Total Score | Interpretation | Treatment Intensity

15-18 | High functioning | Outpatient, minimal family involvement

10-14 | Moderate functioning | Outpatient, active family involvement

5-9 | Low functioning | Intensive outpatient or partial hospitalization

0-4 | Severe impairment | Inpatient or residential care

Temperature | Indicators | Intervention Approach

Cold | Silence, avoidance, no eye contact | Warm the room-validate, normalize, build safety

Cool | Polite, distant, minimal emotion | Structure and psychoeducation

Warm | Engaged, some tension, manageable | Collaborative problem-solving

Hot | Raised voices, interruptions, defensiveness | Slow down, contain, validate

Boiling | Shouting, threats, walking out | De-escalate, reschedule if needed

## Source: `00_START_HERE/_source/Email_Draft_to_Russell_and_Wright.docx`

EMAIL DRAFT - Friendly Version (with safety audit acknowledgement)

Copy text below into Outlook / Teams email.



To:    Philip Russell, MD

Cc:    Angelique Wright

From:  Joshua Moss, MD

Subject: Some BHU ideas for the $500K Annual Fund - would love your eyes on them



Hi Phil and Angelique,

Joy's announcement about the $500K Annual Giving Fund got me thinking, and I ended up putting together a fairly comprehensive set of proposals for BHU improvements. Wanted to share with both of you before anything goes to Kelsey.

Quick overview - there are 14 requests totaling ~$305K, so we're well under the $500K cap with room to maneuver:

5 bigger asks:

  Outdoor therapeutic space (Phase 1 feasibility + Tier 1 patio) - $100K

  Therapeutic meeting suite - solves our 2-windowless-rooms problem for family meetings - $85K

  Telehealth-only carts (Teams Rooms-certified, can pair with the suite) - $15K

  Library + audio engagement program - $28K

  Staff restoration room (24/7 access, night-shift equity) - $20K

9 smaller asks ($57K total): dignity closet (BHU-safe clothing/hygiene), free gun locks at discharge, pill organizers, psychoed displays + supervised iPads, sleep/circadian infrastructure, LGBTQ+ affirming kit, foot + hair care programming, patient memorial book + recovery library, multifaith spiritual cart.

Each one has a pre-filled MaineHealth request form, full SBAR, and (for the larger ones) a procurement-ready itemized list with vendors and pricing.

Quick note on inpatient safety:

I did a clinical safety audit pass over the whole portfolio with our actual unit constraints in mind - ligature, sharps, supervision realities. Several items needed to be re-engineered (e.g., the sleep kit replaced blackout curtains and elastic-strap masks with integrated blinds-between-glass and hardwired fixtures; the psychoed cart became wall-mounted displays + supervised tablets at the nurses' station; the audio piece dropped earbuds in favor of dayroom speakers; the spiritual cart dropped prayer beads). Each affected SBAR documents the revision and the clinical reasoning. The full audit lives in the Strategy Documents folder if you want the detail ("04_Inpatient_Safety_Audit_and_Revisions").

What I could use from each of you:

Phil - your clinical eyes on these, especially the three meatiest ones (Outdoor, Meeting Suite, Library + Audio). If we go forward, you'd be the signatory on the request forms. Want to grab 30 minutes to walk through them, or would you rather I send a couple of the SBARs over for you to skim first?

Angelique - there are a handful of data points I'd love your help pulling so the SBAR backgrounds have real numbers behind them:

  Restraint/seclusion events per 1000 patient-days (12-month rolling)

  12-month turnover rate (overall + first-18-month if you have it)

  Workplace-violence events YTD

  Family-meeting completion rate per discharge in 2025 (and % with remote attendees, if available)

  Any candidate adjacent space for a 3rd family meeting room

  Candidate space for a 24/7 staff restoration room

I've flagged each one in the relevant SBARs as "[NEED INPUT - Angelique Wright]" so it's clean to drop in whatever you find.

Quick note on TIC training:

I'd originally bundled formal TIC/Safewards cohort training with the restoration room, but the realistic timing for a 2026 cohort doesn't work - I've descoped that piece and we can revisit it in a future cycle. The restoration room is moving forward as a standalone $20K request.

Files:

I'll share the whole folder via OneDrive/SharePoint shortly. README at the top is a quick one-pager; each request has its own subfolder so you can dip into whichever ones interest you.

Still open:

I'm planning to ask Joy whether this is capital-only, operating-only, or both (matters because subscriptions are operating but construction is capital - could change which items qualify). Let me know if either of you already has the answer.

Let me know what works for a quick sync - even 15-20 minutes of orientation would be useful and we can go async from there.

Thanks!

Josh

Joshua Moss, MD    Behavioral Health Unit

Joshua.Moss@Mainehealth.com    207-490-7751

## Source: `02_Clinical_Skills/Documentation/_source/Faculty_Facilitator_Guide_1page.docx`

ONE-PAGE FACULTY FACILITATOR GUIDE

From Symptom to System - Facilitator Quick Sheet

Adult psychiatry residents * 60-75 min * case-based family systems didactic

Common derailments -> quick fixes

Companion files: slide deck (with speaker notes), faculty teaching script + answer keys, resident handout, pocket guide, 5 diagrams, references, AI image-prompt pack.  Joshua Moss, MD | Psychiatrist

BEFORE YOU START   Decide polling method (app or hands-up).   Print handout + pocket guide per resident.   Set pods of 3-4.   Whiteboard ready for 3 draw-alongs.   Pick your cut (below) based on the clock. FACILITATION STANCE   Discussion-heavy; you talk <40% of the time.   Protect the role-play - the doing is what transfers.   Keep the case de-identified; redirect real PHI.   Return often to: "How do I run a better meeting tomorrow?" | TWO TIMING CUTS 60 min: run ONE interactive block (exercise OR role-play); compress the lenses (slides 11-13) to 5 min; board Qs as quick cold-calls. 75 min: run BOTH interactive blocks fully; close with the 5 board questions as a lightning round. POLL ANSWER KEY   P1 (when to meet): B - early & deliberate.   P2 (high prior function): B - more curious/concerned.   P3 (religion): both content + relational theme.   P4 (acute lever): restore sleep.   P5 (real plan): specific, assigned, means-safe.

If this happens... | Do this

Group debates theology | Redirect to function: "What is the religious worry doing in this family?" Park the doctrine.

Residents pile on the spouse | Model circular language; reframe to over-/under-functioning reciprocity.

"She's too sick for family work" | Agree on acuity, then use the acuity rule: brief, supportive, operational - not interpretive.

Plans stay vague | Force owner + time + observable trigger for every task; demand the firearm plan.

Someone discloses real PHI | Thank them, redirect to the de-identified teaching case, move on.

### Slide 1
David V Sheehan MD MBA
Distinguished University Health Professor Emeritus
University of South Florida College of Medicine

Quality Psychiatric Care:
Accurate Diagnosis & Measurement - Based  Care

### Slide 2
Things to Know about These Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 9/18/2020.

### Slide 3
Measurement Based Care

All the slides and content in this presentation are copyright by DV Sheehan and may not be copied or reproduced without written permission

                                                    Copyright Sheehan DV 2010.  All rights reserved.

### Slide 4
Learning Objectives

Review the rationale for and use of rating scales and structured diagnostic interviews for major depressive disorder and their value in treating patients and bringing about more positive outcomes
Utilize rating scales to effectively identify and implement specific treatment options that address residual symptoms such as disability, functional impairment, and suicidality
Appreciate the extent to which rating scales are used in clinical practice by a sample of their peers, and discuss the major sources of objection to the use of rating scales and structured diagnostic interviews

### Slide 5
Measurement

"If something exists,
it can be measured"

                                                    Alfred North Whitehead

### Slide 6
Purpose:
To get reproducible diagnostic precision
To track changes in key outcome domains

Measurement Based Care

### Slide 7
Measurement Based Care

Many treatment problems are not treatment problems at all,
they are diagnosis problems.

### Slide 8
Measurement Based Care

If you don't get the treatment straight, you will probably choose the wrong treatment.

### Slide 9
Measurement Based Care

Take a proper history and make an accurate diagnosis before you select a treatment.

### Slide 10
Elements of a psychiatric history

Sections:
Identifying information
Presenting problem(s)
Onset & course of presenting problem
Structured diagnostic interview
Past psychiatric history
Psychiatric medications (current and past)

For a more detailed psychiatric history template see: Sheehan DV, Giddens JM. (2016). Suicidality Assessment and Documentation for Healthcare Providers: A Brief, Practical Guide. (1st ed.). Tampa, FL: Harm Research Press. April 2016. (Available from: http://HarmResearch.org) ISBN: 978-0-9969729-1-8

Family psychiatric & medical history
Social history & stresses

Diagnosis

Scores to Remember

### Slide 54
Definition  of  Suicidality

Suicidality - [sui (of oneself) + cide (a killing) + ality (the state of being real or actual)] - all suicidal phenomena including suicidal ideation, suicidal behaviors, suicidal impulses, suicidal command hallucinations, suicidal dreams, suicidal delusions, and / or precognitive antecedents to suicidal impulses and / or any suicidal phenomenon that arches across a time frame, but did not appear as a suicidal ideation or as a suicidal behavior during that time frame.  
For example, a patient who previously made plans or intends to kill herself at a future date, but may not have thought about it during a particular time frame.  (See event of suicidal intent, event of suicidality, episode of suicidality, and unexpected suicidal impulse attack for further clarification and examples.)  This definition deliberately excludes theories or speculations about, predictions from or likelihood of a suicidal ideation or behavior.  It also excludes experiences that may be comorbid with or correlated with core suicidal phenomena, but in and of themselves are not directly suicidal experiences (e.g. hopelessness, depression, anxiety, grief).

Sheehan, D. V. and Giddens, J. M. 2015. Suicidality: A Roadmap for Assessment and Treatment. Available from: http://www.harmresearch.org

### Slide 55
Suicidality Phenomena Needing Assessment

Assess multiple types of suicidal ideation, in any combination: 
Passive suicidal ideation
Active suicidal ideation
Suicide plan (method + means + location + timeframe) 
Intent to act on ideation
Intent to die

### Slide 56
3 types of suicidal behavior: 
Preparatory behavior (aborted, interrupted or neither)
Suicide attempt (aborted, interrupted or completed as planned)
Suicidal "accidents"
Self-harm without any intent to die                                           (by definition this is non-suicidal)

Suicidality Phenomena Needing Assessment

### Slide 57
3 types of suicidal behavior: 
Preparatory behavior
Suicide attempt
Suicidal "accidents"
Self-harm without any intent to die (by definition this is non-suicidal)

Suicidality Phenomena Needing Assessment

### Slide 58
Additional types of suicidality: 
Impulsive attacks of suicidality
Suicidal dreams
Psychotic Suicidality (e.g. command hallucinations, delusions with suicidal content)
Time spent in suicidality (per day)

Suicidality Phenomena Needing Assessment

### Slide 59
Sheehan DV, Giddens JM, Sheehan IS. Status Update on the Sheehan-Suicidality Tracking Scale (S-STS) 2014. Innov Clin Neurosci. 2014;11(9-10):93-140.

Tracking  Suicidal  Phenomena

of concern

### Slide 61
Green: Allow the patient to go home and monitor for worsening. 
Blue: Get additional information and respond accordingly. Based on findings, use prudent clinical judgment, to continue to monitor, refer, or admit.
Purple: Refer to a psychiatrist or mental health specialist. 
Red: Consider hospitalization. 
Orange: Give them the Sheehan-Suinocerality Tracking Scale (S-SNTS) and review

## Source: `02_Clinical_Skills/Interviewing/_source/608 - Alchohol and Sedative Hypnotic Addiction in the Elderly.pptx`

### Slide 1
Alcohol and Sedative-Hypnotic Addiction in the Elderly

David W. Oslin, MD
Associate Professor
University of Pennsylvania, School of Medicine
Philadelphia VAMC

### Slide 2
2

Things to Know about These Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2018.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 12/11/2020.

### Slide 3
3

Self-Assessment Question 1Which of the following is true about alcohol use disorder in the elderly?

A. 	It is more frequently seen in community-dwelling elderly than in primary care setting populations.
B. 	It is less prevalent in the elderly than cocaine or hallucinogen addictions. 
C. 	Heavy drinking is associated with suicide risk to the same degree or greater than is depression.
D. 	All of the above
E. 	None of the above

### Slide 4
4

Self-Assessment Question 2Which of the following contains the greatest amount of ethanol?

A. 	12 oz of beer
B. 	10 oz of wine
C. 	4 oz of sherry
D. 	1.5 oz of vodka
E. 	Each contains an equal amount of alcohol

### Slide 5
5

Self-Assessment Question 3Which of the following is a benefit of moderate alcohol use?

A. 	Reduced cardiovascular risk
B. 	Decreased risk of fractures
C. 	Increased risk of suicide
D. 	Improved cognitive functioning in men
E. 	None of the above

### Slide 6
6

Self-Assessment Question 4Which of the following is a treatment approach for alcohol use disorder in the elderly?

A. 	Psychoeducation
B.	12-step groups
C.	Care management
D. 	Pharmacotherapy
E. 	All of the above

### Slide 7
7

Self-Assessment Question 5Which of the following is not true of pharmacotherapy of alcohol use disorder in the elderly?

A. 	Naltrexone is considered unsafe and ineffective as an agent in treating elderly patients.
B. 	Naltrexone is an opioid receptor antagonist.
C. 	The presence of a positive family history for alcohol problems predicts better outcome with naltrexone treatment of older adults.
D. 	Antipsychotic treatment has not been shown effective in reducing alcohol use disorder in the elderly.
E. 	Chronic benzodiazepine treatment has not been shown effective in reducing alcohol use disorder in the elderly.

### Slide 8
8

Major Points

At-risk drinking is common and under-recognized in the elderly. Chronic benzodiazepine use (>3 months of daily use) is estimated at 12% in elderly primary care patients.
Alcohol consumption above an average of1 standard drink per day is considered excessive in elderly. 
Treatment of alcohol or sedative hypnotic addiction in the elderly must address age-specific needs and presence of depression.
Both psychosocial and pharmacologic interventions are available.

### Slide 9
9

What is the Extent of this ProblemIn Community-Dwelling Elderly?

Source: NHSDU - SAMHSA 2006

### Slide 10
10

Community	Primary	Mental Health
		Care	Services

15

10

0

Prevalence (in % of elderly)

N=87,915

N=865

N=5,723

N=12,000

N=3,954

N=140

N=5,647

Prevalence of Alcohol Addiction

Liberto JG, Oslin DW, Ruskin PE. Alcoholism in older persons: a review of the literature. Hosp Comm Psychiatry. 1992;43(10):975-984 (Review)

5

### Slide 11
11

Prevalence of Alcohol Use Disorder By Age Group Over Time

Grant, et. al. Drug and Alcohol Dependence 2004

### Slide 12
12

Chronic Benzo. Use in Elderly

NHSDU - SAMHSA 2006

### Slide 13
13

How Much Alcohol is Too Much in Late Life?

Appropriate limit: no more than average of 1 standard drink per day
No binge drinking (4 or more standard drinks in one day) episodes
No drinking while taking certain medications or in patients with certain illnesses - this is a major issue for older adults

### Slide 14
14

What is a Standard Drink?

### Slide 15
15

Suicide

Highest rates of suicide occur in late life among men.
Depression causes a 5.8 fold increase in risk of suicide compared to death from other causes 
At-Risk drinking (1-2 drinks/day) causes a 10.6 fold increase in risk of suicide compared to death from other causes
Heavy drinking (3+ drinks/day) causes a 8.9 fold increase in risk of suicide compared to death from other causes

Grabble, et al. 1997

### Slide 16
16

Past History of Heavy drinking/alcoholism

Many older adults, especially those of the "Woodstock" generation, will enter later life with a history of alcohol or drug abuse
Past use disorder leads to a 5 fold increase in later life mental disorders (depression and dementia)
Treatment of late life depression (3-5 yr outcomes)
88% of those without an alcohol history significantly improved
57% of those with an alcohol history significantly improved

Saunders et al. 1991, Cook et al. 1991

### Slide 17
17

What Harm is a Few Drinks?

Epidemiologic data suggests moderate drinking can be beneficial for
Heart disease
Social aspects

Potential confounds
Sample selection (fit elders with healthy lifestyles)
Surrogate for something else (nutrition, exercise)
No clinical trials data

### Slide 18
18

Examples of Screening Instruments

Michigan Alcoholism Screening Test-Geriatric Version (MAST-G)  

Health Screening Survey (including other health behaviors, e.g. nutrition, exercise, smoking, depressed feelings)

CAGE (Cut down, Annoyed by others, feel Guilty, need 'Eye-opener')

AUDIT-C - 3 questions related to quantity and frequency

### Slide 19
The Spectrum of Interventions

Specialized Treatments

Referral Management

Brief
Interventions

Brief Advice

Prevention/
Education

A	B	C	D	E	F
	Not	Light-Moderate	At-Risk	Alcohol	Mild	Chronic/Severe
	Drinking	Drinking	Drinking	Abuse	Dependence	Dependence

### Slide 20
What are my treatment options?

Past addiction
Light to excessive use
Addiction

### Slide 21
Past Addiction

Relapse prevention - avoid unnecessary medications, cross tolerant medications

Aggressive management of other conditions such as depression as the prognosis is poorer in those with a past history

### Slide 22
Light to Excessive Use

Education and advice

Use of motivational interviewing or brief interventions when the goal is to achieve abstinence as might be the case for someone with diabetes or depression or when a patient is on certain medications.

### Slide 23
23

Brief Advice and Brief Interventions

Brief Advice
<5 minute
Advice on drinking limits
Connection to overall health
Brief Interventions 
20 minute focused discussion
Usually workbook based
2-3 sessions over 3-12 months
Goals 
Facilitate treatment entry 
Reduce alcohol consumption by promoting responsible drinking

### Slide 24
Brief Interventions

Can decrease alcohol use for 12 months
The effect size is similar for men and women
No difference in effect by age
Can lead to decreased use of emergency rooms, clinic visits, etc.

### Slide 25
Key Components of Alcohol Brief Interventions

Screening
Feedback
Motivation to change
Strategies for change
Behavioral contract
Follow-up

### Slide 26
Who Can Conduct Brief Alcohol Interventions?

Physicians
Nurses/Nurse Practitioners
Physician Assistants
Social Workers
Psychologists
Health Educators
Home Health Workers
Other Allied Health Providers

### Slide 27
Empirical Support for Brief Interventions for Older adults

One study (Project GOAL) focused on physician advice for older adult at-risk drinkers:  Physician advice led to reduced consumption at 12 months

Health Profile Project: Findings indicate that an elder-specific motivational enhancement session reduced at-risk drinking at 12 months

### Slide 28
Treating AddictionThere are lots of options

28

Pharmacotherapy

12 step Facilitation

Drug Free Housing

Residential Treatment

Motivational Enhancement

CBT

Supported Employment

AA / Peer Support

Intensive Outpatient

Telephone Aftercare

### Slide 29
Treating Addiction

Things to Avoid
Refer to peer support only
Simple referral
Treat concurrent disorder alone
Abstinence as the only acceptable outcome

Things to Learn
Brief intervention for  referrals
CBT or other evidenced based psychotherapy
Pharmacotherapy
Patients may have different goals than you
Toxicity is often dose dependent. Strive for the lowest dose possible.

### Slide 30
Confrontation vs. Motivational Interviewing or Brief Interventions

### Slide 31
31

Simple vs Brief Intervention Referral

Zanjani F, Oslin D (2005). Telephone Based Referral-Care Management. Grant Supported by Philadelphia Veteran's Affairs: Mental Illness Research Education and Clinical Center (MIRECC)

p = .006

### Slide 32
32

Specialty Addiction Services

Compliance with treatment is greater in older adults compared to younger adults particularly if care is individualized

Age specific programming (groups, individual treatment, etc) appears to have an impact on outcome in 1 randomized study and several observational studies

### Slide 33
33

Use of 12 Step Group Oriented Treatment by Elderly vs Middle-Aged Adults after Rehab Program

Oslin et al 2005

### Slide 34
34

Relapse Reduction in Elderly vs Younger Adults Treated with Naltrexone

Older Adults

Younger Adults

Cumulative rates of relapse to heavy drinking

Oslin et al. 2003

### Slide 35
35

Pharmacotherapy - a real option for treatment

Alcohol dependence
Naltrexone  (oral and depot)
Acamprosate
Antabuse
Unapproved medications with very limited evidence to support use (SSRIs, mood stabilizers, antipsychotics)
Opioids
Buprenorphine
Methadone
Cocaine (no specific pharmacotherapy available)
Nicotine
Nicotine replacement
Bupropion
Verenacline

### Slide 36
36

Naltrexone

FDA approved for the treatment of alcohol dependence
Functions as an opioid receptor antagonist (mu >> delta or kappa)
Development was an example of bench to bedside translational science (opioid effects on reward pathways)
Naltrexone is safe for older adults and may work best is those with a positive family history of problems.

### Slide 37
37

Naltrexone Should Be Used for Patients With:

Prior treatment failure
High level of interest in biomedical therapies
Low level of interest in traditional psychosocial therapies
Cognitive impairment
In most alcohol-dependent patients
Consider depot formulation for added adherence

### Slide 38
38

Acamprosate

FDA approved for alcohol dependence based on experience mostly from Europe
Primary action unknown
Promotes abstinence
No studies conducted specifically in older adults but there is no reason to believe there are age specific problems with use.

### Slide 39
39

Comorbidity of Alcohol Use with other Mental Health Problems

Concurrent alcohol use and depression may be more common in late life than in younger adults
Concurrent moderate or at-risk use may be a much greater problem than dependence
Fragmented care is particularly problematic in late life

### Slide 40
40

41

Current depressive syndrome

Age 55 and over

1/2 of subjects are randomly assigned to receive naltrexone 50 mg
All subjects receive sertraline 100 mg
Outcomes at 3 months

(Oslin, unpublished communication, 2002)

### Slide 42
42

Treating alcoholism is necessary but not sufficient

### Slide 43
43

Relationship between drinking during the trial and depression outcomes

Relapse is defined as drinking 5 or more standard drinks in a day

### Slide 44
44

What about moderate or abusive drinking (non-dependent drinking)

Most common pattern of drinking among those with depression
May be beneficial for heart disease
Safety concerns may be less with newer medications (SSRIs) than older meds (TCAs)

Is Sedative/Hypnotic Use a Co-Occurring Problem?

Associated with falls
Associated with memory impairment

Chronic Use (>3 months)

### Slide 54
54

Caveats About Treatment

Addiction treatment is not one size fits all.  There are many options-use them.
Compliance with treatment is important and tends to be greater in older adults compared to younger adults. Continually support treatment.
Treatment is not a "carve out" available only in select settings.
While abstinence is often the goal, it is not the only goal.
Assess outcomes and change the treatment when it isn't working.

### Slide 55
55

Case Example #1

Ms. Smith is a 76 year-old African American female who recently signed on with an HMO Medicare plan that required her to see a new PCP.  On her initial visit with her new PCP, she was noted to be taking a temazepam 15 mg each night for insomnia as well as a variety of other medications.  Ms. Smith said that she had been taking the temazepam for several years and that it helps to relax her. She denied being depressed or having lost interest in activities.  She reports that her energy is good and that she sleeps throughout the night except to urinate.   She has never tried to go to sleep without her medication because she knows the importance of taking her medication as prescribed. Her medical problems include chronic obstructive lung disease, arthritis for which she uses a cane, and well-controlled hypertension.

### Slide 56
56

Case Example #2

Mr. Jones is a 72 year-old man seen by his primary care practitioner (PCP) for a routine exam. He reports suffering from some ill-described upper abdominal discomfort, but otherwise has no complaints. He currently lives by himself, does his own housework and shopping, and has a limited circle of friends. Upon asking about general health habits, the PCP learns that Mr. Jones does not smoke but does drink each day at dinner and bedtime.  The PCP asks him the CAGE questions, which seem to upset him, but he responds negatively to each question.  His PCP makes a comment to be watchful of his drinking, but does not pursue this further.  
	Six months later, at the urging of his family, the patient undergoes a mental health evaluation.  The family is concerned about his ability to live alone and is considering urging him to move into an assisted living situation.  During his evaluation, it is learned that he is quite functional, although he is somewhat slowed and rarely travels outside of the house.  Cognitively, he shows no signs of dementia, but has some diminution in reaction time and problem solving. It is determined that he routinely drinks one standard drink for dinner and two standard drinks of sherry before bed.  This has been his pattern of drinking for 15 years since becoming a widower.

Fleming, Barry, Manwell, Johnson, London (1997).  Brief physician advice for problem alcohol drinkers.  Journal of the American Medical Association, 277, 1039-1080.

Miller & Rollnick (1991).  Motivational Interviewing:  Preparing People to Change Addictive Behavior.  New York:  Guilford Press.

Barry, Oslin, Blow  (2001) Prevention and Management of Alcohol Problems in Older Adults.  New York, Springer Publishing.

58

A. 	It is more frequently seen in community-dwelling elderly than in primary care setting populations.
B. 	It is less prevalent in the elderly than cocaine or hallucinogen addictions.

A. 	Reduced cardiovascular risk

C. 	Increased risk of suicide
D. 	Improved cognitive functioning in men

A. 	Psychoeducation
B.	12-step groups
C.	Care management
D. 	Pharmacotherapy
E. 	All of the above

### Slide 9
Abuse and neglect proceedings

Abuse, Neglect and Child Custody Matters

### Slide 10
May be used in any stage of Child Abuse/Neglect proceedings
Commonly use in Termination of Parental Rights hearings
Often used in disputed divorce proceedings when custody/visitation cannot be agreed upon

Custody and Parenting Evaluations

### Slide 11
Abuse:
the physical or mental injury of a child by any parent or other person who has permanent or temporary care or custody or responsibility for supervision of a child, or by any household or family member, under circumstances that indicate that the child's health or welfare is harmed or at substantial risk of being harmed 
sexual abuse of a child, whether physical injuries are sustained or not 

Neglect: the leaving of a child unattended or other failure to give proper care and attention to a child by any parent or other person who has permanent or temporary care or custody or responsibility for supervision of the child under circumstances that indicate: (1) that the child's health or welfare is harmed or placed at substantial risk of harm; or (2) mental injury to the child or a substantial risk of mental injury.

Abuse & Neglect

### Slide 12
Who are the mandated reporters?
Health care practitioners
Police Officers
Teachers
Human Service Workers

It is the obligation to make reports to DSS regarding cases of suspected abuse or neglect

Mandated Reporters

### Slide 13
Reporting Requirement

Notify the local department or the appropriate law enforcement agency

Written report to local department no later than 48 hours after the and copy to the local State's Attorney

### Slide 14
Disclosing Information for Purposes of Reporting Child Abuse

The health care provider shall disclose only the information that will, in the professional judgment of the provider, contribute to the:

1. Assessment of risk
2. Development of a service plan
3. Implementation of a Safety Plan
4. Investigation of the suspected case of abuse or neglect

### Slide 15
Include in the report:

name, age, home address of child
name and address of parent/caretaker
whereabouts of the child
nature and extent of abuse/neglect and evidence of possible previous instances
any other information on cause and identity of individual responsible for abuse/neglect

Disclosing Information for Purposes of Reporting Child Abuse (cont)

### Slide 16
Parental rights may only be terminated by court order 
Occurs when the department determines that adoption is the appropriate permanent plan for the child 
Parents may voluntarily give up their parental rights

Termination of Parental Rights (TPR)

### Slide 17
Overall, the court must determine what is in the child's best interest by Clear and Convincing Evidence
Factors that the court considers
Child's health and safety
DSS efforts to reunify
Services before placement
Case plan compliance
Parent's progress
Child's emotional ties
Child's adjustment

TPR Hearing

### Slide 18
If the child has already be adjudicated CINA, the court must also consider the following
Parental disability
Abuse or neglect
Repeated neglect
Drug-addicted infant
Aggravating circumstances (torture, chronic abuse, sexual abuse, chronic or life-threatening neglect, convicted of crime of violence against child, involuntarily lost parental rights to the child's siblings)

TPR Hearing (cont)

Court
An attorney for one of the parents
Parent

All states have juvenile court but might be combined under family court

### Slide 28
In 1990's, in reaction to increasing violent crimes by youth, nearly all states enacted laws to facilitate transfer from juvenile to adult criminal courts

Juvenile transfers have a tremendous impact on a young person's life

History of Transfers

### Slide 29
Maryland Transfers

Discretionary Waiver

The juvenile court may waive its exclusive jurisdiction over a child who meets specified age/offense criteria if it finds, after ordering an investigation and holding a hearing, that the child is not a fit subject for juvenile rehabilitative measures

### Slide 30
Maryland Transfers (cont)

Statutory Exclusion

For certain crimes committed at certain ages, the juvenile court "does not have jurisdiction over" various age/offense categories, unless the adult criminal court waives its jurisdiction and transfers the case to the juvenile court

### Slide 31
Statutory Exclusion -  continued

14 years old alleged to have done an act that, if committed by an adult, would be a crime punishable by death or life imprisonment
	
16 years old alleged to have committed any of the following crimes
Abduction
Kidnapping
Second degree murder/Attempted 
Manslaughter, except involuntary manslaughter
Second degree rape/Attempted
Robbery/Attempted
Second degree sexual offense/Attempted
Third degree sexual offense
Using, wearing, carrying, or transporting a firearm during and in relation to a drug trafficking crime
Use of a firearm
Carjacking or armed carjacking
Assault in the first degree

Maryland Transfers (cont)

### Slide 32
Maryland Transfers (cont)

Reverse Waiver

Court with criminal jurisdiction over a case excluded by statute from juvenile jurisdiction may waive it and transfer the child to juvenile court if such a transfer is "in the interests of the child or society"

### Slide 33
The law specifies various factors that must be considered in making a transfer determination

Transfer hearings usually involve a mental health evaluation of the juvenile to assist in the analysis of the factors

Role of Mental Health Expert in Juvenile Transfers

### Slide 34
Age - Current and Time of the Offense
Mental and Physical Condition of the Youth
Nature of the Offense 
Public Safety Risk
Amenability to Treatment (in an institution, facility, or program available to delinquent children)

The Five Factors

### Slide 35
Expert shall examine the child and prepare a report stating whether the child is incompetent to proceed  

In conducting the examination, the qualified expert shall review all available medical, educational, and court records concerning the child and the child's case

The State shall bear the burden of proving the child's competency beyond a reasonable doubt

Juvenile Competency Proceedings

### Slide 36
Factors Considered

Consider the following factors
child's age 
maturity level 
developmental stage
decision-making abilities
Capacity of the child to:
Appreciate the allegations against the child
Appreciate the range and nature of allowable dispositions that may be imposed in the proceedings against the child
Understand the roles of the participants and the adversary nature of the legal process
Disclose to counsel facts pertinent to the proceedings at issue
Display appropriate courtroom behavior
Testify relevantly

### Slide 37
Describe the treatment is necessary for the child to attain competency 
State whether the child poses a danger to the child or to the person or property of others 
Address likelihood of the child attaining competency under the treatment or education recommended, including the probability that the child will attain competency in the foreseeable future
Address whether the child meets the criteria for involuntary admission

Additional Report Requirements

### Slide 38
Services

At a competency hearing, if the court determines that the child is incompetent to proceed and can attain competency, the court may order DHMH to provide competency attainment services

Any competency attainment services shall be provided in the least restrictive environment but the court may order a child to be placed in a facility for children 
A child may not be placed in a detention facility or a psychiatric hospital solely for the purposes of competency attainment

### Slide 39
Services (cont)

Court education services are typically provided by a special educator in the community

The court may order that services be continued in increments of not more than six months

DHMH to reassess competency status every six months 
If the court determines that the child is unlikely to attain competency in the foreseeable future charges may be dismissed

The court shall dismiss the charges if the child has not attained competency within eighteen months for felonies and six months for misdemeanors

### Slide 40
Typically, create a treatment plan for the youth that includes a recommendation on placement.  Address the following areas:

Placement
Substance abuse needs
Educational needs
Psychotherapy needs
Psychiatric/medical needs

Pre-Disposition Evaluations

### Slide 41
Special Populations

### Slide 42
Risk Assessments
School Threats
Sexual Offenders

Special Populations

### Slide 43
Risks assessments are an analysis of risk factors related to topic being evaluated (e.g. violence, recidivism, sexual perpetration, etc.)
Risk assessments are not predictions
Generally, a risk level is assigned (low, medium, high)
Generally, risk factors are the same for many of topics being assessed
Protective factors should also be considered in analysis
Risk factors are both static and dynamic

Risk Assessments

### Slide 44
History of violence/previous offense
Male gender
History of abuse
History of violence exposure
Low IQ
Early sociopathic behaviors

Common Static Risk Factors

### Slide 45
Substance use 
Impulsivity
Emotional volatility
Unstable/violent home or neighborhood
Poor supervision
Limited social attachments and/or attachment to a criminological group

Common Dynamic Risk Factors

### Slide 46
Weapon access
Problematic social media/internet use
Violent video games
Pornography/sexual deviancy patterns

Unique Risk Factors

### Slide 47
Threat vs. Risk Assessment

Threat Assessment: Concern about the potential for violence toward a particular person in a particular setting

Risk Assessment: Likelihood an individual will commit some general type of crime of violence in the future

### Slide 48
Risk Factors Affecting School Threat Level

Personality of student
Family dynamics
School dynamics and student's role in those dynamics
Social dynamics

### Slide 49
Assessment of Juvenile Sex Offenders

Juvenile sex offenders are difficult to distinguish from other delinquents
Assessment is different from that of adults
Unlike adults, adolescents are still very much developing
No aspect of their development, including their cognitive development is static or stable

## Source: `02_Clinical_Skills/Interviewing/_source/Intern Call Expectations 6.27.2022.pptx`

### Slide 1
Short Call Expectations

For Interns :)

### Slide 2
What is short call for interns?

A chance for you to learn how to complete admissions under the direct supervision of a senior resident. 
This will help you prepare for your call shifts later in residency, as well as give you opportunities to practice interviewing patients, writing notes, creating formulations, etc. 

You are expected to participate in Short Call on the following days at the following hospitals:
1. Sheppard Pratt in Towson: Mondays - Fridays
2. UMMC Downtown campus: Thursdays and Fridays (except for the months of July and January)

Arrive at 5PM and stay until 9PM. You are allowed to stay until midnight, if necessary, to finish your notes, however you must leave by midnight to avoid violating duty hours. Please let your senior know if you anticipate that you may not finish before midnight so that they can assist. 

You are never scheduled over the weekends or on holidays recognized by UMMC.

### Slide 3
What is expected of me during short call?

You will complete a maximum of 2 admissions during your shift:  The first admission that comes in between 5-7PM, and the first admission that comes in between 7-9PM. If no admissions come in between 5-7PM, then you take the first 2 admissions that arrive between 7-9PM. 
You will lead the psychiatric interview/complete a physical exam, enter orders into the EMR, and write the admission note (further details to follow). 
Your senior resident is required to be present for the entire interview while you are on Direct Supervision. 
Once you have been approved to transition off of Direct Supervision by an attending,  your senior must briefly see all patients, but don't need to be present for the entire interview. 
Of note, the Direct Supervision form is located in the Psychiatry Residents One Drive folder.. Must be signed by an attending within the first 1-2 months of residency (while on the psych half).

### Slide 4
Sheppard pratt

In the past, the intern sat with the senior resident in their senior call room. Due to COVID precautions, the intern now sits in the ROPC (the third-year outpatient clinic). 
Enter through the Weinberg entrance of Sheppard Pratt. 
To get to the ROPC, walk through the Weinberg building to the original part of SEPH, past the cafeteria towards the other entrance. If you are standing in that main atrium, looking out towards the entrance, turn to your RIGHT and walk down the hallway to the end. There will be an elevator there - take it to the top floor.
When you exit the elevators, take a LEFT and you will be in the ROPC. It is mostly offices for the third year residents, but there is a small workroom with a computer for the interns.
Highly recommend texting/calling your senior your first few nights there and have them meet you in Weinberg or the cafeteria so that you can walk together.

### Slide 5
IPHB

You will sit in the 12W workroom with the senior resident.
Use the North Tower elevators to get to the 12th floor, and your #12 key lets you into the unit (both doors) and the resident work room
Can text your senior ahead of time and have them meet you somewhere to walk up together

### Slide 6
Objective Forms

On the Psychiatry One Drive, under Call Guidelines -> Junior Call Objective Forms, there are 2 documents that should be completed by the end of your 6 months on psychiatry:
1. IPHB Junior Call Objectives -> send to IPHB chief when completed
2. SEPH Junior Call Objectives -> send to SEPH chief when completed

### Slide 7
How do I know when I am on call?

Will be on AMION. Email will be sent out by the Exec Chief once it has been published for viewing.
