## Source: `03_Core_Topics/Anxiety/_source/514 - Childhood OCD - July 2020 revised 112420.pptx`

### Slide 1
Childhood OCD

Vishal Madaan, M.D.*, Jenny Lee, M.D. * and 
Kate Fitzgerald, M.D.**

*University of Virginia Health System, Charlottesville, VA
**University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, MI

### Slide 2
Things to Know about These Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 11/11/2020.

### Slide 3
Question 1

Early onset OCD is characterized by:

A) Increased comorbid tic disorders
B) Decreased comorbid ADHD
C) Onset of OCD precedes tics by many years
D) Minimal genetic loading

### Slide 4
Question 2

Common comorbid diagnoses with OCD include all of the following except:

A) ADHD and ODD
B) MDD and anxiety
C) Somatoform disorders
D) Motor tics

### Slide 5
Question 3

The following medications are effective in the treatment of OCD, except:

A) Clomipramine
B) Fluoxetine
C) Desipramine 
D) Fluvoxamine

### Slide 6
Question 4

The POTS trial in OCD found that the greatest reduction in CY-BOCS scores results from:

A) Sertraline alone
B) CBT alone
C) Combined CBT + Sertraline
D) Family Therapy

### Slide 7
Question 5

Criteria for diagnosing PANDAS include:

A) Motor and vocal tics
B) OCD of childhood onset
C) Tourette disorder
D) Sudden onset of OCD after a streptococcal infection

### Slide 8
Teaching Points

Distinguish between normal rituals vs. OCD; young children may not recognize their rituals as unreasonable or excessive

Most common obsessions: Concerns regarding contamination, self harm, doing the right thing (scrupulosity), reassurance or sexual thoughts

Treatment: the most efficacious choice may be a combination of SSRI+CBT

PANS/PANDAS

### Slide 9
Outline

OCD vs. Normal childhood rituals
DSM-5 changes
OCD epidemiology and symptomatology
Comorbidity of OCD
Assessment of OCD 
Prognosis of OCD
Treatment options: CBT & Pharmacotherapy
The POTS and POTS-II studies
Special issues: PANDAS/PANS

### Slide 10
OCD vs. Normal rituals

Obsessions: Persistent thoughts, images or impulses that are ego-dystonic & intrusive

Compulsions: Repetitive, purposeful behaviors in response to an obsession, usually to relieve anxiety

Young children may not recognize their rituals as unreasonable or excessive. Important for clinician to help recognize associated impairment

### Slide 11
OCD vs. Normal rituals

Normal developmental rituals may include:
avoiding stepping on cracks in sidewalks (rituals related to belief in power of wishing)
normal collecting behaviors (i.e. baseball cards)

Must distinguish vernacular "obsessive" and "compulsive" from clinical syndrome

Look for severity of behaviors and functional impairment

### Slide 12
Obsessive Compulsive Disorder
Body Dysmorphic Disorder
Hoarding Disorder
Trichotillomania
Substance/medication-induced obsessive compulsive and related disorder
Obsessive compulsive and related disorder due to another medical condition
Other specified obsessive compulsive and related disorders
Unspecified obsessive compulsive and related disorder

DSM-5 Obsessive Compulsive and Related Disorders

### Slide 13
Specifiers:

A Spectrum of Insight:
Good or Fair Insight
Poor Insight
Absent Insight/Delusional OCD beliefs

Tic related specifier for OCD
Comorbidity may have important clinical implications

DSM-5Obsessive Compulsive and Related Disorders

### Slide 14
Common symptoms

Excessive worries about danger, separation & contamination -> rituals of checking, hoarding & checking
Common obsessions: Concerns regarding contamination, self harm, doing the right thing (scrupulosity), reassurance or sexual thoughts
Common compulsions: Washing, repeating, checking, counting, touching, arranging and hoarding
Content of obsessions may be age dependent

### Slide 15
Symptoms of Childhood OCD

Obsessive thoughts and washing- sometimes in 85%
Repeating rituals in 50%: need to be perfect
Checking in 46%  (e.g., doors, windows, appliances)
Ordering, arranging and symmetry in 17%, 
Scrupulosity in 13% 
Takes 4-6 months before parents aware of sxs, secretiveness can lead to long time before diagnosis (Leonard 1993) 
In teens: sexual and religious obsessions (Scahill et al 2003)
Symptoms shift over time (Rettew et al 1992)

### Slide 16
Obsessions over the Life Span

*

*

*

Geller et al, 1998

### Slide 17
Compulsions over the Life Span

*

Geller et al, 1998

### Slide 18
Symptoms: Cluster Analysis
(Ivarssaon et al 2006)

N = 213; 96 F, 117 M

Aggressive/Checking (55%)
Sexual (10%)
Contamination/Cleaning (36%)
Ordering/Arranging (32%)
Repeating, "Just Right" (annoying) (45%)
Health (35%)
Misc.: Confess, prevent, touch, blink (60%)
Magical, Superstitious (32%)
Religious/Praying (18%)
Hoarding (35%)

Mental, Touch, Order
 (little cog content)
Contamination or Cleaning
Superstitious, repeating to prevent bad
Obsessions (harm, religion, sex), 
Confessing, Checking
Somatic (illness)

### Slide 19
Epidemiology

Point prevalence 0.8%; lifetime prevalence 1-4% (Keeley et al 2007)
Boys more likely with prepubertal onset; girls with pubertal onset
Male/female ratio equalizes in adolescence
Early-onset OCD:  tic disorders;  comorbid ADHD
onset of tics often precedes OCD by many years
Early-onset OCD: Stronger genetic loading
One-third to one-half adults with OCD have childhood-onset

### Slide 20
Etiology

Genetic transmission: Twin studies - "genetic factors" in 45-65% of variance of OCD in children (van Grootheest et al 2005)
Higher rates of OCD in 1 relatives 
(Nestadt et al 2000) 
30% of adolescents had 1 relatives with OCD (Lenane 1990)
Pediatric Autoimmune Neuropsychiatric Disorders Associated with Streptococcal Infections (PANDAS) subgroup: Post streptococcal production of autoantibodies which may cross-react with cellular components of basal ganglia

### Slide 21
Imaging

Enlarged thalamus, smaller nucleus accumbens in unmedicated pediatric OCD patients compared to controls (Premika et al 2016) 
T1-weighted MRI brain scans from 25 international research institutes
No significant differences in medicated vs unmedicated pediatric OCD patients
No significant associations in illness severity and subcortical volumes
An enlarged thalamus has been seen in Tourette's syndrome and ADHD

### Slide 22
Imaging

Basal ganglia: 
Functional deficits in cortico-striato-thalamo -cortical circuit underlie OCD (Saxena et al, 2000; Fitzgerald et al, 2011)
Neurochemicals implicated: Serotonin, glutamate
Functional MRI data showed patients with OCD had reduced function and structure in rostral and dorsal anterior cingulate/medial prefrontal cortex and decreased gray matter volume in the ventromedial prefrontal area (Norman et al 2016)
Increased activation in the lateral and medial orbitofrontal cortex (Pauls et al 2014)

### Slide 23
Differential Diagnosis 
(Leonard et al 2006)

Depression and Anxiety disorders
Eating disorders
Tic disorders
Body dysmorphic disorder
Normal childhood rituals

### Slide 24
OCD Comorbidity: 80%!

Internalizing: ~60%
MDD: 40% child, 50-60% adolescents = adults
Separation anxiety: 50-60% child, 35% adolescents, 17% adults
Social anxiety, GAD, specific phobia, secondary panic
Externalizing: ADHD: 30-50% (boys>>girls) 
Tics: up to 25% child, 9% adolescents, 6% adult
PDD?

### Slide 25
OCD Comorbidity: 80%!

"OC Spectrum Disorders"
trichotillomania, nail biting, skin picking, pathologic gambling, paraphilias, BDD, eating disorders 
Less common in children and emerge more frequently in adolescence
Ego-syntonic, more difficult to treat

### Slide 26
Childhood OCD with and without tics: Are there differences?

Patients with OCD & comorbid tic disorder: Higher rates of symmetry, touching, rubbing, staring, blinking (Leckman 1994)
Patients with OCD alone have more contamination and cleaning 
OCD comorbid with tics more familial, more common in boys and has early onset (Geller 2001)

### Slide 27
OCD with comorbid ADHD

In a survey of youth with OCD, 25% patients had  comorbid ADHD (Masi et al 2006) 

Comorbid ADHD can compromise school performance (Geller et al 2003); concentrating on school and homework common problems (Piacentini et al 2003)

More problems in social functioning, school and depression (Sukhodolsky et al 2005)

ADHD + OCD: Higher rate in males, an earlier onset of OCD, a greater psychosocial impairment, and a stronger comorbidity with bipolar disorder, tic disorder, and oppositional defiant disorder/conduct disorder (Masi et al 2006)

### Slide 28
Assessment

Use multiple sources of information
Look for comorbidities
Estimate extent of impairment
Review developmental and family history
Look for possible medical causes
Utilize standardized instruments

### Slide 29
Assessment: Instruments 
(Merlo et al 2005)

Semi-structured interviews (e.g. Anxiety Disorders Interview for Children): Clinician administered with parents and children interviewed separately for 45-60 mins or K-SADS with one of five subscales.  
Child's Leyton Obsessional Inventory: Cards sorted 3 times for type, resistance and interference (Berg 1988)
Most often used scale in clinical trial to monitor response: C-YBOCS

### Slide 30
Children's Yale Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (CYBOCS)

Checklist of symptoms and 10 item clinician-driven questionnaire with 4 degrees of severity- 2 subscales Obsessions (20 points) and Compulsions (20 points) = 40 points
Integrate parent, child & clinician observations
Scoring:
0-7 = subclinical
8-15 = mild	*10=remission
16-23 = moderate
24-31 = severe
32-40 = extreme
Good interrater reliability (Yucclen et al 2006)

### Slide 31
Assessing response 
using CY-BOCS

CY-BOCS not extremely sensitive to change at highest severity

A 25% or 35% decrease is generally taken as efficacy and a score of 10 indicates full remission

### Slide 32
Assessment 
(Gilbert et al 2018)

Longitudinal observational cohort study (2003-2005) with 12 year follow-up
Performance-based observational task: ~300 children observed persistent negative evaluation
Those with heightened performance monitoring were twice as likely to develop OCD (odds ratio: 2)
Imaging: smaller right dorsal anterior cingulate cortex volume
Conclusions: Early childhood observed performance monitoring is an observed risk factor for OCD that can be identified in preschoolers.

### Slide 33
Prognosis and long term outcome
(Stewart 2003)

Meta-analysis:
16 samples, n=521 children & adolescents with OCD: followed for 1-15 yrs.
Pooled data: 41% had full OCD at follow-up, 60% full or sub-threshold OCD
Predictors of full OCD: Early age of onset,  OCD duration & being inpatient; Comorbid psychiatric illness & poor initial treatment response were poor prognostic factors 
Long-term medication studies: Modest incremental improvement but not normalization over 52 weeks of SSRI treatment; relapse rate after SSRI discontinuation possibly high

### Slide 34
Prognosis 
(Bloch 2009)

Longitudinal study: 45 of 62 eligible children with OCD reassessed ~9 years later, in early adulthood
44% of subjects had subclinical OC symptoms at f/u
Absence of a comorbid tic disorder & presence of prominent hoarding symptoms were associated with the persistence of OCD symptoms.
Female gender, earlier age at childhood assessment, later age of OCD onset, more-severe childhood OCD symptoms, and comorbid oppositional defiant disorder also were associated with persistence of OCD symptoms into adulthood

### Slide 35
Prognosis - Poor insight 
(Selles et al 2020)

The impact of avoidance, insight, and impairment recognition concordance on CBT outcomes, n=573
Reduced likelihood of treatment response was seen with more avoidance, limited child recognition of impairment, older age, and lower baseline severity but not insight
Insight and avoidance improved significantly with CBT
Conclusion: CBT response potential is not limited by poor insight by the child

### Slide 36
Prognosis - Family Functioning (Stewart et al 2017)

Family functioning of pediatric patients with OCD and their mothers and fathers, n =354, multi-center
Impairment was more extensive when OCD severity was at its worst; it was greater in the mother's viewpoint vs the father's
Emotional repercussions: stress and anxiety in all, frustration/anger in the children, and sadness in the parents
Occupational impairment was reported in nearly 1/2 of the mothers and 1/3 of the fathers

### Slide 37
Cognitive Behavioral Therapy

Hierarchy-based Exposure and Response Prevention  (March 1994, Scahill 1996, Franklin 1998): 14 sessions over 12 wks. with toolkit approach, self-monitoring, mindfulness - "fear" thermometer 
Imaginal exposure for obsessions: in vivo
Habit reversal for "just-so" phenomena
Cognitive restructuring for negative thoughts 
Self observation, extinction, operant conditioning, and modeling used in adolescence: Behavioral rewards
Flooding, graded exposure, and response prevention (March et al 1994) Family and group forms of treatment are effective as well (Barrett et al 2004)

### Slide 38
Pharmacotherapy

FDA approval for youth: 
Clomipramine >10 yrs.
Fluoxetine >8 yrs.
Fluvoxamine >8 yrs. 
Sertraline >6 yrs.

May need 10-12 weeks at highest tolerated doses

Up to 30-40% reduction in OCD symptoms with pharmacotherapy alone

Black Box Warning for suicidality for all antidepressants in youth, regardless of disorder being treated

### Slide 39
Pharmacotherapy: Important points

All SSRIs appear equally effective
Choice made on side effects, pharmacokinetic profiles and drug interactions
Check for sexual side effects 
Go slow with upward titration
12 months of treatment better than 6 months
Slow taper when discontinuing

### Slide 40
Childhood OCD: Meta-analysis
(Geller et al 2003)

Meta-analysis of all DBPC medication trials in pediatric OCD including paroxetine 12 studies met inclusion criteria: 1044 children included; 8-12 weeks of treatment
Overall effect size of 0.46 (modest effect)
Clomipramine superior to each SSRI (which were indistinguishable from each other); this may however have resulted from its use (as the first such medication available) in non-refractory population

### Slide 41
Clomipramine (CMI)

3 studies support efficacy:
Flament et al 1985, 10wk DB cross-over of 23 youth 3mg/kg
DeVeaugh-Geis et al 1992 led to FDA approval 
Leonard et al 1989 DB crossover between CMI 93-5mg/kg/day and DMI 
Ask about sudden death in first-degree relatives
In youth, CBC, LFTs, creatinine, EKG, BP and HR
Start at 25 mg, increase by 25 mg every 10-14 days, get EKGs and at least one plasma level of CMI and desmethylCMI before next level and aim for 3mg/kg/day but not higher than 5mg/kg/day
Watch for anticholinergic, seizures, BP/HR changes

### Slide 42
Treatment Resistant OCD in Youth (Reinblatt and Walkup 2005)

If partial response, add CBT
Switch SSRIs
Augmentation with atypical antipsychotics, esp. risperidone with schizotypy and tics
Augmentation with Clomipramine, Buspirone, Lithium, Pindolol, another SSRI/SNRI, clonazepam, Lamotrigine
SSRI and Clomipramine
Memantine, Riluzole, NAC, Inositol
Psychosurgery

### Slide 43
Pediatric OCD Treatment Study (POTS)
(Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

Compared efficacy of 4 different treatment options (n=112; 12 weeks) 
Sertraline alone
Sertraline +CBT
CBT alone
Placebo alone
Randomized parallel groups
Entry criteria CY-BOCS=16; Mean of 24.6 with only ADHD meds allowed
Sertraline - titration from 25-> 200 mg over 6 weeks
Outcome measure of remission: CY-BOCS <10

### Slide 44
POTS (Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

### Slide 45
POTS (Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

### Slide 46
POTS (Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

### Slide 47
POTS (Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

### Slide 48
POTS: Outcome
(Team POTS,JAMA 2004)

Mean daily dose of sertraline: combined treatment:133 mg/day and Sertraline alone: 176 mg/day
Effect sizes:
CBT: 0.97 
Sertraline alone: 0.67 
Combined treatment:1.4
Remission rates (CY-BOCS=10) 
Combined 53.6%
CBT alone 39.3%
Sertraline 21.4% 
Placebo 3.6%

### Slide 49
Treatment algorithm

CBT

Effective after 13-20 sessions?

SSRI + CBT*

SSRI

Effective after 8-12 weeks?

No

Yes

Yes

CBT: booster session for 3-6 mos., and then prn

Meds: 1-2 years, then consider gradual taper

Partial response: atypical augmentation, add CBT
No response: switch SSRI, switch CMI, atypical augmentation

No

Effective after 
8-12 weeks?

Yes

No

*Consider starting here if OCD w/ FH (Garcia 2010), or based on illness severity

Consider options for treatment-resistant OCD (slide 37)

### Slide 50
POTS-II 
(Franklin et al 2011)

Examined the effects of augmenting SSRIs with CBT (or a brief form of CBT); instructions in CBT delivered in the context of medication management.

12-week RCT involving 124 outpatients (7-17 years old) with OCD as primary diagnosis and CYBOCS >=16 despite adequate SRI trial (Franklin et al., JAMA 2011).

### Slide 51
Randomly assigned to 1 of 3 treatment strategies that included 7 sessions over 12 weeks
42 in the med. management only
42 in the med. mgmt. + instructions in CBT
42 in the med. mgmt. plus CBT
Responders:
'plus CBT' group - 68.6%
 plus instructions in CBT' group - 34% 
'med. management only' group - 30%

POTS-II 
(Franklin et al 2011)

### Slide 52
The medication management plus CBT strategy was superior to the other 2 strategies on all outcome measures. 
Addition of CBT to medication (compared to medication alone)         significantly greater response rate; augmentation of medication management with the addition of instructions in CBT did not

POTS-II 
(Franklin et al 2011)

### Slide 53
SSRI Meta-analysis in OCD(Bloch et al, 2009)

Meta-analysis to determine differences in efficacy and tolerability between different doses of SSRIs in OCD
Nine randomized DBPC studies with 2268 subjects reviewed: Higher doses of SSRIs had improved treatment efficacy, using either Y-BOCS score or proportion of treatment responders as an outcome
SSRIs' dose not associated with the number of all-cause dropouts
Higher doses of SSRIs associated with significantly higher proportion of dropouts due to side-effects

### Slide 54
SSRI Meta-analysis in OCD
(Ost et al 2016)

Meta-analysis and systematic review of randomized control trials for treatment of OCD in youth
25 trials for CBT and 9 for SSRIs

Response rates:
CBT - 70%
CBT+SSRI - 66%
SSRI - 49%
Placebo - 29%

For remission:
CBT - 53%
CBT+SSRI - 49%
SSRI - 24%
Placebo - 15%.

### Slide 55
Cognitive-Behavioral Family Treatment (O'Leary, 2009)

Cognitive-behavioral family treatment (CBFT) for childhood obsessive-compulsive disorder: 38 participants (age 13-24 years) from a randomized controlled trial of individual or group CBFT for childhood OCD assessed 7 years post-treatment
7 years after treatment: 79% participants from individual therapy & 95% from group therapy did not have a diagnosis of OCD
No significant differences found between treatment conditions except that depressive symptoms were more for individual treatment
CBFT for obsessive-compulsive disorder is effective 7 years post-treatment

### Slide 56
Riluzole Augmentation
(Grant et al 2013)

12 week, DBPC study in 60 children and adolescents with treatment resistant OCD
Mean age: 14.5  2.4 years
Mean CY-BOCS: 28.2  3.7
Randomized to Riluzole (final dose 100 mg/d) and placebo in addition to existing treatment regimen
No group differences; all subjects improved

### Slide 57
Augmentation of CBT + Weight-Adjusted d-Cycloserine
(Storch et al 2016)

142 youths enrolled (age range: 7-17 years) randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to either 10 sessions of d-cycloserine plus CBT or placebo plus CBT
No significant interaction between treatment group and changes in the CY-BOCS and CGI-S
d-cycloserine augmentation of CBT did not confer additional benefit relative to placebo among youth with OCD

### Slide 58
Pediatric Autoimmune Neuropsychiatric Disorders associated with Streptococcal infections (PANDAS)

Five clinical criteria of the PANDAS subgroup:
Presence of OCD and/or tic disorder
Pre-pubertal symptom onset
Episodic course characterized by acute, severe onset and dramatic symptom exacerbations
Neurological abnormalities present during symptom exacerbations
Temporal relationship between group A strep infections & symptom exacerbations

### Slide 60
Pediatric Acute-Onset Neuropsychiatric Syndrome (PANS) 
(Swedo et al 2012, Chang et al 2015)

Foudroyant (lightning-like) onset of  OCD or eating restrictions and at least two of seven categories: 
anxiety (particularly separation anxiety)
emotional lability or depression
irritability
aggression and/or severely oppositional behaviors
deterioration in school performance [ADHD-like behaviors, memory deficits, and cognitive changes]
sensory or motor abnormalities
somatic signs and symptoms (sleep disturbances, enuresis, or urinary frequency)

### Slide 61
PANS/PANDS

>25% of children with PANS/PANDAS report auditory, olfactory, or visual hallucinations (distinct from OCD intrusive images (Frankovich et al 2015)
Nearly 70% present with or develop tics (Swedo et al 2015, Murphy et al 2015) 
The sudden onset of reduced and restricted food intake fulfills the first criterion for PANS, even in the absence of more typical OCD symptoms (Swedo et al 2012 and Toufexis et al 2015)

### Slide 62
Refutation of PANDAS hypothesis (Kurlan and Kaplan 2004)

Level of severity of tics and OCD symptoms not defined
Age of onset the same as 'regular' TS and OCD. Further, there has been a post pubertal case reported 
Abrupt onset not clinically specific since tics may not be identified gradually e.g. at least 2 studies show 38-50% of  children with TS described as acute onset with no diagnosis of PANDAS
Does presence of choreiform movement suggest that diagnosis is Sydenham's?
GABHS as causative agent hard to show since a) carrier states b) infection worsens any tics
There is imprecision in temporal course of PANDAS

### Slide 63
OCD + Strep tests
(Orlovska et al 2017)

Danish Psychiatric Central Register of over one million individuals who had a psychiatric diagnosis from 1996-2013 with a follow-up of up to 17 years
Over 40,000 had a diagnosis of a mental disorder, and 38% had a positive streptococcal test
1000+ patients with OCD, >50% of the children had a previous positive streptococcal test

### Slide 64
Patients with a positive strep test:
18% higher risk of any mental disorder
51% higher risk of OCD
35% of tic disorders
Individuals with negative streptococcal test results: 
8% increased risk of any mental disorder
28% increased risk of OCD
25% increased risk of tic disorders compared with individuals who had never tested for strep

OCD + Strep tests
(Orlovska et al 2017)

### Slide 65
Prognosis of PANDAS

Longitudinal follow up: patients with presumed PANDAS were no more likely to have strep-related exacerbations of OCD than those with regular OCD (Leckman et al 2011)
Prospective study followed 33 children up to 4.8 yrs. after DBPC of IVIG (Leon et al 2017)
Initial follow-up: 88% did not have clinically significant OCD symptoms
72% experienced at least one exacerbation of PANDAS symptoms, median: 1

### Slide 66
Treatment Guidelines for PANDAS(Swedo et al 1998)

Assess for GABHS infection by 48 hour culture in child with abrupt onset OCD/Tic D/O. Treat positive culture with 10-day course of antibiotics
If abrupt onset of OCD/tics occurred 4-6 weeks before visit-> Check ASO & Anti-Dnase-B + 48 hr GABHS throat culture-> Do not treat with antibiotics if culture is negative (despite  titers)
Throat cultures at time of relapse of OCD symptoms
Immunomodulatory treatment (Plasma exchange or IVIG) only used for most severely affected patients

### Slide 67
Clinical implications: PANDAS (Kurlan and Kaplan 2004)

Only get Streptococcal culture when there are clinical signs and symptoms (otherwise carrier state can be confusing)
Antineuronal antibodies and D8/17 are NOT reliable indicators (even though elevated D8/17 on B lymphocytes is a susceptibility marker for Rh-F) 
Do not use prophylactic antibiotics and do not use plasma exchange and IV immunoglobulin
Azithromycin showed significantly greater reductions in OCD severity compared to placebo, but the difference in CY-BOCS was not significant (Murphy et al 2017)

### Slide 68
IVIG Treatment of PANDAS
(Williams et al 2016)

35 children; PANDAS + moderate/severe OCD
DBPC, 6-week RCT of IVIG (1 g/kg/day on 2 consecutive days)
Double-blind phase: Mean decrease in CY-BOCS score was 24%  31% in the IVIG group (n = 17) and 12%  27% in the placebo group (n = 18); 6 responders in the IVIG group (35%) versus 4 (22%) in the placebo group; these differences were not statistically significant.

### Slide 69
Question 1

Early onset OCD is characterized by:

A) Increased comorbid tic disorders
B) Decreased comorbid ADHD
C) Onset of OCD precedes tics by many years
D) Minimal genetic loading

### Slide 70
Question 2

Common comorbid diagnoses with OCD include all of the following except:

A) ADHD and ODD
B) MDD and anxiety
C) Somatoform disorders
D) Motor tics

### Slide 71
Question 3

The following medications are effective in the treatment of OCD, except:

A) Clomipramine
B) Fluoxetine
C) Desipramine 
D) Fluvoxamine

### Slide 72
Question 4

The POTS trial in OCD found that the greatest reduction in CY-BOCS scores results from:

A) Sertraline alone
B) CBT alone
C) Combined CBT + Sertraline
D) Family Therapy

### Slide 73
Question 5

Criteria for diagnosing PANDAS include:

A) Motor and vocal tics
B) OCD of childhood onset
C) Tourette disorder
D) Sudden onset of OCD after a streptococcal infection

### Slide 74
Answers

1) A
2) C
3) C
4) C
5) D

### Slide 1
2020 Posttraumatic Stress Disorder AlgorithmFrom the Psychopharmacology Algorithm Project at the Harvard South Shore Program

ASCP Model Curriculum for Psychopharmacology,     
11th Edition 2020
David N. Osser, MD, Associate Professor of Psychiatry

### Slide 2

Updated from the last publication of this algorithm: Bajor L, Ticlea A, Osser DN. Harvard Review of Psychiatry 2011;19:240-258 


For access to all Harvard South Shore Algorithms, please visit www.psychopharm.mobi

### Slide 5
Pre-Lecture ExamQuestion 1

All of the following are true regarding the 2013 study of prazosin in active duty soldiers with PTSD by Raskind et al, except
	A.  Median final bedtime dose in males was 15.6 mg
	B.  Median final bedtime dose in females was 7 mg
	C.  The number needed to treat was 2.7
	D.  Significantly more patients on prazosin had lowered blood pressure than those on placebo

### Slide 6
Question 2

2. All of the following are true about trazodone as a hypnotic except
	A.  It is FDA-approved as a hypnotic agent
	B.  It was comparable to zolpidem in primary insomnia in a randomized, placebo-controlled study (N=278) in 2005
	C.  In 2005 it was the most widely prescribed medication used for hypnotic purposes in the US
	D.  Stephan Stahl in 2009 described it as an "ideal hypnotic agent" based on its pharmacodynamics

### Slide 7
Question 3

3. Which of the following statements about SSRIs for PTSD is not true?
	A.  Paroxetine and Sertraline are FDA-approved
	B.  Sertraline has never been found effective in studies of Veterans with PTSD
	C.  There are more positive studies showing efficacy for sertraline than for the antihypertensive prazosin
	D.  Sexual dysfunction, worsening of nightmares, and increased GI bleeding are common side effects

### Slide 8
Question 4

All of the following are true about venlafaxine for treatment of PTSD except
	A.  Effect size was greater than that of the SSRIs
	B.  There was no improvement in hyperarousal symptoms, insomnia, or nightmares
	C.  Small increases in pulse and blood pressure were noted.

### Slide 9
Question 5

All of the following have been compared with placebo for PTSD symptoms.  Positive results have occurred with
	A.  Valproate
	B.  Lamotrigine
	C.  Guanfacine
	D.  Topiramate
      E.   B and D

### Slide 10
Introduction

PTSD is associated with dysregulation of neurotransmitter, neuroendocrine, and neuroanatomical systems in the brain, thus, pharmacotherapy has potential role in treatment

Psychotherapeutic modalities in treatment of PTSD are very important.  
Medication is not necessarily first-line
This algorithm assists with choice of medication if you and the patient decide you want to use medication.

### Slide 11
Method of Algorithm Development

Core values of our algorithm working group:

Efficacy and long-term safety are given equal consideration
Knowledge of the evidence base is necessary but not sufficient for clinical decision-making; there will be instances where clinical judgement justifies deviation from these recommendations

### Slide 12
Introduction - 2

Paroxetine and sertraline remain the only FDA-approved medications for PTSD

As such, most other recent algorithms and guidelines recommend SSRIs as "first-line" 

Our focus on quality of evidence regarding SSRIs and other medications led us to propose a treatment sequence for PTSD that starts with treating sleep disturbance, usually with prazosin

### Slide 13
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

If comorbidities are present, they may alter the algorithm: 
Substance use disorders 
Depression
Bipolar disorder
Psychosis
Female patients of childbearing potential

Node 1: Diagnosis of PTSD

### Slide 14
Comorbidities - Substance Use

Avoid benzodiazepines

Desipramine has one small positive study with this comorbidity1 (no effect otherwise) 

Topiramate 300 mg helpful vs. placebo in a small study of alcoholics with PTSD2

Prazosin is not effective in patients who are actively drinking3

1 Petrakis et. al. 2011
2 Batki et. al. 2014
3 Verplaetse et. al 2019

### Slide 15
Comorbidities - Major Depression

Increases risk of developing PTSD

Patients with both diagnoses may not respond to antidepressants as well as those with depression alone

### Slide 16
Comorbidities - Bipolar Disorder

Screen for PTSD in bipolar patients (risk is significantly elevated)

Avoid antidepressants1 and skip to other options at the point where they are recommended in the algorithm

1 El-Malakh et al, 2015

### Slide 17
Comorbidities - Psychotic Symptoms

Could indicate a comorbid psychotic disorder or could be PTSD-related psychosis

If PTSD-related psychosis, consider skipping sleep phase of algorithm and move directly to an SSRI

### Slide 18
Comorbidities - Pregnancy Risk

Avoid paroxetine - schedule D due to risk of fetal septal defects

Fluoxetine may have comparable risk1

1 Reefhuis  et. al. 2015

### Slide 19
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

Rule out and address other contributing factors:
Sleep apnea
Excessive caffeine use
Sleep hygiene issues
Restless leg syndrome
Medical problems associated with sleep  
   fragmentation:
Pain, nocturia, nicotine withdrawal

Node 2: Is Sleep Disturbed?

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

### Slide 20
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

Sleep Problems in PTSD
Hyperarousal linked to difficulties initiating and/or maintaining sleep

Trauma-related nightmares

Disturbed awakenings w/o nightmare recollection

Node 2: Is Sleep Disturbed?

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

Sleep impairment - a core symptom in PTSD

### Slide 21
*Ensure other causes have been addressed

### Slide 22
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

TRY PRAZOSIN

Non-sedating alpha-1 antagonist
Of drugs in class, most able to cross blood brain barrier

Node 2a: Nightmares or 
Disturbed Awakenings?

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

### Slide 23
Prazosin

Of 8 placebo-controlled RCTs so far 5 have been positive
4 were small and from one site.  One was large and multicentered.
Very impressive effect sizes in first 4 studies
In most studies, prazosin was added to other medications

### Slide 24
Fourth Study: Raskind et al. 2013

Prazosin vs. Placebo
N=67 Active Duty Soldiers, > 80% male
15 weeks
16% dropout in each group     
Results:  on CGI, 64% responders to prazosin vs. 27% to placebo, NNT = 2.7
All other outcomes strongly favored prazosin
No difference in BP
Two placebo patients became suicidal

### Slide 25
Raskind et al. 2018

Does this negative study, though large, cancel out  all other positive data with prazosin?  NO
But it showed need to find predictors of response
Higher starting blood pressure predicted greater response (Raskind 2016) 
Predictors of poor response:  actively drinking alcoholics, suicidal patients (Verplaetse 2019, McCall 2018)
Prazosin still is the best medication option
 Contrast it with FDA-approved sertraline which has 4 RCTs in Veterans, all negative.

Raskind et al: 2013,2016,2018; Petrakis 2016; Ahmadpanah 2014

### Slide 26
Prazosin Uptitration Protocol for Men from Raskind 2013

At bedtime
1 mg for 2 nights   
2 mg for 5 nights
4 mg for 7 nights
6 mg for 7 nights
10 mg for 7 nights
15 mg for 7 nights
20 mg was maximum 
15.6 mg median dose

Mid-morning
Week 2:     1 mg
Week 3-4: 2 mg
Week 5-6: 5 mg

### Slide 27
Prazosin Uptitration for Women from Raskind 2013

At bedtime
1 mg for 2 nights   
2 mg for 12 nights
4 mg for 7 nights
6 mg for 7 nights
10 mg maximum
7 mg median dose

Mid-morning
Week 2-3: 1 mg
Week 4-5: 2 mg

(As of yet, there has been no explanation for why doses are lower for women)

### Slide 28
*Ensure other causes have been addressed

### Slide 29
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

Consider trazodone first, then hydroxyzine1 
If prazosin worked for NMs and disturbed awakenings but initial insomnia still present, consider adding trazodone or hydroxyzine
Consider clonidine - based on case series data

Node 2b: Sleep Initiation Difficulties

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

1 Ahmadpanah et. al. 2014

### Slide 30
Clonidine

Early evidence promising: retrospective study of 79 veterans, 72% experienced improved CGI score, 49% were "much improved" or "very much improved", minimal side effects reported1
Suggested use 0.1--0.2 mg qHS plus 0.05 tid prn daytime PTSD symptoms, can transition to patch if effective/tolerated.
Helpful for sleep onset as well as maintenance



1 Burek et al (poster at APA 2019, manuscript now under review, cross-over study w N=30 to start early 2021)

### Slide 31
Undesirable Initial Choices for Sleep

TCAs:  imipramine, amitriptyline, doxepin
Quetiapine (see next slide)
Benzodiazepines: 
Alprazolam ineffective1
Benzodiazepines have high potential for abuse by patients with PTSD2
May reduce short or long-term effectiveness of exposure therapy3
Might be considered in a few situations4

1 Braun et. al. 1990    
2 Chilcoat et. al.  1998
3 Rosen et. al. 2013
4 Pollack et. al. 2011

### Slide 32
Should Quetiapine be used 
as a Hypnotic?

"...benefits did not justify risks and it should not be used as a first-line treatment for insomnia"1
Side effects
Weight gain/metabolic side effects are not dose related and occur even at low doses
Dose related cardiac side effects2
Risk of tardive dyskinesia

1 Wine et. al.  2009
2 Ray et. al. 2009

### Slide 33
Next Steps...

Having completed the assessment and management of sleep difficulties, the next step in the algorithm is to assess whether there are significant remaining PTSD symptoms

If so, addition of medication that may address the general symptom clusters in PTSD should now be considered

SSRIs are first line

### Slide 34
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

Paroxetine/sertraline are FDA approved BUT
Evidence for all SSRIs is weak
Overall effect size SMD from placebo = 0.231 
Side effects: insomnia, sexual dysfunction, GI bleeding risk
Sertraline is approved only for women in the UK--not effective in studies w/ male combat veterans

Node 3: Have You Tried An SSRI?

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

(3): Have you tried an SSRl?

1 Hoskins et. al. 2015

### Slide 35
NICE Guidelines Findings on SSRI Effect Sizes

Paroxetine  0.41
Fluoxetine   0.02 - 0.28
Sertraline    0.06 - 0.26

0.5

Clinically meaningful

Clinically important

0.8

1 National Institute for Clinical Excellence, UK

### Slide 37
FDA-Approved SSRIs for PTSD: Older Evidence

### Slide 38
SSRIs for PTSD: Focus On The Newer Evidence

1 Zoellner et. al 2019

### Slide 39
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

PTSD-related psychotic symptoms:
Hearing soldiers scream 
Visual hallucinations of an attacker
Non-bizarre delusions

Node 3a: If no response and PTSD-related psychosis is present?

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

(3): Have you tried an SSRl?

### Slide 40
Yes

No

Yes

Was there any response?

### Slide 42
Quetiapine

(One always wonders in double-blind studies of quetiapine whether the blind was successful - the obvious side effects of sedation and the "munchies" make it easy to know if you were randomized to it)

### Slide 43
PTSD with psychosis: consider aripiprazole first?

Evidence for risperidone and quetiapine monotherapy are stronger
Consider aripiprazole first if side effects of the others are unacceptable, e.g., sedation, metabolic and cardiovascular effects

### Slide 44
Yes

No

Yes

Was there any response?

### Slide 45
(1) Diagnosis of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder

Evidence for SNRIs*:
Venlafaxine: 
Effect for re-experiencing and avoidance, but not hyperarousal, insomnia, or nightmares (possibly due to noradrenergic effects)1
Duloxetine: 
Improvement for PTSD, depression, anxiety  plus nightmare reduction2

Node 4: Tried a second SSRI, an SNRI, or mirtazapine?*

(2) Is Sleep Disturbed?

(3): Have you tried an SSRl?

(4): Have you tried a second SSRl, SNRI or mirtazapine?

*Elevated HR/BP may be contraindications for some patients.

1 Davidson et. al. 2006
2 Villareal et. al. 2010

### Slide 46
What about mirtazapine at Node 4?
maybe as an augmentation

Combined mirtazapine and sertraline improved PTSD and depression symptoms1,2

Combined mirtazapine and paroxetine produced faster response3

But latest mirtazapine monotherapy study showed zero efficacy4

1 Schiener et. al. 2015
2 Xing et. al. 2018
3 Sulejmanpasic-arslanagic et. al. 2016
4 Davies et. al. 2020

### Slide 47
Yes

No

Yes

Was there any response?

### Slide 48
Node 5.  Tried a third medication?  Consider another SSRI, SNRI, mirtazapine, or daytime prazosin.

Some of the prazosin studies noted earlier used a morning dose for daytime symptoms of hyperarousal, irritability from triggers, hypervigilance with good results (e.g., Raskind et al 2015).
  
Clonidine might be used this way also

### Slide 49
Node 5a.  What is left for treatment-resistant cases?

We list some remaining options in a table

 Supporting evidence ranges from unconvincing to sometimes fairly robust

### Slide 51
More options:

There is some evidence for transcranial magnetic stimulation1, CBD2, ketamine3, and stellate ganglion block4  
None has sufficient evidence to have an earlier spot in the algorithm
Cannabis: some evidence shows increases in anger/irritability5 

1 Watts 2012, Nam 2013, Oznur 2014, Carpenter 2018, Kozel 2018, Fryml 2019, Kozel 2019, Ahmadizadeh 2018 
2 Greer 2014, Elms 2019, Bedard-Gilligan 2018,  Bonn-Miller 2011,  Wilkinson 2015.  
3 Feder 2014, Albott 2018, Hartberg 2018, Abdallah 2019
4 Olmstead 2019, Stein 2020, Hanling 2016
5 Wilkinson 2015

### Slide 52
Summary and Conclusion

Our algorithm authors proposed in 2011 that the first-line medication for PTSD featured by prominent nightmares and disturbed awakenings might be prazosin, before an SSRI. 

In 2020 this recommendation still seems based on a reasonable interpretation of the current evidence.  Other anti-adrenergic agents (doxazosin, clonidine) are also promising

SSRIs and SNRIs have only minor benefits

### Slide 1
2020 Generalized Anxiety Disorder AlgorithmFrom the Psychopharmacology Algorithm Project at the Harvard South Shore Program

David N. Osser, MD, Associate Professor of Psychiatry
Harmony Abejuela, MD, Formerly, Clinical Fellow in Psychiatry
Harvard Medical School

### Slide 2
Things to Know about these Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 9/18/2020.

### Slide 3
Updated from the last publication of this algorithm: Abejuela H, Osser DN. Harvard Review of Psychiatry 2016;24(4):243-56

### Slide 4
The authors have no financial relationships with any 
pharmaceutical or other commercial firms.

Disclosure Slide

### Slide 5
WWW.PSYCHOPHARM.MOBIis the website of the Harvard South Shore Algorithm Project.  All the algorithms are there.

### Slide 6
Pre- and Post-Lecture ExamQuestion 1

All of the following are FDA-approved for GAD except
	A.  Escitalopram
	B.  Gabapentin
	C.  Buspirone
	D.  Duloxetine     
      E.  Alprazolam

### Slide 7
Question 2

All of the following, though not FDA-approved for GAD, have positive placebo-controlled trials, except
	A.  Hydroxyzine
	B.  Pregabalin
	C.  Lavender oil
	D.  Clonidine
    E.  Sertraline

### Slide 8
Question 3

Which of the following treatments for GAD has a D rating for use in pregnancy due to risk of fetal atrial septal defects?
	A.  Paroxetine
	B.  Buspirone 
	C.  Venlafaxine 
	D.  Alprazolam

### Slide 9
Question 4

Which of the following was shown to be superior to an SSRI in a randomized controlled study in GAD patients?
	A.  Valproic acid
	B.  Bupropion
	C.  Kava
	D.  Quetiapine

### Slide 10
Question 5

Which has a controlled trial with positive results as an augmentation of SSRIs after unsatisfactory response in GAD?
	A. Pregabalin
	B. Benzodiazepines
	C. Hyroxyzine
	D. Quetiapine

### Slide 11
Outline of Lecture

Why an algorithm for Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD)?
Comorbidities and other conditions that could change the algorithm
The algorithm:
FDA-approved medications
Off-label medications with evidence
Role of benzodiazepines
Treatment-resistant GAD cases

### Slide 12
Generalized Anxiety Disorder

Chronic, debilitating
Most common anxiety disorder in primary care setting
7 million Americans have it

Davidson et al, A psychopharmacological treatment algorithm for generalized anxiety disorder. 
J Psychopharmacol 2010; 24:3-26.

### Slide 13
Introduction (Cont.)

Psychosocial interventions are important in GAD
But, this algorithm focuses only on medications 
Consider algorithm if clinician and patient determine that medication is desirable
In some patients, a combination of medication and psychotherapy is utilized, though the effectiveness of combination treatment compared to either treatment alone is unclear (Bandelow 2007 - meta-analysis)

### Slide 14
Medications for GAD

FDA-approved options 
SSRIs: escitalopram, paroxetine
SNRIs: venlafaxine, duloxetine 
Benzodiazepine: alprazolam 
Azapirone (buspirone) 
Off-label options
Other SSRIs and benzodiazepines
Hydroxyzine, pregabalin, bupropion, kava, lavender oil.  ? gabapentin

### Slide 15
Node 1: DSM-5 Criteria for GAD

Excessive worry occurring more days than not for at least 6 months
Difficult to control the worry
Associated with 3 or > of the following: (only one item required in children)
1. Restlessness or feeling keyed up or on edge
2. Being easily fatigued
3. Difficulty concentration
4. Irritability
5. Muscle tension
6. Sleep disturbance
D.  Causes significant impairment
DSM 5 American Psychiatric Association, 2013

### Slide 16
Beginning of the Algorithm: Node 1: Met DSM Criteria for GADNode 2:  Evaluate for Comorbidity

### Slide 17
Node 2-2A: Evaluate for Comorbidity and Special Patient Populations... first one is Sleep Disturbance/Insomnia

Multiple contributing causes
Some treatments may result in worsening insomnia (e.g., SSRIs and SNRIs)
More sedating agents like hydroxyzine and pregabalin (not approved in U.S. for GAD) may be preferred over SSRIs and SNRIs (Holsboer-Trachsler 2013)
Add Trazodone to SSRI (Kaynak 2004, Nierenberg 1994)
Add Benzodiazepines but may cause rebound effects the night after stopping (Pollack 2008, Walsh 2007)

### Slide 18
Node 2A (Cont.): Elderly

All medications have side effects/monitor closely
SSRIs: sertraline or escitalopram
Gait impairment, gastrointestinal bleeding, bone loss, low sodium levels
SNRIs: venlafaxine (raises blood pressure at ~200 mg)
Pregabalin -sleepiness, dizziness, and falls with fracture occurred (Montgomery 2008)
Benzodiazepines - falls, decreased respiratory drive, auto accidents, substance use, sedation, but probably NOT Alzheimer's Disease (Salzman 2020)
Quetiapine - metabolic risks and increase in QTc  - must monitor

### Slide 19
Node 2A (Cont.): Women of child-bearing potential and who may become pregnant during treatment

Benzodiazepines: Category D rating for pregnancy due to cleft palate risk
SSRIs and SNRIs: all Category C rating except paroxetine: category D due to atrial septal defects (Reefhuis 2015 - also found fluoxetine almost as bad)
SSRIs may affect risks of postpartum hemorrhage, premature delivery, and other postnatal complications (hard to tell because of confounding by indication)

### Slide 20
Node 2A (Cont.): Active Substance Use Disorders

~15% with this comorbidity (Kessler 1996)
Avoid benzodiazepines
Pregabalin is prone to abuse (schedule IV)
Gabapentin probably has comparable abuse potential.  Evidence-base for pregabalin and gabapentin to be discussed.

### Slide 21
Node 2A (Cont.): Major Depression

GAD and depression can co-exist and be distinguishable (Goldberg 2010)
Major depression with comorbid anxiety responds much less well than without anxiety (Fava 2008 - STAR*D)
Speculate that there is a role for benzodiazepines as augmentation of antidepressants in these cases
Hydroxyzine and pregabalin could work

### Slide 22
Node 2A (Cont.): Bipolar Depression

Rates of GAD are higher in bipolar compared with unipolar depression 
Antidepressants normally good for GAD such as SSRIs not recommended in bipolar rapid cyclers or mixed (Pacchiarotti 2013)
Even if not RC or mixed, some studies find increased risk of mood destabilization with antidepressants (Leverich 2006, McGirr 2016)

### Slide 23
Recommendations for Bipolar Depression

For bipolar depression: consider lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, and cariprazine
For GAD: consider adding hydroxyzine, pregabalin, and benzodiazepines
Surprise: quetiapine was not effective in the one randomized trial in bipolar depression comorbid with GAD (Gao 2014)

### Slide 24
Node 2A (Cont.) : Posttraumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD)

Prazosin 
 Add-on for nightmares, and disturbed awakenings, supported by 5 of 8 placebo-controlled studies as of 2020.  See Raskind et al., 2013 for a good dosing protocol.  Also helps with daytime symptoms (e.g., with 5 mg at 10-11 AM)
Benzodiazepines thought not effective and have high abuse potential in PTSD

### Slide 26
Node 3.  First-Line Recommendation:An SSRI

### Slide 27
Node 3: SSRIs

SSRIs: 1st-line medication for uncomplicated cases of GAD
FDA-approved: escitalopram and paroxetine 
Sertraline: comparable efficacy in 3 randomized controlled trials (RCTs) 
Citalopram: QTc prolongation (Bird 2014)
Overall effect size of SSRIs: Cohen's d = 0.36 (modest effect) - See Hidalgo 2007

### Slide 28
Problems with SSRIs

Sexual side effects (tough to discuss, do not remit with time)
GI bleeding 9x more common over 3 months if on SSRI and ibuprofen (or similar) than control group not on NSAIDs, but mitigated if patient on omeprazole or similar
Suicidal ideation 2% > placebo if age <25
Cascade et al., Real-world data on SSRI Antidepressant Side Effects. Psychiatry (Edgmont) 2009;6:16-18. 
de Abajo et al., Risk of upper GI bleeding associated with SSRIs and venlafaxine therapy. Arch Gen Psychiatry 2008:65:795-803.  Wang et al., Short-term use of serotonin reuptake inhibitors and risk of GI bleeding. Am J Psychiatry 2014;171:54-61. Richards et al., Effect of selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors on the risk of fracture. Arch Intern Med 2007;167:188-94. Pelissolo. Efficacy and tolerability of escitalopram in anxiety disorders: A Review. Encephale 2008;34:400-8.

### Slide 31
Node 3A: Alternatives to an SSRI as the First-Line Choice

If the risks of adverse effects from an SSRI are unacceptable, several options to consider (not necessarily in order of preference):
Duloxetine (over venlafaxine): maybe first choice
Buspirone
Hydroxyzine
Pregabalin
Bupropion
Why NOT Benzodiazepines?

### Slide 32
Duloxetine

FDA-approved with comparable efficacy to SSRIs
Favored SNRI to try because
Blood pressure effects comparable to SSRIs
Lower rates of sexual side effects at least compared with paroxetine (Delgado 2005)
Contraindicated in patients with liver impairment 
Perspiration and infrequent urinary retention (0.4%) are other concerns (Whiskey 2013)

### Slide 33
Duloxetine- 2

Effective at 60-120 mg with no advantage to the higher dose (Carter 2009)
Common adverse effects: nausea, dizziness, dry mouth, fatigue, somnolence, constipation
Also FDA-approved for pain in diabetic neuropathy, chronic musculoskeletal pain, and for fibromyalgia
However, it is a MYTH that the effect on pain is clinically significant in depressed patients. Effect size (Cohen's d) = 0.115 (Spielmans 2008), 0.13-0.25 (Gebhardt et al 2016)

### Slide 34
Buspirone

Effective at 30 mg daily (Bandelow 2013)
Useful in placebo-controlled trial in abstinent anxious alcoholics at average dose of 50 mg daily (Kranzler 1994)
No abuse potential, good side effect profile
Little overdose toxicity, no cognitive or psychomotor impairment, no sexual side effects
FDA-approved, but only one placebo-controlled trial to confirm efficacy in DSM-IV or 5 GAD
Worth consideration given benign side effects

### Slide 35
Hydroxyzine

Antihistamine with 5-HT2 receptor blocking effects
Efficacy at 50 mg daily - 3 controlled studies (e.g., Llorca 2002)
Low abuse potential, sedating properties, lack of sexual side effects
Mean effect size of 0.45 (Guaiana 2010)
However, no known benefit for comorbid disorders like depression or other anxiety disorders
Commonly used as a "PRN" because of its rapid onset of sedation (15-30 minutes), half-life of 3 hours, and duration of effect lasting between 4-6 hrs
Increases QTc: Maximum daily dose now = 100 mg

### Slide 36
Pregabalin

Approved in Europe for GAD
Has 7 positive RCTs versus placebo (Wensel 2012)
Recommended 1st line in international guidelines
Not approved in U.S. for GAD: rejected twice 
Compared to SSRIs: rapid onset, helps with sleep
Dose-dependent effect: > 300 mg daily more effective (Bech 2007)
Effective in elderly patients with caution re: falls
Side effects: somnolence, dizziness, increased risk for fractures in elderly

### Slide 37
Pregabalin vs. Gabapentin

Speculate that gabapentin could be used instead of pregabalin
But: no controlled studies of gabapentin in GAD
However...
They share indications for neuropathic pain and seizures
Gabapentin frequently used off-label as an anxiolytic
Gabapentin was effective for chronic anxiety states, e.g., postoperative anxiety (Clarke 2013)

### Slide 38
Gabapentin

In another trial, gabapentin at 300 mg and 900 mg was superior to placebo for anxiety symptoms in 420 breast cancer survivors (Lavigne 2012)
Right now, gabapentin is much less expensive
However, as noted earlier, concerns have been raised about its potential for misuse - so avoid in patients with history of substance use disorders (Schifano 2011, Smith 2015)

### Slide 39
Bupropion

Clinicians do not tend to consider bupropion for any kind of anxiety...  HOWEVER...
Double-blind controlled trial: 24 outpatients aged 18-64 with DSM-IV GAD (Bystritsky 2008)
Randomized: bupropion XL 150-300 mg/day or escitalopram, 10-20 mg/day
Efficacy measures: Clinical Global Impression of Improvement and HAM-A
RESULTS: Endpoint scores favored bupropion (5.3) over escitalopram (11.4), p = 0.01
Response and remission rates favored bupropion
Differences in depression ratings non-significant

### Slide 40
Bupropion: indirect support

Bupropion vs. sertraline in patients with anxiety symptoms and MDD (Trivedi 2001)
Found comparable anxiolytic and antidepressant effects with onset of improvement equally rapid
No differences in activating side effects or insomnia
STAR*D (Fava 2008) found augmentation with bupropion gave 18% remissions vs. 9% buspirone
Zimmerman 2005: Suggested consider bupropion as 1st-line over SSRIs for MDD

### Slide 41
Why NOT a Benzodiazepine as a first-line option??  They are the MOST PRESCRIBED anxiolytic agents world-wide

Advantages
 Alprazolam got FDA-approval for GAD at the time of DSM III
 Improvement is rapid
 Clonazepam (long acting) has been used to improve symptoms while waiting for an SSRI to work
 Both somatic and psychic symptoms improve (Lydiard 2011, Bandelow 2013)

### Slide 42
Benzodiazepines Now the Bad News - growing concerns

Sedation
Memory impairment
Short term - well known
Is long term use (> 3 months) associated with increased risk of Alzheimer's disease or other dementias?  Latest study suggests probably not (Osler et al, AJP 2020)
Psychomotor dysfunction
Falls especially in the elderly (see Olfson 2015 for rebuttal of claims that these concerns are exaggerated and that BZs are safer than long-term antidepressants)
Increased car accidents (Rapoport 2009)

### Slide 43
Benzodiazepines: More concerns

40% patients develop tolerance or dependence or rebound withdrawal  (Salzman et al 1990, Soldatos 1999, Huh 2011)
Respiratory depression: avoid in patients with sleep apnea (Loachimescu 2012)
FDA 2017 added black box warning about combining with opiates - can be fatal
Avoid in patients with substance use disorders

### Slide 44
Griffiths and Wolf. J Clin Psychopharmacol 1990:237-43.

Drug abuser ratings of relative "high"

### Slide 47
Node 3B: Tried SSRI and Got Partial Response?

Consider Augmentations:
 Hydroxyzine
 Pregabalin
 Benzodiazepine

(No second-generation antipsychotic until third trial)

### Slide 48
Augmentation for Partial Response

Consider if partial improvement thought to be due to medication and NOT due to psychotherapy and/or other aspects of treatment
Avoid adding another medication when partial response to first medication likely from placebo
Evaluate for other causes of incomplete response
Comorbidity
Non-adherence
Pharmacokinetic/pharmacogenetic variables

### Slide 49
More Favored Augmenting Agents

Hydroxyzine: no GAD augmentation trials but effective as monotherapy
Pregabalin: one placebo-controlled augmentation trial showing small effect with 150-600 mg/day (Rickels et al. 2012) 
Benzodiazepines: no GAD augmentation trials but effective as monotherapy
Trial of clonazepam added to sertraline for social anxiety disorder: effective and safe (Pollack 2014)
BUT: needs to be studied in GAD

### Slide 50
Why Not Buspirone?

No studies using buspirone as augmenting agent in GAD without depression
Buspirone was not impressive in STAR*D as augmenter in patients with depression and comorbid anxiety (Fava et al. 2008)
Therefore, not a prime option for augmentation

### Slide 51
Why Not Antipsychotic Augmentation for Partial Response?

Quetiapine: 3 placebo-controlled augmentation RCTs - only one (small) study was positive
    (Altamura 2011)
Risperidone: 2 positive augmentation studies
Olanzapine: 1 small study, added to SSRIs
However, given high risks of metabolic side effects, negative effects on insulin resistance, 
    and weight gain, 2nd generation antipsychotics not recommended at this point

### Slide 54
Node 4: Try a 2nd SSRI or Duloxetine

Unfortunately, no studies of what to do next after failure on initial SSRI
SSRIs, though, are not identical in spectra of receptor activity
It may be that a different one will have better constellation of effects to address patient's needs

### Slide 55
Node 4A: Alternatives to the First-Line Options for the Second Trial

Buspirone
Hydroxyzine
Pregabalin
Bupropion
Benzodiazepine
Venlafaxine
Agomelatine (not available in the United States)
Herbs: Kava, Rhodiola rosea, and lavender oil

### Slide 56
New Alternatives Offered at Node 4ABenzodiazepines

Once again, avoid if history of comorbid SUDs
Possible exceptions to #1 (Osser 1999): 
History of response without misuse
Well-motivated patient with sustained period of abstinence from other substances
Short term use in high risk suicidal patients with panic attacks
Temporarily while waiting for a longer-acting agent to work
Alprazolam: FDA-approved, while other BZs are 
      off-label

### Slide 57
Node 4A (Cont.): Venlafaxine

Reasonable here, though duloxetine better choice if not tried yet
Remissions most likely at maximum dose of 225 mg daily (Rickels 2010)
Maintenance efficacy (Rickels 2010)
In a comparison with escitalopram and placebo, discontinuations due to adverse effects from venlafaxine 13% vs. 5% on placebo, p=0.03); escitalopram = placebo in terms of side effects 
    (Bose 2008)
Common adverse effects: dry mouth, drowsiness, headache, increased sweating

### Slide 58
Node 4A (Cont.): Agomelatine

"Antidepressant" that works by blocking serotonin 2C receptors and stimulating melatonin receptors
20 RCTs in depression: overall effect size d=0.24 (Taylor et al 2014)
Did not do well in studies in U.S., so did not get FDA approval and not available in U.S.
For GAD: three 12-week placebo-controlled RCTs showing efficacy at 10-50 mg daily and one 26-week maintenance RCT (Stein et al 2012,14,17)
Comparable to escitalopram 10-20 mg (Stein et al 2018): agomelatine 61% response, escitalopr 65%
Helpful for sleep with minimal side effects

### Slide 59
Node 4A: (Cont.): Kava (Piper methysticum)

A 2013 study comparing kava (120/240 mg of kavalactones/daily) with placebo in 6-week trial in 75 GAD patients (Sarris et al 2013)
Efficacy on HAM-A, p = 0.046
Effect size by Cohen's d = 0.62
26% remissions on kava (Ham-A < 7) vs. 6% on placebo
Side effects: headaches
However, earlier trials of had mixed results and reports of severe hepatotoxicity (Russmann 2001)

### Slide 60
Other Herbs: Rhodax (Rhodiola rosea)

Small study from UCLA Anxiety Disorders Program (Bystritsky et al 2008)
10 GAD participants ages 34-44 yo
Rhodax extract (340 mg) given daily for 10 weeks
Significant improvement on HAM-A (p = 0.01)
However, anticholinergic side effects

### Slide 61
Other Herbs: Lavender Oil

2014 German study in 539 patients found efficacy compared with placebo (Kasper et al 2014, 2017)
Optimal dose was 80-160 mg daily
Very well tolerated
Comparable to 20 mg of paroxetine in the 2014 study.
Marketed there under brand name Silexan.

### Slide 63
Partial Response to Second Medication Trial?

### Slide 64
Node 4B: Got Partial Response to the Second Trial?

If the second SSRI or duloxetine resulted in partial response thought to be from the medication, then consider the same augmentations as in Node 3B
 Hydroxyzine
 Pregabalin
 Benzodiazepine
(No second-generation antipsychotic until third trial)

### Slide 66
No Response to the 2nd GAD Trial?

### Slide 67
Node 5: Tried an SNRI?

If patient has had  two trials (from among the recommended 1st and 2nd line treatments in Nodes 3 and 4) and has not had a trial with SNRI, then the recommended third monotherapy trial is with an SNRI

### Slide 69
Node 5/5A: Try an SNRI or Alternative

### Slide 70
Node 5A: Alternatives to the First-Line Choice for the Third Trial

Try an alternative from Nodes 3A and 4A: buspirone, hydroxyzine, pregabalin, bupropion, benzodiazepine, agomelatine, or herbs 
OR - additional alternatives for the third trial
Second generation antipsychotics (SGAs)
Quetiapine
Risperidone 
(Avoid olanzapine)
Valproate

### Slide 71
FINAL REMARKS

In studies of algorithm usage, better results occur when time is spent educating users in accurate use and educating patients about the reasoning involved in their care decisions. (Osser 2013)
Algorithms should not be followed rigidly.  Add clinical experience, special considerations for individual patients

	A.  Escitalopram
	B.  Gabapentin
	C.  Buspirone
	D.  Duloxetine     
      E.  Alprazolam

Question 2

All of the following, though not FDA-approved for GAD, have positive placebo-controlled trials, except
	A.  Hydroxyzine
	B.  Pregabalin
	C.  Lavender oil
	D.  Clonidine
    E.  Sertraline

### Slide 74
Question 3

Which of the following treatments for GAD has a D rating for use in pregnancy due to risk of fetal atrial septal defects?
	A.  Paroxetine
	B.  Buspirone 
	C.  Venlafaxine 
	D.  Alprazolam

### Slide 75
Question 4

Which of the following was shown to be superior to an SSRI in a randomized controlled study in GAD patients?
	A.  Valproic acid
	B.  Bupropion
	C.  Kava
	D.  Quetiapine

### Slide 76
Question 5

Which has a controlled trial with positive results as an augmentation of SSRIs after unsatisfactory response in GAD?

	B. Benzodiazepines

	D. Quetiapine

Answers to Questions

	2.  D

### Slide 8
Major Points/Outline

Dementia is underrecognized and undertreated in primary care and in mental health settings.
Dementia can be recognized and treated beneficially in primary care and mental health settings.
Both pharmacological and nonpharmacological interventions may benefit overall brain health and dementia course.

8

*

### Slide 9
Major Points (cont.)

Neuroimaging with PET scanning can show a pattern of regional glucose metabolism and/or accumulation of beta amyloid, improving early detection of Alzheimer's disease with greater specificity.
Novel approaches to in vivo plaque and tangle imaging will be useful in monitoring potential disease-modifying agents.

9

### Slide 11
DSM 5 "Major Neurocognitive Disorder"

Evidence of significant cognitive decline in 1 or more cognitive domains based on 
	1. Expressed concern,  AND 
	2. Substantial cognitive impairment (assessed 		quantitatively)
B. Cognitive deficits interfere with independence in everyday    	activities
C. Not Delirium
D. Not another mental disorder
*Specify: AD, FTLD, LBD, VD, TBI, SUD, HIV, prion, PD, HD, other, multiple, unspecified

Adapted from APA: Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition. Arlington, VA, APA, 2013, p 602.

Neuroimaging and Dementia

Recommended by American Academy of Neurology in assessment of memory complaints.
Can reveal alternate/additional pathology, e.g. subdural hematoma
MRI better for seeing atrophy (especially hippocampal), small vessel disease, some unusual disorders.
CT can be adequate and more convenient for  agitated patients.

### Slide 26
Functional Imaging: Amyloid Tracer (Pittsburgh Compound B or PIB)Glucose Metabolism (18FDG PET)

Frontal, parietal, temporal, occipital cortices and striatum of AD patients retained PIB 
PIB retention correlated inversely with cerebral glucose metabolism determined with 18F-fluorodeoxyglucose

Klunk et al: Annals of Neurology 2004;55:306-19

*

### Slide 27
Appropriate Use Criteria for Amyloid PET1*

Yes	
 
persistent or progressive and unexplained mild cognitive impairment 
 
possible AD with unusual features such as rapid course, unexpected symptoms or concurrent medical issues

unusually early onset of dementia and possibility of benefit from different treatment or from referral to a clinical trial on the basis of a scan result

No

typical AD symptoms, age of onset, and course
 
without symptoms or with symptoms not confirmed by clinical examination

seeking to know risk for AD because of family history or particular ApoE genotype

strong family history of early onset AD and seeking an alternative to genotyping 
 
screening for insurance coverage, employment, or other nonmedical purposes

Grundman et al. Dement Geriatr Cogn Disord. 2016;41(1-2):80-92
* Not currently reimbursed by insurers

Delaying Onset Reduces Prevalence/Costs

### Slide 30
Current Challenges in Dementia Diagnosis

Primary care physicians (PCPs)
PCPs care for most dementia patients (64%) 
Barrett et al, 1997 found that only 40% of PCPs knew Alzheimer's was most common cause of late-life memory loss (vs. 97% of experts)
PCPs usually do not use standardized dementia diagnostic criteria
Missed diagnosis*
>75% of patients with moderate dementia 
97% of patients with mild dementia
Underrecognition of dementia leads to increased:
Motor vehicle accidents
ER visits	
Hospitalization rates		
Medication errors
Mortality

*Callahan et al. Ann Intern Med 1995;122:422-9.

### Slide 31
Dementia Management Requires A Comprehensive Approach

Education of patient/family/caregivers
Support of caregivers
Determination of locus for care
Home, Day Programs, Long Term Care, Hospice Care
Behavioral care
Safety, structure, stimulation
Behavioral response to noncognitive symptoms
Medical care
Address medical risk factors and aggravating factors
Medications for cognitive/noncognitive symptoms

*

*

### Slide 32
Rationale for Cholinergic Treatments of AD

Cholinergic function including choline acetyltransferase (CAT) activity is reduced with aging1
Number of cholinergic neurons (particularly in basal forebrain) is reduced in late AD2
In AD, nicotinic receptors in hippocampus and cortex are reduced1,3

32

1Bartus RT et al. Science. 1982;217:408-414; 2Whitehouse PJ et al. Science. 1982;215:1237-1239; 3Guan ZZ et al. J Neurochem. 2000;74:237-243 (from Small G: Dementia. ACNP Curriculum)

### Slide 41
Donepezil Effect On Caregiver Time

24 wk RCT, n=290, donepezil 5    10 mg/d vs placebo
Baseline carer time (ADLs+iADLs) ~300 min/d
Donepezil vs placebo reduced mean daily carer time for iADLs + ADLs by 52.4 min (p=0.004)
Self-reported daily care time reportedly remained stable      or decreased for:
72% of donepezil caregivers 
52% of placebo caregivers (p=0.002)

Data from Feldman H, et al. J Am Geriatr Soc. 2003;51:737-744.

### Slide 44
Clinical/Safety Issues

Before use, assess medical and psychosocial factors
Side effects lower with slower titration 
Common: nausea, vomiting, dyspepsia, anorexia, diarrhea, insomnia, vivid dreams, fatigue, increased urination, cramps
Uncommon: syncope, bradycardia, confusion, depression, agitation
Caution with liver/gastric disease, COPD,  bradycardia, sick sinus, inadequate supervision 
Dropouts due to adverse events in trials:
7-32% on active drug
1-8% on placebo

### Slide 47
Pharmacology of Memantine

Aminoadamantane derivative (1-amino-3,5-dimethyladamantane)
NMDA receptor uncompetitive (open channel) antagonist - low/moderate affinity
5-HT3 receptor allosteric antagonist of low/moderate affinity
Binds with low affinity to human nicotinic acetylcholine receptors - but does not alter AChE activity in the presence or absence of AChEIs

### Slide 49
Memantine: Characteristics

*

### Slide 50
Memantine: Clinical Pharmacokinetics

100% oral bioavailability
Crosses blood-brain barrier rapidly
No effects of food, age and gender
Linear, dose-proportional kinetics (range 5- 40 mg) 
Elimination half-life of 60 - 80 hours
Basis for dosing recommendation:
BID dosing better tolerated than QD dosing in early trials
Up-titration improves tolerability
Metabolism: 
Eliminated mostly in urine as parent drug and inactive metabolites 
Lower dose recommended in moderate renal disease.
No or minimal effects on P450 isoenzymes
No PK/PD interactions with donepezil

50

*

### Slide 51
Range Of Memantine Effects

Cognitive effects
ADLs
NCBS
Caregiver burden

*

### Slide 52
Clinical/Safety Issues

Before use, assess medical and psychosocial factors
Side effects:
More common: headache, constipation
Uncommon: confusion
Agitation less frequent with Memantine than with placebo
Caution with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance < 30 mL/min)

*

### Slide 53
Cognitive Enhancer Polypharmacy: Cholinesterase Inhibitors & Memantine

RCT assessed addition of memantine 10 mg bid to donepezil 10 mg/d in subjects with moderate to severe dementia.
Addition of memantine to donepezil was associated with significant benefits in domains of cognition, ADLs, and behaviors (agitation, lability, eating behavior)
Augmentation of other ChEIs with memantine may also be beneficial, has been investigated less.

Tariot PN, Farlow MR, Grossberg GT, et al. JAMA. 2004;291:317-24

*

### Slide 54
When To Stop Cognitive Enhancers

Consider stopping for:
Adverse effects (gastrointestinal, insomnia, urinary frequency, syncope, worsening confusion, and agitation)
Emergence of new medical contraindications
Poor compliance/poor supervision
Rapid decline 
Benefits are rapidly lost on discontinuation1 even in late-stage patients2
Discontinuation concerns with memantine are less well described/understood
Clinical recommendation: taper before stopping, resume if decompensation observed.

Doody et al. Arch Neurol. 2001;58:427-433.
Herrman and Gauthier. CMAJ. 2008;179:1279-1287.

*

### Slide 55
Warning: Many Alternative Therapies are Advocated - Proceed with Caution

Nutraceuticals advocated for AD include:
Fish Oil (may be beneficial prior to onset of cognitive decline)
Ginkgo biloba extract (not proven useful)
Ginseng/Adaptogens (may be hazardous)
Coconut oil (not proven useful)
Ventricular shunt (limited data)
Please remember that even when use of a non-effective, non-evidence-based treatment is not harmful in itself, it still may delay or prevent the use of other treatments with established usefulness.

*

The Spectrum Of Non-Cognitive Behavioral Symptoms (NCBS) In Dementia

### Slide 57
NCBS are common in patients with dementia
Prevalence 60-80%
Lifetime risk approaches 100%
NCBS are associated with significant morbidity, more rapid functional decline1,2
No medication is FDA indicated for NCBS
There are no established standards for the management of NCBS

Lyketsos CG et al.  Am J Psychiatry 2000;157:708-714.
Tractenberg RE et al. J Neuropsychiatry Clin Neurosci 2002; 14:11-18.

Importance Of NCBS

### Slide 58
DICE: A Systematic Nonpharmacologic          Approach to NCBS

Kales et al. JAGS 2014;62:762-9.

Describe

Investigate

Create

Evaluate

*

### Slide 59
Describe

Caregiver describes problematic behavior
Context (who, what, when and where)
Social and physical environment
Patient perspective
Degree of distress to patient and caregiver

Kales et al. JAGS 2014;62:762-9.

### Slide 60
Investigate

Patient
Medication side effects
Pain
Functional limitations
Medical conditions
Psychiatric comorbidity
Severity of cognitive impairment, executive dysfunction
Poor sleep hygiene
Sensory changes
Fear, sense of loss of control, boredom
Caregiver effects/expectations
Social and physical environment
Cultural factors

Kales et al. JAGS 2014;62:762-9.

### Slide 61
NCBS Treatment Requires A Differential Diagnosis

Medical:
Medication toxicity
Pain (e.g. constipation)
Sensory deficit
Medical illness
Environmental (e.g. frustration, conflict, boredom, interpersonal stress, sensory overload)
Psychiatric
Comorbid diagnosis: anxiety, depression, mania, psychosis
Secondary to dementia

*

### Slide 62
Create

Respond to physical problems
Strategize behavioral interventions
Providing caregiver education and support
Enhancing communication with the patient
Creating meaningful activities for the patient
Simplifying tasks
Ensuring the environment is safe
Increasing or decreasing stimulation in the environment

Kales et al. JAGS 2014;62:762-9.

### Slide 63
Behavioral Interventions: The First LineTreatment for NCBS

Assure safety / Adequate supervision
Behavioral analysis: Identify precipitants and response  
Behavioral interventions can include:
Caregiver education
Prosthetic (habilitative) environment
Activity/exercise
Distraction and redirection
Individualized music therapy
Aromatherapy / massage
Treatment should not exceed patient's capacity to learn/remember

*

### Slide 64
Evaluate

Has the intervention(s) been effective for the problem behavior?
Have there been any unintended consequences or "side effects" from the intervention(s)?
Which interventions did the caregiver implement?
If the caregiver did not implement the interventions, why?
What changes in the environment have been made?

Kales et al. JAGS 2014;62:762-9.

Definition of Apathy

Apathy1:

Impaired ADLs
In AD: up to 92% of severely impaired2

2. Landes et al. J Am Geriatr Soc. 2001;49:1700-7.

Distinguishing Apathy from Depression1

APATHY

### Slide 69
Treatment of Apathy in Dementia

Behavioral treatments
Maintain meaningful activities
Encourage preexisting interests
Structured music and art therapy program better than "free activities"
Schedule pleasant activities at energy nadirs
Potential pharmacologic treatments
Consider
Cholinesterase inhibitors +
Memantine +/-
Methylphenidate +
Antidepressants: Bupropion +, SSRIs +/-
Antipsychotics -, Anticonvulsants -
Avoid overmedication
Take drug interactions into account

Landes et al. J Am Geriatr Soc. 2001;49:1700-7; 
Galynker et al. J Neuropsychiatry Clin Neurosci. 1997;9:231-9.

### Slide 70
Depression in Alzheimer's Disease

Prevalence of clinically significant depression in AD: 30-50% (half or more is minor)
Rate in Vascular Dementia may be higher
Not solely determined by
awareness of dementia
severity of cognitive impairment 
Proposed mechanisms
Noradrenergic cell loss in locus ceruleus
Serotonergic cell loss in dorsal raphe nuclei

Lyketsos and Olin. Biol Psychiatry 2002;52:243-52.

### Slide 71
Provisional Criteria: Depression in AD

3 or more of following in 2 week period
Depressed mood
Decreased positive affect/pleasure in usual activities/contacts
Social isolation or withdrawal
Disruption in appetite
Disruption in sleep
Psychomotor changes
Irritability
Fatigue/loss of energy
Worthlessness, hopelessnesss, guilt
Thoughts of death, SI or behavior

Olin et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry. 2002 Mar-Apr;10:125-8.

Meets criteria for DAT
Distress or disruption
Not delirium, drug, 
medication, or better 
accounted for by 
other conditions

### Slide 72
Also Note "Masked" Depression in Persons with Dementia

Likelihood that depression is present is increased in the presence of:
Delusions1
Verbal/physical aggressive behaviors2
Suicidal or self-destructive behaviors
Disruptive vocalizations3
Weight loss4

1. Bassiony et al. Int J Geriatr Psychiatry. 2002;17:549-56;; 2. Menon et al. Int J Geriatr 
Psychiatry. 2001;16:139-46; 3. Dwyer and Byrne.  Int Psychogeriatr. 2000 ;12:463-7; 
4. Morley and Kraenzle. J Am Geriatr Soc. 1994 Jun;42:583-5

### Slide 73
Treatment of Depression in Persons with Major NCD

Non-Pharmacologic: Address dependency fears, self-esteem; Avoid frustration; Schedule pleasant events, including Music Therapy and other interventions.
Antidepressant trials: Inconsistent conclusions 
Positive: moclobemide, clomipramine, citalopram, sertraline
Negative: imipramine, fluoxetine, venlafaxine, sertraline, mirtazapine
ECT effective in retrospective study
Little support for antidepressant effect of stimulants
No evidence supporting the use of cognitive enhancers or  antipsychotics in treating depressive symptoms

1. Nyth et al. Acta Psychiatr Scand. 1992 ;86(2):138-45;  2. Lyketsos et al. Am J Psychiatry. 2000;
157:1686-9; 3. Petracca et al. J Neuropsychiatry Clin Neurosci. 1996;8:270-5 ;4. Roth et al. 
 Br J Psychiatry. 1996;168:149-57;  5. Rao and Lyketsos  Int J Geriatr Psychiatry. 2000 ;15:729-35

### Slide 78
Behavioral ConsiderationsFor Treating Agitation/Aggression

Causes include
Adverse effects of medications
Inadequate or excessive stimulation
Disorientation
Helplessness
Pain,fear
Sleep deprivation
Interventions require:
Assure safety / Adequate supervision
Intervention can't require learning/memory
Identify/modify specific precipitants
Provide predictable routine
Arrange pleasant experiences

Physical restraints are rarely necessary

Alexopoulos et al. Postgrad Med. 2005;Spec No:6-22.
