### Slide 79
Brief Psychosocial Therapy (BPST): A Non-Pharmacologic Approach

Data from CALM-AD trial
Open trial, n=318, duration = 4 weeks
30% improvement on CMAI in 43% of subjects
One of 3 interventions, individualized, administered according to manual: 
Social interaction
Personalized music
Removal of environmental triggers

Ballard et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry 2009;17:726-33.

### Slide 80
Pharmacological Treatment Of Psychosis/Agitation In Dementia

No medication is FDA-indicated for treatment of psychosis or agitation in dementia, and none is fully safe or effective.
Support for medications is based on short-term trials.
Evidence base beyond antipsychotic medications is even more limited.
Evidence-based off-label use of medications may be appropriate, is common in practice, but requires special considerations.

*

### Slide 81
APA Practice Guideline on the Use of Antipsychotics to Treat Agitation or Psychosis in Patients With Dementia (2016)

Stronger emphasis on nonpharmacologic interventions than 2008 treatment guideline
Key recommendations are for pre-treatment thorough assessment, individualized treatment plan, nonpharmacologic approaches first (in non-emergency), measurement based treatment (e.g. using NPI), avoid haloperidol and long-acting injectables, try taper after 4 months even in responders without contraindications to discontinuation.

Reus et al. Am J Psychiatry. 2016 May 1;173(5):543-6.

### Slide 82
CATIE-AD: CONCLUSIONS

All cause discontinuation: drugs = placebo 
EPS a common reason for drug discontinuation
Olanzapine & Risperidone equally effective in treating behavioral problems and superior to Quetiapine and placebo, but only in patients who did not develop EPS
"No large clinical benefit of treatment with atypical antipsychotic medications as compared with placebo"

Schneider LS et al. NEJM 2006;355:1525-38.

### Slide 83
Atypical Antipsychotics:Safety Issues In Elders

Somnolence, orthostatic hypotension, gait  disturbance1
 FDA warning of increased CVAEs and increased mortality in elderly patients with dementia2,4
 ADA warning for risk of diabetes with all atypical antipsychotics3
 Extrapyramidal symptoms; tardive dyskinesia1

1. McDonald WM. J Clin Psychiatry. 2000;61(suppl 13):3-11;   2. Wang et al. N Engl J Med. 2005;353:2335-2341; 3. American Diabetes Association, et al. Diabetes Care. 2004;27:596-601;  4. Schneider et al. JAMA. 2005;294:1934-1943.

*

### Slide 84
Class-Associated Severe AE And Mortality Concerns

FDA Boxed Warning (April 11, 2005) notes: 
		increased risk of death compared with placebo
In 17 PCTs, the death rate was 4.5% among  3611 antipsychotic-treated
	patients, while 2.6% of 1766 placebo-treated  patients died (OR = 1.6)
Causes of death mostly heart related (heart failure, sudden death) or infectious (pneumonia)
Studied medications: aripiprazole (3), olanzapine (5), risperidone (7), quetiapine (2), ziprasidone (1), haloperidol (2)
Warning was extended to clozapine , Symbyax (olanzapine/fluoxetine), and later also to typical antipsychotics, based on additional case-controlled studies.

US Food and Drug Advisory. http://www.fda.gov/cder/drug/advisory/antipsychotics.htm.

*

### Slide 85
Are Typical Antipsychotics A Better Alternative?

Typical antipsychotics are NOT a safer choice.
Efficacy appears similar
Side effects differ
EPS including tardive dyskinesia
Sedation, weight, anticholinergic, hypotension
Mortality rate no lower than with atypicals1,2

1. Wang et al. N Engl J Med. 2005;353:2335-41;    2. Ray et al. NEJM 2009;360:225-35.

*

### Slide 86
Antipsychotics: Clinical Recommendations

Document use of behavioral and environmental interventions 
Document antipsychotic's target symptoms
Educate health care representative about benefits, risks 
Coordinate care with that of other involved clinicians 
Establish time frame for assessment of results 
Frequently assess (and document) benefits and AEs
Use lowest doses necessary for the shortest time period 
Evidence does not support greater safety of typicals.

*

### Slide 87
Other Agitation Medications(All Are Off Label)

Cognitive enhancers +/-
 Antidepressants
 Antiepileptics
 Anxiolytics 
 Miscellaneous

Bianchetti et al. Aging Clin Exp Res. 2006;18:158-162.
Parnetti et al. Mech Ageing Dev. 2001;122:2063-2069.

### Slide 89
89

Divalproex In NCBS

Divalproex:  case series & 4 placebo controlled trials in patients with dementia and agitation show inconsistent benefits.1-5
Negative studies and negative Cochrane review have discouraged use of divalproex in treating dementia-associated agitation. 
Effect in secondary mania has been proposed.

1. Tariot. Psychopharmacol Bull 2003;37(suppl2):116-128; 2. Porteinsson et al. Am J Geriatr  Psychiatry 2001; 9:58 -66; 3. Tariot et al. Curr Ther Res 2001;62:51-67; 4. Sival et al. Int J Geriatr Psychiatry 2002;17:579-585; 5. Tariot et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry 2005; 13:942-949

### Slide 90
90

Divalproex For AgitationIn Persons with Dementia: Guidelines (If Used)

Side effects: somnolence, thrombocytopenia, weight gain, tremor, hepatotoxicity, pancreatitis (rare)
Current dose and titration recommendations
Initial dose 125-250 mg twice daily; increase by 125-250 mg/d every 5 days
Usual range 500-1,250 mg/day
Usual level 40-90 g/ml
Clinical response more important than serum level
Start low and go slow! Recognize that support is limited.

### Slide 91
91

Carbamazepine

Shown effective in 2 RCTs for agitation but use is limited due to drug/drug interactions and side effects1,2
Autoinduction of 3A4 cytochrome enzyme
Lowers haloperidol levels about 60% and may affect other 3A4 substrates 
Can cause G.I. upset, rash
Usual dose in dementia about 300 mg/d
Slow response - Go slow with treatment.

1. Tariot et al. Am J Psychiatry. 1998;155:54-61; 
2 .Olin et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry. 2001;9:400-405.

### Slide 92
92

Lamotrigine For Aggression In Dementia

Case reports suggest possible benefit.
Dose: 25 - 400 mg range
Slow titration required
Antiaggressive effects seen
Main side effects: mild tremor, ataxia, sedation; dermatologic effects.

Devarajan and Dursun. Am J Psychiatry. 2000 Jul;157:1178.

93

### Slide 95
95

SSRIs

Citalopram vs. placebo:1
10 point change on NBRS agitation and lability subscales
50% dropout rate in both groups
Citalopram vs. risperidone:2
12 week study in hospitalized dementia patients with agitation/psychosis.
Equivalent efficacy on NBRS, Less EPS or sedation than risperidone.
Citalopram vs. placebo:3
9 week study in 186 Alzheimer patients, 10 to 30 mg/d, 40% of cit vs 26% of pla subjects improved on Neurobehavioral Rating Scale agitation subscale. Caregiver distress significantly diminished.
Typical dose: 10-40 mg/d  (but be aware of the FDA warning re QTc prolongation in older adults above 20 mg/d)

1. Pollock BG. Am J Psychiatry. 2002;159:460-465; 
2. Pollock BG, et al. Am J Geriatric Psychiatry. 2007; 15:942-952.
3. Porsteinsson et al. JAMA 2014;311:682-91.

### Slide 96
96

Trazodone

Shown to reduce agitation among persons with dementia in 2 controlled trials1,2
Comparable to haloperidol 1.8 mg/d, Behavior Management Techniques, or placebo in agitated AD patients1
Comparable to haloperidol in treating agitation in dementia (no control group)2
Typical dosing: 50 - 250 mg per day
Important side effects: sedation, hypotension, priapism
Cochrane Review: Data insufficient to recommend3

1. Teri et al. Neurology. 2000;55:1271-8. 2. Sultzer et al. J Am Geriatr Soc. 2001;
49:1294-1300; 3. Martinon-Torres et al. Cochrane Database Syst Rev. 2004 Oct 18;(4):CD004990.

 Opiates1,2

### Slide 98
Benzodiazepines

Controversial treatment because of: 
Sedation
Falls/fractures
Disinhibition/worse agitation
Impaired cognition
Dependance/tolerance/withdrawal
Minimal efficacy data
Short term (emergency) use may sometimes be appropriate

### Slide 99
Benzodiazepines

3 studies demonstrate efficacy and safety:
1) Retrospective review of clonazepam (mean 1.2 mg/d), 2 - 21 months in 24 geropsych inpatients, 12 of them demented, improvement on BPRS, CMAI, and Rating Scale for Side Effects1
2) 2 hours after acute treatment of agitation in demented subjects, effects and side effects were similar with IM lorazepam 1 mg or IM olanzapine 2.5 or 5 mg in DB RCT. After 24 hours olanzapine but not lorazepam beat placebo.2 
3) Alprazolam was as effective as haloperidol in reducing disruptive behaviors in nursing home residents with dementia treated for 12 weeks in DB RCT3

1. Calkin et al. Int J Geriatr Psychiatry. 1997;12:745-9; 2. Meehan  et al: Neuropsychopharm 2002:26:494-504; 3. Christensen and Benfield. J Am Geriatr Soc. 1998;46:620-5.

### Slide 100
Buspirone

An azapirone anxiolytic; relative of trazodone
Used primarily as an anxiolytic
5HT1a agonist effect
Limited data (case reports), limited support
Mild side effects: headache, nausea, rarely may increase agitation
Usual dosing: 15-90 mg/d in divided doses
Can be used adjunctively

Tiller et al. Lancet 1988;2(8609):510;Cooper JP. Psychiatry Neurosci. 2003;28:469.

### Slide 101
Opioid Treatment for Agitation in Patients with Advanced Dementia

Unrecognized pain may lead to agitation, aggression
4 week double-blind study of 25 completers from 47 nursing home subjects with dementia/agitation
Long-acting oxycodone 10 mg q 12 h or long-acting morphine 20 mg q d
No significant difference in total sample, but significant reduction of agitation (beyond effect of sedation) in subgroup of 13 completers >= 85 years old.

Manfredi et al: Int J Geriatric Psychiatry 2003;18:700-5.

### Slide 102
Delta-9-Tetrahydrocannabinol for Nighttime Agitation in Severe Dementia

Open-label study of 5 AD and 1 VaD patients
Dronabinol 2.5 mg q d x 14 days
Actigraphy showed reduced nocturnal activity
NPI total score improved
Side effects:
None observed at low dose of this study
At higher doses (in other studies), dronabinol may cause drowsiness, dizziness, hypotension, hallucinations, dysphoria, headaches, palpitations

Walther et al: Psychopharmacology 2006;185: 524-528

### Slide 103
ECT: Antiaggressive Effects

Grant and Mohan. ECT 2001;17:205-9.

ECT may be safe, tolerable in selected NCBS patients
Onset of benefit is rapid
Only case reports and retrospective series are available at present
Controlled trial is in progress

### Slide 104
Cyproterone: Antiaggressive Effects

Huertas et al. J Clin Psychiatry 2007;68:439-44.

Cyproterone 100 mg/d vs haloperidol 2 mg/d in 27 elderly patients with AD and aggressive behavior, mean age 80.7 years
90 day randomized assignment after 15 day washout
Primary outcome measure: Staff Observation Aggression Score
Results:
Tolerability: AE in 71% of haloperidol subjects vs 31% of cyproterone subjects (p = .035). 
Efficacy: Elimination of aggression at endpoint in 69% of cyproterone subjects vs 14.2% of haloperidol subjects (p = .012). 
Further studies needed

### Slide 105
Cyproterone Adverse Effects

Huertas et al. J Clin Psychiatry 2007;68:439-44.

### Slide 106
Prazosin for Agitation/Aggression in AD

Nonsedating postsynaptic -1 NE antagonist
Rationale: Reduce heightened NE activity associated with upregulated LC presynaptic cells and postsynaptic receptors in projection areas (prefrontal, hippocampal)
Shown effective in reducing NPI, BPRS, and CGIC         scores in small  DBPCT1 
N = 11 for prazosin, 11 for placebo
Mean dose 5.7 mg/d (began at 1 mg/d) 
Duration = 8 weeks
Settings = nursing home and university AD center
Adverse effects, including BP changes, similar to placebo

Wang et al. Am J Geriatric Psychiatry 2009;17:744-51.

### Slide 107
What Is "Best Practice" for Treatment of NCBS in AD?

Behavioral analysis and nonpharmacologic treatment when possible
Consider full range of medications
Begin with atypical antipsychotics used with appropriate education/caution/brevity.
Consider other pharmacologic agents and/or ECT with monitoring of response of target symptoms.
Comply with regulatory guidelines for use.

### Slide 109
Current Standard of Care

No clear consensus or treatment algorithm for agitation or psychosis in dementia
Failure to show greater efficacy of antipsychotics for dementia-related agitation may be due in part to clinical trials designs
Use of antipsychotics should be:
Short-term 
With education of caregivers/family
Caution by clinicians
Careful documentation
Further trials, new agents, FDA-approval needed.

### Slide 5
Categories of Neuropsychiatric Symptoms (NPS) of MNCD1

RHitching               5

Adapted from Nowrangi MA, Lyketsos CG, Rosenberg PB. 2015

### Slide 6
6

Agitation

Motor activity that is purposeless, excessive, does not advance any goal, and is often unresponsive to suggestion. 

Manifestation of serious mental illness or cognitive impairment.

Disrupts an aspect of daily functioning or Activity of Daily Living (ADL.)

Not necessarily assaultive or aggressive. 

Disruptive to care-giving and/or medical care.

Wandering may or may not include agitation.

### Slide 7
Major Neurocognitive Disorder (MNCD) with Behavioral Disturbance (DSM-5)*

DSM-5 specifies Major Neurocognitive Disorder (MNCD) as "with" or "without" behavioral disturbance. 
Alzheimer's Disease International: World Alzheimer Report 2014 (World Alzheimer Report 2014.) 98% of people with MNCD will experience non-cognitive, or behavioral, symptoms at some point in their illness. 
Behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) can include: depression, anxiety, apathy, agitation, wandering, aggressive behavior, repetitive complaints, obsessive-compulsive behaviors, delusions, hallucinations, and disordered sleep. 
Neuropsychiatric symptoms (NPS) often accompany MNCD, can disrupt living situations, interfere with delivery of medical treatment, and be harmful to patient and others
Psychiatric inpatient hospitalization or admission to a similar unit may result.
Management is crucial when patient and others may be at risk of injury  
The slides review the phenomenology and diagnosis of MNCD with Behavioral Disturbance, as well as treatment and management options

### Slide 12
CME Self-test Question #5

APA Recommendations for Antipscyhotic (AP)  use include all the following EXCEPT: 
Outside of situations involving an imminent threat to patient or others, APs should be used for agitation or psychosis only when symptoms are severe, dangerous, or cause significant distress to the patient

2.     The AP should be started at the highest tolerable dose to quickly achieve control of behavior.
  
3.     After a 4-week trial of an adequate dose of an AP, if no clinically significant response is evident, the  AP should be tapered and withdrawn,.

4. For patients who do show an adequate response to AP, an attempt to taper should made within    4 months drug initiation unless a recurrence of the target symptoms during the previous taper process.

5. During tapering of an AP, symptoms should be assessed at least monthly, for at least 4 months after medication discontinuation, to seek any sign of recurrence; a risk-benefit analysis should review need for treatment.
							HHFenn/RHitching/09/04/2018

12

Outside of situations involving an imminent threat to patient or others, APs should be used for agitation or psychosis only when symptoms are severe, dangerous, or cause significant distress to the patient
A clinical response to non-pharmacologic interventions should be reviewed before any nonemergency use of an AP.
The AP should be started at a low dose and titrated up to the minimum effective dose tolerated.
After a 4-week trial of an adequate dose of an AP, if no clinically significant response is evident, the AP should be tapered and withdrawn,.
For patients who do show an adequate response to AP, an attempt to taper should made within 4 months drug initiation unless a recurrence of the target symptoms during the previous taper process.
During tapering of an AP, symptoms should be assessed at least monthly, for at least 4 months after medication discontinuation, to seek any sign of recurrence; a risk-benefit analysis should review need for treatment.
In the absence of delirium, if nonemergency AP treatment is needed, haloperidol should not be used first-line

### Slide 13
13    hhfenn/09/05/2018

Prevalence of Neuropsychiatric Symptoms (NPS) in MNCD*

35 million estimated world-wide with Major Neurocognitive Disorder (MNCD) (dementia), prevalence of following Neuropsychiatric Symptoms: 
>80 %       Agitation during Major Neurocognitive Disorder
60%-98%  Neuropsychiatric Symptoms in general
50%          Psychotic symptoms
30-40%     Delusions
15-30%     Hallucinations
30% of the cost of Alzheimer's Disease has been attributed to management of Neuropsychiatric Symptoms.(NPS)

Kindermann et al. Pharmacological treatment of psychosis and agitation in elderly patients with dementia. Drugs 
&Aging. 2002; 19 (4):257-276

Common Pharmacological Categories for Treatment of NPS in MNCD                         *

--SSRI Antidepressants       Citalopram, sertraline, paroxetine (SSRIs)
--First generation antipsychotics ( FGA)-haloperidol, fluphenazine
--Second generation antipsychotics  (SGA)-olanzapine, risperidone,  	quetiapine, aripiprazole
--Anticonvulsants:      Valproate, carbamazepine 
--Cholinesterase inhibitors (ChEI's)  Rivastigmine, galantamine, donepezil
--Psychostimulants: methylphenidate, dextroamphetamine
--NMDA inhibitor:	Memantine
--Benzodiazepines:	Alprazolam, lorazepam, diazepam
--Dextromethrophane+ quinidine
--Other agents            Trazodone and buspirone ine

### Slide 15
Contributors to Neuropsychiatric Symptoms (NPS) of MNCD*

Medication adverse effects (e.g. anticholinergic)
Chronic and acute pain
Somatic symptoms (eg., constipation, urinary retention, urinary tract infection, dehydration, rash and itching)
Functional limitations 
Psychiatric co-morbidity and pre-existing major mental illness
Disrupted  sleep  and parasomnias
Sensory impairment, eg visual impairment, auditory deficit
Communication barriers: language, motor (articulation), thought processes
Fear, isolation, loss of control, dependence, boredom

Helen C. Kales, MD,a,b,c Laura N. Gitlin, PhD,d,e,f,g and Constantine G. D-I-C-E Non-Pharmacologic Assessment Process  *

Describe the  problem behavior
Investigate the antecedents/contributors  and/or contributors
Create a behavioral planEvaluate the results and revise plan accordingly
*Adapted from : Helen C. Kales, MD,  Laura N. Gitlin, PhD,  and Constantine G. ### Slide 17
Strategies to Minimize Agitation and/or Aggression in NPS

Offer  choices
Respect privacy
Focus on person, not task
Maintain a calm gentle manner
Take time 
Explain any intervention
Maximize  pleasant experiences
Reduce noise, confusion, crowding, overstimulation
Foster a predictable routine
Educate support/supervising caregivers
Supervise and monitor caregivers
Use medications ONLY when non-pharmacological interventions fail and/or to prevent dangerous behavior.

--Adapted from Alexopoulos et al. 1998
--James Ellison, MD, 2011

### Slide 23
PAIN: Evidence of itsContribution to Agitation/aggression (1, 2)

Pain significantly and positively associated with:
	-- resistance to care
	-- abnormal thought process
	-- delusions.
Pain (especially worst pain) is important in development of aggression (2).
Design: Longitudinal, Cross-sectional study (1)
2822 cognitively impaired, community-dwelling subjects (1)
Subjects: Mean age of 84 (SD=9.1 years). 538 (19%) presented with pain.
Indicators: moaning, crying, wincing, frowning, posturing, guarding.
Adjusted for confounders


1. Tosato M, Lukas A., van der Roest HG, Danese P, Antocicco M, Finne-Soveri H, Nikolaus T, Landi F, Berabei R, Onder G.  Association of pain with behavioral and psychiatric symptoms among nursing home residents with cognitive impairment: results from the SHELTER study. Pain. 153 (2012) 305-310

2. Kunik ME, Snow AL, Davila JA, Steele AB, Balasubramanyam V, Doody RS, Schulz PE, Kalavar JS, Morgan RO, Causes of aggressive behavior in patients with dementia. J Clin Psychiatry 2010; 71: 1145-1152

Hhfenn/RHitching 07/22/202023

### Slide 24
Treatment of Pain for NPS of MNCD:            A Randomized Clinical Trial

Design: Stepwise analgesic interventions vs. usual care x 8 weeks 
Subjects N=352 moderate-severe dementia + pain + significant behavioral disturbances. 
Treatment step-wise: acetaminophen morphine buprenorphine transdermal patch, or pregabalin. (N=103) 
Primary Outcome: Cohen-Mansfield Agitation Inventory (CMAI)
Secondary outcomes: aggression (NPI), pain, ADLs, MMSE
Agitation/aggression in Intervention group were significantly reduced (average reduction 17%) after 8 weeks, correlated with reduction in pain.(1)
Agitation WORSENED weeks 8 to 12 AFTER withdrawal of pain treatment.
Improvement likely NOT associated with sedation-because 25.6% of intervention group was treated with opioid or sedating analgesic agents 
Neither ADLs /cognition worsened in treatment group vs. the control group.

*Husebo B., Ballard C, Nilsen OB.,  Aarsland D. Efficacy of treating  pain to reduce behavioral disturbances in residents of nursing homes with dementia; cluster randomized clinical trial. BMJ. 2011; 343:d4065

12/22/2020/        RHitching HHFenn

24

### Slide 25
Assessment of Pain in Non-Verbal Patients

Facial expressions
 Grimacing, tighter face, wrinkled nose
 Brow lowering, closed or tightened eyes, upper lip or cheek raising
 Wincing
 Squinting or narrowing of eyes
 Mouth opening

Vocalisations
 Moaning, groaning, grunting, crying
 Specifc sounds or words for pain (eg, "ow", "ouch", and "that hurts")
 Gasping or noisy breathing

Body movements
 Flinching or pulling away
 Thrashing, rocking
 Refusing to move, moving slow
 Bracing, avoidance of certain body positions
 Rubbing, holding, and/or guarding sore area
 Limping
 Clenched fist
 Going into fetal position, knees pulled up/Stiff or rigid/ Shaking or trembling

Adapted from Hadijstavropoulos T, Herr K, Prkachin KM, Craig KD, Gibson SJ, Lukas A, Smith JH. Pain assessment in elderly adults with dementia, www.thelancet.com/neurology Vol13, December 2014]

25

### Slide 26
Indicators of Pain in Non-Communicative Patients*

Changes in interpersonal interactions
 Not wanting to be touched, not allowing people near
 Decreased social interactions and communication
 Difficult to console or reassure

Changes in activity patterns or routines
 Sleep changes
 Sudden cessation of common routines, decreased activity

Mental status changes
 Are there mental status changes that could be due to pain, and cannot be attributable
to another cause (eg, delirium due to medication)?
Modified with permission from the AGS Panel on Persistent Pain in Older Persons.112
A list of validated pain behaviours from well established observational pain scales and from studies using Pain behaviours are most reliably assessed with a complete validated pain instrument.


Adapted from the Lancet Neurol 2014; 13: 1216-27

26

### Slide 27
Treatment of Pain  Reduces Agitation in NCS due to Dementia

Design:
'Cluster randomized controlled trial'   x 8 weeks
Subjects: 352 residents of nursing homes: Inclusion: age>65, C-M Agitation score > 39 x 1 week
Exclusion: severe aggression [ >/=8 on NPI]
Intervention group: step 1, 2, 3, 4.
Outcome measures: Primary and Secondary: Cohen-Mansfield Agitation Inventory (CMAI), NPI nursing home, FAST, ADLs, MMSE
Outcome: 
Significant difference between groups on CMAI agitation inventory after 8 weeks
Average reduction agitation was 17%
Change from baseline  agitation differed between groups at weeks 2, 4, 8
Intervention group: CMAI worsened between week 8 & 12 after withdrawal of pain treatment.
Pain management can reduce NSD after 8 weeks of stepwise pharmacological treatment.

*Husebo, BS, Ballard C, Sandvik R, Nilsen O.B. Aarsland D. Efficacy of treating Pain to reduce behavioural disturbances in residents of nursing homes with dementia: cluster randomised clinical trial. BMJ 2011:343

HHFENN/  RHITCHING

27

### Slide 28
Pain is Correlated with Less Wandering*

More severe pain has been associated withLess frequent wandering behaviors 
Pain associated with more frequent aggressive and agitated behaviors, after controlling for   covariates. 
Results suggest that the relationship between pain and disruptive behaviors depends on the type of behaviors examined. 
Direction of the relationship between these variables depends on whether   the disruptive behaviors are accompanied by locomotion.  

 *Ahn and Horgas BMC Geriatrics 2013, 13:14,, The relationship between pain and disruptive behaviors in nursing home resident with   dementia  [Hyochol Ahn  and Ann Horgas http://www.biomedcentral.com/1471-2318/13/14]

### Slide 29
Sleep Disturbances and Nocturnal Agitation

24% of cognitively impaired older adults with nocturnal agitation behavior had probable Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS)
4-11% of older adult population had probable RLS.

Factors associated with frequency of nocturnal agitation behaviors:
Severe cognitive impairment on MMSE.
Low Apnea/Hypopnea index (AHI)  associated with daytime agitation, not night-time)
Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS)-associated with iron deficiency and depression.

*Rose, Karen M.  PhD1; Cornelia Beck, PhD2; Pao-Feng Tsai, PhD2; Pham H. Liem, MD2; David G. Davila, MD3; Morton Kleban, PhD4; Nalaka S. Gooneratne, MD5; Gurpreet Kalra, MS4; Kathy Culpepper Richards, PhD5   Sleep and Nocturnal Agitation in Dementia. SLEEP, Vol. 34, Nov. 6, 2011, 779-786

HHFENN   09/05/2018        29

### Slide 30
Sleep-Related Conditions                             NOT associated with Nocturnal Agitation*

Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) -brief repetitive episodes of breathing pauses, causing microarousals and hypoxia.

Periodic Leg Movements of Sleep-symptoms are largely unnoticed.

*Rose, Karen M.  PhD1; Cornelia Beck, PhD2; Pao-Feng Tsai, PhD2; Pham H. Liem, MD2; David G. Davila, MD3; Morton Kleban, PhD4; Nalaka S. Gooneratne, MD5; Gurpreet Kalra, MS4; Kathy Culpepper Richards, PhD5   Sleep and Nocturnal Agitation in Dementia. SLEEP, Vol. 34, Nov. 6, 2011, 779-786

### Slide 31
Medications with Evidence of Benefit for NPS of MNCD*

Citalopram: starting dose  5.0 mg to 10.0 mg, maximum dose 20.0 mg daily 
Risperidone: starting dose, 0.25 to 1.0 mg daily;  maximum dose 2.0 mg daily
Olanzapine:  starting dose 1.25 mg to 5 mg daily; maximum dose 10 mg daily 
Aripiprazole: starting dose, 5 mg daily; maximum dose 15 mg/day 
Haloperidol:  starting dose, 0.25 to 0.5 mg daily; maximum dose, 2 mg daily  
Donepezil: starting dose 5.0 daily, maximum dose 10.0 mg daily
Memantine 10mg daily added to donepezil 5mg TID

*Micromedex, 2014

Rhitching/Hhfenn 06/12/2020     31

### Slide 32
RHitching  32

Medications for NPS of MNCD

### Slide 33
Antidepressants (1, 2)*

At least 11 Randomized Clinical  Trials, (8 using SSRIs, and 3 with trazodone) showed benefit in MNCD with Behavioral Disturbance (1)
19 RCT studies between January 1, 1990 to December 31, 2010 were reviewed, [out of 714 articles identified]--15 studies of SSRIs / 4 studies of trazodone (1).
Sink, 2005 found no efficacy for antidepressants in NCS (2)
Sertraline, citalopram most commonly studied SSRIs. (2)
Tolerance: SSRIs were well-tolerated in 14 of 19 trials (2)

1)  Henry, G, Williamson D, Tampi RR, Efficacy and Tolerability of antidepressants in Treatment of Behavioral and psychological Symptoms  of  dementia, A literature Review of Evidence Am J Alzheimers Disease and other Dementias 2011; 26 (3) :169-183 [Data summarized  from Jan 1, 1990 to Dec 31, 2010. regarding all classes of antidepressant medications.] 

2)  Sink KM, Holden KF, Yaffe K. Pharmacological Treatment of Neuropsychiatric Symptoms of Dementia. A Review of the Evidence. JAMA    Feb 2, 2005 Vol293:596-608

HHFENN/RHitching /07/23/2020      33/

### Slide 34
JAMA Recommendations Antipsychotic Use for NPS of MNCD*  *

Outside of situations involving an imminent threat to patient or others, APs should be used for agitation or psychosis only when symptoms are severe, dangerous, or cause significant distress to the patient
A clinical response to non-pharmacologic interventions should be reviewed before any nonemergency use of an AP.
The AP should be started at a low dose and titrated up to the minimum effective dose tolerated.
After a 4-week trial of an adequate dose of an AP, if no clinically significant response is evident, the AP should be tapered and withdrawn,.
For patients who do show an adequate response to AP, an attempt to taper should made within 4 months drug initiation unless a recurrence of the target symptoms during the previous taper process.
During tapering of an AP, symptoms should be assessed at least monthly, for at least 4 months after medication discontinuation, to seek any sign of recurrence; a risk-benefit analysis should review need for treatment.
In the absence of delirium, if nonemergency AP treatment is needed, haloperidol should not be used first-line

Adapted from Yohanna, D., & Cifu, A. S. (2017). Antipsychotics to Treat Agitation or Psychosis in Patients with Dementia. Clinical Guidelines Synopsis JAMA, 318 (11), 1057-1058. doi:10.1001/jama.2017.11112
.

34

### Slide 35
Antidepressants (1,2,3)

Deficiencies in the serotonin (5-HT) system  implicated in behavioral symptoms of NCD patients (1,2,3)
Citalopram comparable to risperidone for agitation, with superior tolerability (1)
 No definitive studies of bupropion, mirtazepine, MAOIs, SNRIs, TCAs, buspirone 
"Support for using trazodone, buspirone, selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRI) is limited and is not recommended for routine treatment of agitation or psychosis in patients with dementia" (2)

1) Henry, G, Williamson D, Tampi RR, Efficacy and Tolerabilityof antidepressants in Treatment of Behavioral and psychological Symptoms  of dementia, A literature Review of Evidence Am J Alzheimers Disease and other Dementias 2011; 26 (3) :169-183 [Data summarized from Jan 1, 1990 to Dec 31, 2010. regarding all classes of antidepressant medications.] 
2) Micromedex 2.0, 2014
3). Borson S & Raskind MA : Clinical features and pharmacologic treatment of behavioral symptoms of Alzheimer's disease. Neurology 1997; 48 (5 Suppl 6):S17-S24.

Hhfenn/09/05/2018         35

### Slide 36
Adverse effects: Falls inSSRI and SNRI antidepressants*

Several studies have reported that SSRIs increase fall risk in the aged (Kvelde, et al., 2015) with odds ratios of 1.6-1.9 depending on the study(Lenze et al., 2017).
Some have suggested that an association  of increased fall risk with SSRIs  does not constitute a cause and effect (Gebara et al, 2015)  
 Orthostasis, sedation, or decreased postural control (Kvelde et al., 2015) may contribute to falls, serious concern because  10% to 15% of falls cause major injuries in this population (Lord, 2017), especially during an inpatient stay. 
A retrospective study compared outcomes of post-fall proximal femoral fractures; fractures acquired during inpatient settings had worse outcomes compared to outpatient (Murray et al., 2007). 
Other risk factors for falls include: gait instability, agitated confusion, urinary incontinence and frequency, and a history of falls (Murray et al., 2007).

36

### Slide 37
Adverse Effects: Discontinuation syndrome in SSRIs and SNRIs

Symptoms of insomnia, nausea, imbalance, sensory disturbances, and hyperarousal can emerge following discontinuation of SSRI medications.
Alteration of mood, sleep, and appetite, may be mistaken for signs of a relapse into depression, especially if timing of relationship of discontinuation and emergence of symptoms
Discontinuation symptoms are often mild and self-limited, typically last about a week, which can often facilitate their differentiation.

37

### Slide 38
Citalopram vs. Placebo to TreatNCS/Agitation of Dementia

Subjects:  N=186, Dx Probable AD and significant agitation but without major depression.
Design: Citalopram, (10mg-30mg) vs. placebo X 9 weeks.
Outcome: 
18 point Neuro Behavioral Rating Scale/agitation subscale (NBRS-A), CMAI
Citalopram group 40% much-improved/improved over baseline agitation vs. 26% of  placebo
No difference from placebo on NPI agitation subscale, ADLs, or less use of rescue lorazepam.
Recommended dose NOT TO EXCEED 20mg Citalopram per day -- QTc interval prolongation and arrhythmias were present with >30mg per day.)

*Porsteinsson AP, Drye, LT, Pollock GG, Devanand DP, Franqakis C, et al. Effect of citalopram on agitation in Alzheimer disease: the CitAD Randomized Clinical Trial. JAMA 2014 Feb 19;311(7):682-91. doi: 10.1001/jama.2014.93.

Hhfenn/09/05/2018 38

### Slide 39
First Generation Antipsychotics (FGA) (1, 2, 3)

First Generation Antipsychotics (FGAs): 11 RCTs: FGAs  59% vs  41% Placebo improvement x 4-12 weeks; 30% reduction in baseline agitation is considered improvement (2, 3)
Haloperidol: 4 RCTs: superior improvement for aggression (but not agitation) and psychosis vs. placebo 
Few RCTs available of other FGAs for treatment of behavior in dementia and little evidence for improvement in agitation. 
Adverse effects: prolongation of QTc, cardiac arrhythmia, increased mortality, Parkinsonism, dystonia, TD, cognitive decline             
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
 1)   Corbett A,  Smith J, Creese B, Ballard C, Treatment of Behavioral and Psychological symptoms of Alzheimer's Disease,   Current Treatment Options in Neurology  February, 2012,14:113-125
         
  2)  Ballard C, Corbett A. Agitation and aggression in people with Alzheimer's disease. Current Opinion in Psychiatry 2013;  26:252-259

HHFENN/       09/05/2018             39

### Slide 40
Second Generation Antipsychotics (SGA) (1,2,3)

18 RCTs of Second Generation Antipsychotics vs. placebo-over 6-12 weeks. (1, 2)
Risperidone: 5 RCTs showed modest improvement in aggression at 2mg/day vs. Placebo ----Adverse Effects: 3-fold increase in (CVAEs) Risperidone vs placebo
Olanzapine:  Mixed evidence of benefit.
Aripiprazole: 2 RCTs vs. placebo  showed improvement for psychosis 
Quetiapine:  no evidence of benefit for agitation in 2 RCTs- but well tolerated.
Recommendation from Beers Criteria of American Geriatrics Society: 
	"Avoid First and Second Generation antipsychotics for behavioral problems of 	dementia unless non-pharmacological options have failed and patient is threat to 	self or others."  (3)

Corbett A,  Smith J, Creese B, Ballard C, Treatment of Behavioral and Psychological symptoms of Alzheimer's Disease, Current Treatment Options in Neurology  February, 2012,14:113-125
Ballard C, Corbett A. Agitation and aggression in people with Alzheimer's disease. Current Opinion in Psychiatry 2013;  26:252-259
3)  American Geriatrics Society Updated Beers Criteria for Potentially Inappropriate Medication Use in Older Adults: the American  Geriatrics Society 2012   Beers Criteria Update Expert Panel. J. Am Geriatr Society. 2012  April; 60 (4): 616-631

HHFenn/ RHitching 07/24/ 2020    40

### Slide 41
Stimulants: Methylphenidate for Apathy*

"Methylphenidate has shown benefit as an adjunctive therapy to citalopram in older adults with major depression [138], and smaller studies also suggest that it may improve apathy as well as other outcomes in patients with dementia

 In the largest trial of methylphenidate in 77 men with mild Alzheimer disease (AD), methylphenidate improved apathy scores compared with placebo over a 12-week treatment period (9.9-point greater improvement on an 18-point scale) [140]. Measures of cognition, functional status, caregiver burden, and depression also improved more in the methylphenidate group. ...Mean final dose of methylphenidate was 10 mg twice daily. Treatment was well tolerated...

Because methylphenidate has the potential to precipitate agitation or worsen nighttime sleep, ...start with low doses (eg, starting with 5 mg once daily in the morning, maximum dose 10 mg twice daily, last dose no later than lunchtime) with careful monitoring during titration..."

Up-To-Date, June 2020

41

### Slide 42
42

Cardiovascular Adverse Events (CVAEs) and Death in Elderly Patients on Antipsychotic Medications (1)

22 studies evaluated risk of  Cerebrovascular Adverse Events (CVAEs) with Antipsychotic agents: 2 were placebo-controlled.
No one drug found safer in terms of CVAEs (1)
Risk of CVAE's was higher in Antipsychotic-treated group by 1.3-2.0 times
Risk of CVAE's is similar in both atypical and typical antipsychotics.
Risk factors for CVAE's: --higher than median dose--Dx of VaD, AFIB.
Higher risk of death associated with--Older age, male-- Severe dementia, functional impairment  	


1) Mittal V, Kurup L, Williamson D, Muralee S, Tamppi R. Cardiovascular Adverse Events and Death in Elderly with Dementia When Treated with Antipsychotic Medications:  a Literature Review of  Evidence.  Am J Alzheimer's Disease 2011; 26: 10-28:10

Hhfenn/09/05/2018

### Slide 43
Hhfenn/09/05/2018     43

FDA Warning (2005) : All atypical antipsychotics based on published and unpublished proprietary clinical trials data

Elderly patients with dementia-related psychosis treated with atypical antipsychotic drugs are at an increased risk of death compared to placebo. Analyses of seventeen placebo-controlled trials (modal duration of 10 weeks) in these patients revealed a risk of death in the drug-treated patients of between 1.6 to 1.7 times that seen in placebo treated patients. Over the course of a typical 10-week controlled trial, the rate of death in drug-treated patients was about 4.5%, compared to a rate of about 2.6% in the placebo group. Although the causes of death were varied, most of the deaths appeared to be either cardiovascular (eg heart failure, sudden death) or infectious (eg pneumonia) in nature. 
These drugs are not approved for the treatment of patients with dementia related psychosis. 
2008: FDA Warning  extended to include Typical Antipsychotics.

### Slide 44
44

SGAs for NPS Randomized Controlled Trial of SGAs:Clinical Antipsychotic Trials of Intervention Effectiveness (CATIE-AD) (1)*

Design:
Subjects: N =  421 outpatients: dementia + agitation/aggression
42 sites, double-blind, placebo-controlled x 36 weeks
Olanzapine 5.0 mg vs. quetiapine 56.5mg vs. risperidone  1.0 mg  vs  placebo
Outcomes: Time to discontinuation for lack of efficacy or due to adverse effects

Results:  
No differences among SGA medication groups in improvement on Clinical Global Improvement (CGIC) scale. 
Beneficial effects NOT SEEN for  function, quality of life, or care-giving time.
Adverse effects offset efficacy in treatment of psychosis, aggression, or agitation in Alzheimer's Disease	

	1) Sultzer DL, Davis SM, Tariot PN, et al. Clinical Symptoms Responses to Atypical Antipsychotic Medications in Alzheimer's Disease: Phase 1 Outcomes from CATIE-AD Effectiveness Trial Am J Psychiatry 2008: 165:844-854

### Slide 47
FGA vs. SGAs did NOT differ in

Neither Conventional (FGA) nor Atypical APs (SGA) were associated with:
--time to nursing home admission 
--time to death

After adjustment for relevant covariates, only psychiatric symptoms-(including psychosis and agitation)-not medications--were correlated with increased risk of institutionalization and death.


1) Lopez OL, Becker JT, Chang YF., et al. The long-term effects of conventional and atypical antipsychotics in patients with probable Alzheimer's disease. American Journal of Psychiatry 2013 Sep 1;170(9):1051-8. doi: 10.1176/appi.ajp.2013.12081046.

Hhfenn/09/05/2018      48

Anticonvulsants (AEDs) (1)No consistent evidence of benefit

Advantages             Disadvantages  
Valproate           	tolerable 			No efficacy 
Carbamazepine  	slight evidence of benefit     	Enzyme induction
Oxcarbazepine	tolerable			No efficacy
Gabapentin      	pain attenuation     		No efficacy

### Slide 49
Cholinesterase Inhibitors- I (1,2)

14 RCTs: 9 studies of Donepezil, 3 of Galantamine, 2 of Rivastigmine 
Subjects: Low Neuropsychiatric Inventory (NPI) scores at baseline. 
Few studies were powered to detect differences in individual behavioral symptoms; majority not designed specifically to detect changes in NP outcomes
Mean treatment length: 24 weeks
Outcome: Neuro Psychiatric Inventory (NPI) or specific scales of agitation or apathy. 
3 studies found statistically significant, moderate differences in change of NPI total in drug vs. placebo.
At best, only minimal evidence of benefit for agitation/aggression (1)

1)   Rodda J, Morgan S, Walker Z. Are cholinesterase inhibitors effective in the management of the behavioral and psychological  symptoms of dementia in  Alzheimer's disease? A systematic review of randomized, placebo-controlled trials of donepezil, rivastigmine, and galantamine. International Psychogeriatrics 2009; Oct.(5) 21:813-824
2) Corbett A. Smith J, Creese B, Ballard C. Treatment of Behavioral and Psychological Symptoms of Alzheimer's Disease. Agitation and aggression in people with Alzheimer's disease. Current Opinion in Neurology; (2012)     14:113-125.

Hhfenn/09/05/2018      49

### Slide 50
Cholinesterase Inhibitors-II (1)

5 of 8 studies of Rivastigmine, Donepezil, Galantamine found small, but statistically benefit, based on the non-cognitive, NPI scores.(1)
Most subjects enrolled in studies had low severity of behavioral symptoms, which indicates that the results do  NOT confirm a specific benefit for agitation or aggression. (1)
Conclusion: Cholinesterase inhibitors are NOT proven effective for treatment for agitation and/or aggression in Alzheimer's Disease

1)  Sink, KM, Holden KF, Yaffee K. Pharmacological treatment of neuropsychiatric symptoms of dementia: a review of the evidence. JAMA. 2005; 293;596.e

09/04/2018            /HHFENN                  50

### Slide 51
Donepezil (1, 2)

Design: 
Donepezil vs. Placebo x 12 weeks 
Subjects: N=272 with AD (median MMSE=8) + clinically significant agitation.
	Donepezil= 113    Placebo=108 
221 subjects had agitation so severe it did not respond to 4 week psychosocial treatment. 
Exclusion criteria: delirium, dementia with Lewy bodies, poor compliance
Results:   
Outcome on CMAI and NPI did not differ  significantly between groups  
Conclusions:
Donepezil found NOT more effective than placebo for clinically significant agitation in AD patients for 12-week trial.(1)
A randomized withdrawal study found discontinuation of donepezil was associated with significant worsening of NPI score within 6 weeks. (2)



      1) Howard RJ, Juszczak E, Ballard CG, et al. Donepezil for the Treatment of Agitation in Alzheimer's Disease. NEJM 2007; 357:1382-1392
       2) Holmes C, Wilinson D, Dean C, et al. The efficacy of deonepezil in the treatment of neuropsychiatric symptoms in Alzheimer disease. Neurology 2004:63-214-219

Memantine (1)

"The potential efficacy of memantine to ameliorate the behavioral effects in AD requires further study. 

Post hoc analyses of clinical trial results suggest that patients assigned to memantine treatment may have diminished agitation/aggression, irritability, and other behavioral disturbances [47,48]. 

However, systematic reviews have concluded that studies have not demonstrated a clinically significant effect of memantine for neuropsychiatric symptoms of dementia [45,49,50].."

Memantine + Donepezil stable doses (1)

### Slide 57
ECT*

A systematic review of 17 studies of ECT for NPS (which did not include RCTs) reported that clinically significant improvements could be attained in treatment-refractory agitation and aggression of MNCD (Glass et al., 2017). 
A pilot study of 9 patients with NPS of MNCD concluded that six patients who received ECT showed improvement without risk to their safety; however, no significant improvement over the non-treatment group could be found. (Burton et al., 2017).

HHFenn/Rhitching 57

### Slide 58
Pimavanserin for NPS of        Parkinson's Disease Dementia*

Pimavanserin was found effective, compared to placebo, for hallucinations and delusions, and without significant motor adverse effects or orthostasis
5-HT2A inverse agonist (Fox, 2014) with minimal dopamine (D2) receptor antagonism approved by FDA (US) for treatment of delusions and hallucinations in PD.
Receptor profile may lower the risk of extrapyramidal side effects seen in other antipsychotic agents. 
Without dopaminergic, adrenergic, histaminergic, or muscarinic effects, it is apparently safe (Bozymski et al.,2017; Cummings JL et al. 2014).

58

### Slide 59
Pharmacotherapy Summary

Second Generation Antipsychotics: Increased Mortality- FDA Black Box warning, 2005
First Generation Antipsychotics:  Increased Mortality FDA- Black Box warning, 2008
Risperidone:  Greater improvement than placebo in selected psychotic symptoms, disordered behavior, aggression; MDD 1mg x short-term (6-12 weeks)
Olanzapine:   Evidence of benefit for agitation/aggression
Aripiprazole: Inconclusive evidence of benefit. 
Quetiapine:    Minimal evidence of benefit but tolerated well
Haloperidol:   Evidence of benefit for uncontrolled aggression 
Citalopram:   Evidence of benefit
Cholinesterase inhibitors: Inconclusive evidence 
Donepezil + Memantine: Some evidence of benefit 
Trazodone, Buspirone: Inconclusive evidence 
Anticonvulsants: Inconclusive evidence  
Benzodiazepines: Avoid unless very short-term use

HHFENN/RHITCHING  07/24/2020 

59

### Slide 60
General Recommendations

Non-pharmacological interventions should be the first line.
Institute medications with low doses, assessing for tolerability, adverse effects, toxicity, drug-drug interactions, duplication of properties, and anticholinergic burden (AB)
Avoid unwarranted drug switching, polypharmacy, or restarting meds that have not been beneficial in past.
Monitor over time regularly and closely for adverse effects.
Discontinue medications which are not effective.

1) Editorial,  Benoit H. Mulsant, MD, MS: "Challenges of the Treatment of Neuropsychiatric Symptoms Associated with Dementia" AJGP: 22:4, April 2014, 317-320-Benoit H. Mulsant, MD, MS

Hhfenn/RHitching60

### Slide 61
Recommendations

R/O Persistent delirium/hypoactive delirium:Treat etiology
Wandering behavior: intervene with D-I-C-E process 
Pain:   Analgesic medication   
Psychosis*: begin with low doses of SGAs
Medication adverse effects, drug-drug interactions, toxicity 
Sleep disruption:  Consider parasomnias, environment

*Psychosis is associated with nursing home admission and time to death.

hhfenn/rhitching/          61

### Slide 62
Take-Away

62

Assess baseline severity of neuropsychiatric symptoms with Neuropsychiatric Inventory.
Determine details of behavioral disturbance, neuropsychiatric symptoms, antecedents, presumed precipitants, and their context.
Determine prior medication treatments, trials, outcomes, doses, duration, and adverse effects 
Review current and past medication regimens for drug-drug interactions, anti-cholinergic burden (AB), adverse effects, and toxicity.  
Discover factors which exacerbate, contribute to, or mimic NPS, such as delirium, pain, sleep disorders, pre-existing mental disorders, drug, alcohol, or substance withdrawal, adverse medication effects, and  co-morbid medical conditions.
Develop non-pharmacologic interventions based upon aforementioned information, and assist staff/caregivers/family in implementation. 
Administer medications with minimal doses, one at a time, considering age-related pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics, frailty, and risk of adverse effects. 
Consider neuromodulation interventions such as ECT or rTMS 
Adhere to antipsychotic (AP) APA prescribing recommendations for use of antipsychotics (ch02)
Adhere to Quality Use of Medications in Dementia (QUM-D) tool

### Slide 63
Answers to CME Questions

CORRECT ANSWERS IN RED

Question 1. Wandering
Question 2. The patient's room 
Question 3. SSRI antidepressants
Question 4. Echolalia
Question 5. The AP should be started at the highest tolerable dose to quickly achieve control of behavior.

Recent myocardial infarction
Bundle branch block
Urinary retention
Untreated glaucoma
Patients hospitalized for severe melancholic depression

### Slide 7
Question 3
All of the following are reasonable strategies for addressing unsatisfactory response to an antidepressant, except:
Augmenting a partial response that is a placebo effect, by adding another medication
Trying a sequence of up to three monotherapy trials with different antidepressants
Treating insomnia/nightmares with appropriate hypnotics
Switching to bupropion or mirtazapine if the patient is having sexual side effects

All of the following augmentation strategies after unsatisfactory response to an SSRI are effective but one has a significantly larger effect size than the others:
Lithium

Aripiprazole
Bupropion

### Slide 9
Question 5

All of the following are findings from the STAR*D study except

Depressed patients with high levels of anxiety respond much less well than patients with fewer anxiety symptoms
There was a nonsignificant trend toward better results with lithium augmentation than with thyroid (T3) augmentation 
There was a nonsignificant trend toward better results with venlafaxine combined with mirtazapine, compared with the MAOI tranylcypromine
The remission rate with citalopram was 28%

### Slide 10
Major Teaching Points of this Lecture

Use antidepressants when indicated: for major depressive syndromes, not necessarily for depressed mood in multiple other contexts.
Know what the drugs cost.  This will sometimes help you practice cost-effectively.
Do one treatment at a time and use efficient dosing strategies
Manage side effects by using as little polytherapy as possible
Be mindful of placebo and non-specific effects of treatment.

### Slide 11
How Are We Doing in Treating Depression?*

Lifetime prevalence:   16.2%
12 month prevalence:   6.6%
59% had severe or very severe role impairment
51.6% of depressed patients received some treatment
Of these, 41.9% were rated as adequately treated.                 
           *Kessler et al. JAMA 2003 June 18:3095-3105

### Slide 14
An Algorithm for Selecting Medications for Depression: STAR*D Remission Results*

Level 1: Citalopram: 28% (N=2,876) ("response" = 47%)
Level 2: Switch to sertraline 18%  (N=238), bupropion SA 21% (N=239), venlafaxine XR 25% (N=250)
Augment with buspirone 30% (N=286), bupropion SA 30% (N=279)
Augmenting or switching: retrospective matched comparison showed no difference (OR 1.14 for remission with augmenting)
Level 3: Switch to mirtazapine 12% (N=114), or nortriptyline 20% (N=121)
Augment with (N=142) lithium 15%, or thyroid (T3) 25%
Level 4: Switch to (N=109) tranylcypromine 7%                                    
                           or mirtazapine + venlafaxine 14%               
*AJP 1/06, 7/06, 9/06; NEJM 3/06; J Clin Psychopharm 2/12

### Slide 15
Depressed Patients with High Levels of Anxiety Responded Poorly in STAR*DFava et al. AJP 2008;165:342-51

About 50% of STAR*D patients had significant levels of anxiety
Defined as a score of 7 or more on the Ham D: anxiety/somatization items
In Level 1 remission rate with low anxiety = 33%
                               with significant anxiety = 22%
In Level 2 switch or augmentation studies, remission rates with 
Study                                 low anxiety  (%)  significant anxiety (%)
Switch             Sertraline           29                           8
    "                   Bupropion          34                         10
    "                   Venlafaxine        36                         12
Add                  Bupropion          37                         18
    "                   Buspirone          39                           9

### Slide 16
12 month Naturalistic Followup of STAR*D:Relapse Rates of Remitters

Step 1 - 40%
  Step 2 -  55%
  Step 3 - 65% 
  Step 4 - 71%

(Rush J et al AJP 2006)

### Slide 17
Generalizability of STAR*D results

Good, because the patients were more like patients in typical practice than the patients in Phase 3 drug-company-sponsored efficacy trials that comprise most of the studies in meta-analyses*
Lack of placebo controls limits ability to interpret the outcome data, however.
Non-use of atypical antipsychotics means we do not know how they would compare
*Wisniewski SR et al.  AJP May 2009

### Slide 18
And from other evidence: If the first antidepressant fails, does it matter if you switch in class, out of class, or just continue the first antidepressant?

No.
Souery D et al.  J Clin Psychopharmacol 2011;31(4):512-16
Bschor T.  Acta Psychiatr Scand 2010;121:174-9

### Slide 19
The "Humbling Reality" about antidepressants (Trivedi, 2015)

There is no FDA-approved antidepressant that does not work via the catecholamine and indoleamine mechanisms.  We do not have anything novel
So it should be no surprise that in STAR*D, with each sequential trial, the antidepressants worked less well, and if patients remitted it didn't last.
The treatment people get depends on the prescriber rather than the illness

### Slide 20
Is it better to start with combinations?  The Co-Med Study (Rush et al 2011)

685 patients with moderately severe depression and little previous treatment, randomized to 12 weeks of (with 7 month extension)
Escitalopram  (to 20 mg) plus placebo
Escitalopram plus Bupropion SA (to 400 mg) 
Venlafaxine SA (to 300 mg) plus mirtazapine (to 45 mg)

### Slide 21
CO-MED results - remission rates

Escitalopram                                  39%
Escitalopram plus Bupropion SA   39%
Venlafaxine plus mirtazapine         38%

Improvement rates were 52% for each
7 month remission rates ranged from 42 to 47%
Adverse events were higher with ven-mirtaz
No advantage for the combos in patients with high or low anxiety (Chan HN et al 2012)

### Slide 22
Cost-Conscious Treatment

Physicians have a responsibility to know what the medications cost
After appropriate clinical evaluation and determination of the most evidence-supported and safest treatment, costs should be taken into consideration.

Since many antidepressants are activating and there may be a need for a hypnotic, here is a table of options, along with their costs

lorazepam 2 mg                                        0.57
quetiapine 50 mg                                      3

trazodone 50 mg                                       0.78
hydroxyzine 25-50 mg                              1

prazosin 5 mg                                            6

### Slide 27
Drugs Used as Hypnotics in the VA System (Monthly Procurement Cost, Continued - Sept. 2018)

zaleplon (Sonata) 10 mg                   $      3
ramelteon (Rozerem) 2 mg                   219

### Slide 28
Dosing Strategies: General

Avoid frequent dose increases but make contact with patient every 1-2 weeks, as recommended in the 2010 APA Practice Guidelines for Treatment of Depression
Wait 1-4 weeks* with total non-response (or partial response that has plateaued) before increasing. Wait 8-12 weeks if gradual response that has not plateaued
With inpatients and pressure by managed care, clinicians often make changes earlier than 1-2 wks.
*Taylor et al.  Arch Gen Psychiatry 2006;63:1217-23

### Slide 29
Dosing Sertraline

Start with 50 mg in AM (25 mg for elderly, and those with panic disorder)
Maintain 50 mg/day for 1-4 weeks before increasing, depending on the setting.  If no response or partial response that plateaus, increase in 50 mg increments every 2-4 weeks.  Change if no response at 200 mg for 2-4 weeks
However: one study showed better outcome with staying with 100 mg for weeks 6-11 vs. going to 200 mg, after response was unsatisfactory for 6 weeks. (Licht and Ovitzau 2002)

### Slide 30
Dosing Escitalopram (the s-enantiomer of racemic citalopram)

Begin 10 mg in AM or PM, including most elderly and hepatic impaired patients.
If tolerated and no/partial plateaued response in 1-4 weeks, you can increase to 20 mg for 2-4 weeks.  However, no difference was found between 10 and 20 mg in fixed dose comparisons. (Pkg insert)
Note that 10 mg is equivalent to 40 mg of citalopram in clinical potency,* and may produce fewer side effects                                  *Stahl SM.  Essential Psychopharmacology 2005, p. 159.

### Slide 31
Dosing Bupropion SR*

Contraindicated in patients with history of seizures (any cause), anorexia nervosa and bulimia. 
Begin with 100-150 mg qAM
Increase to 100-150 mg bid after 4-7 days; 
Maintain 150 bid for 2-4 weeks before increasing.  If no/partial plateaued response, increase to 200 bid (PDR max. dose for SR). 
Change if no response to 400/d for 2-4 weeks
If using bupropion XR, PDR max. is 450 mg.  Caution: generics have had low bioavailability
*Avoid using bupropion HCl. Seizure rate is 4x higher

### Slide 32
Dosing Citalopram

Begin 20 mg in AM or PM, 10 mg for elderly, unprecipitated panic attacks.
Increase to 40 mg after 1 week.  Continue 40 mg for 1-4 weeks if tolerated.  If no/partial plateaued response, consider switch.
20 mg daily may = placebo*
Study suggests best results if serum level > 50 ng/ml**
Warning: Rector (2016) found, in large VA study, sudden reductions in citalopram dose from 60 mg produced added suicides and admissions
*Feighner and Overo. J Clin Psychiatry 1999;60:828 (fig. 4)
**Haji et al.  J Clin Psychopharmacol 2011;31(3):281-286

### Slide 33
Citalopram Cardiac Safety Information September 1, 2011

FDA recommendations re: risk of Torsades
Do not use in patients with congenital long QT
Correct low K and Mg before using and monitor lytes
Frequent EKGs in patients with CHF, bradyarrhythmias, or on other meds that prolong QTc e.g., quetiapine (new warning in 2011 also)
Hepatic impairment, age >60, 2C19 poor metabolizers, on cimetidine: use 20 mg
Evaluate if any subjective HR or rhythm change
Good News: large cohort study found no adverse outcomes with doses > 40 mg (Zivin, AJP, June 2013)

### Slide 34
Relative QTc Safety of Citalopram vs. Escitalopram (www.fda.gov/Drugs/DrugSafety/ucm297391)

Citalopram      Mean QTc change (ms)
20 mg                           8.5 
40 mg                         12.6
60 mg                         18.5
*moxifloxacin 400 mg 13.4 
Escitalopram 
10 mg                           4.5
20 mg                           6.6
30 mg                         10.7
*moxifloxacin 400 mg   9.0
*used as control in these studies: known to increase QTc

### Slide 35
Escitalopram:  Superior?      Recall The CO-MED Study*                 Conclusions

Escitalopram monotherapy was as good as these two highly regarded combination strategies. (Rush et al 2011)

### Slide 36
Dosing Fluoxetine

Begin 10-20 mg/morning, 5-10 mg for age > 60 or if hx of unprecipitated panic attacks, or to avoid side effects.
Increase to 20 mg after 1 week.  Continue with 20 for 2-4 weeks.  If no/partial plateaued response, increase in 20 mg increments every 2-4 weeks as tolerated (Fava M et al.  J Clin Psychopharmacol 2002; 22:379-387)
Change if no improvement after 2-4 weeks at 60 mg/d

### Slide 37
Dosing Mirtazapine

Avoid if weight gain risk a major concern
Begin with 15 mg qPM 
Increase to 30 mg in one week if tolerated (STAR*D dosing protocol and see Ueno et al 2015).  Continue for 1-4 weeks before increase.  If no/partial plateaued response could increase to 45 mg (PDR maximum) though Ueno et al found no benefit.  
Change if no response to 45 mg after 2-4 wk
Somnolence may be less at higher doses (Fawcett and Barkin, J Affect Disord 1998;51:267-285)

### Slide 38
Dosing Venlafaxine SR

Dosing protocol (STAR*D): Start with 37.5 mg in AM for one week
Increase to 75 mg/day in 2nd week, then 150 mg in 3d week,then hold 3 for weeks 
If no/partial plateaued response, increase in 75 mg increments every 2-4 weeks, if tolerated.  
Change if no response after 2-4 weeks at 300 mg/day (but 225 is the PDR max for XR)
Hypertension risk - 1-2% low doses, up to 10% at doses 300 mg daily and higher.  Check pre-treatment blood pressure.
Observational study in ~ 300,000 patients found rate of strokes was higher with SNRIs - HR 1.2 (Leong et al 2017)

### Slide 39
Duloxetine Dosing and Side Effects

Begin with 40 mg daily in single or divided dose (may help with nausea)
After 3-7 days, increase to 60 mg
If no response/partial plateaued response after 2-4 weeks at 60 mg, you could consider going to 120 mg daily but an RCT found this no more effective, but more toxic, than adding placebo. (Kornstein, 2008)
GI side effects may be reduced by taking with food (unpublished data cited in Schatzberg text, 2007)
Olanzapine 1.25-2.5 mg can also help (Zhong 2014)  
Like venlafaxine, it raises blood pressure but probably not as much. 
1% get liver toxicity: monitoring recommended

### Slide 40
"Duloxetine does not relieve painful physical symptoms in depression: a meta-analysis."*

*Spielmans GI. Psychother Psychosom 2008;77:12-16
Analyzed 5 studies with 1,448 patients involving 714 on duloxetine, 562 on placebo & 172 on paroxetine
Effect size on the analgesic effect was tiny:  0.115 by Cohen's  d.
Conclusion:  the claims being made in advertising are not supported by solid evidence despite FDA-approval for musculoskeletal pain, fibromyalgia, and diabetic neuropathic pain
These results CONFIRMED by meta-analysis by Gebhardt 2016 - all antidepressants studied had only small effects on pain and duloxetine not better.

### Slide 41
Dosing Tricyclics - e.g., nortriptyline

Caution: Overdose risk.  10 day supply can be fatal
Contraindicated if recent MI, ischemic heart disease, cardiac conduction defects, urinary retention, narrow angle-closure glaucoma, renal failure, orthostasis (nortriptyline has least)
Obtain baseline EKG.  If bundle branch block, risk of serious arrhythmia is higher.  
Begin with 10 mg bid or 25 mg hs.  (5 bid in elderly).  Increase by 10 mg every two days until you get to 50 mg and then increase by 25 mg every two days until you get to 100 - 150 mg given in one dose.  If response unsatisfactory after 4 weeks and results have plateaued get a blood level.  Therapeutic range is 50-150 ng/ml. Do not exceed 150.
Check at least one blood level to rule out slow metabolism and potentially toxic level.

### Slide 42
Dosing the MAOI tranylcypromine

Initiate with 10 mg bid
Increase to 10 tid after a week.  If no response or partial response that plateaus at an unsatisfactory level, raise by 10 mg every 1-3 weeks until maximum dose of 60 mg daily.
MAOI diet required to avoid tyramine-induced hypertension. Watch for orthostatic hypotension, insomnia, agitation
Seizures, hepatotoxicity
Trazodone may be used (cautiously) for sleep.  Also benzodiazepines.
Low frequency of sexual side effects and weight gain compared with phenelzine and isocarboxazid.

### Slide 43
Something New?  Vilazodone

SSRI plus a 5-HT1a inhibitor (like buspirone). 
Two 8-week placebo-controlled studies, (J Clin Psychiatry 2009;70:326-33; 2011;72:441-7) but hasn't been compared to other antidepressants
Dosing:  10 mg for one week, 20 mg for second week, 40 mg for third week and maintain on 40.  Must take with food.
Sedating.  Nausea, vomiting, diarrhea. No weight gain.  Has sexual side effects but unclear if they are less common than with SSRIs. (Laughren TP et al, 2011)
Mfgr. no longer trying to get approval for GAD

### Slide 44
Something New?  Vortioxetine

SSRI plus a 5-HT3 and 5HT7 inhibitor (and others) 
11 studies, 7 positive.  8-week placebo-controlled studies, (Zhang 2014) 
Dosing:  10-20 mg doses were effective.  Start: 10 
Same sexual side effects as other SSRIs but otherwise well tolerated
It seems to have superior benefits (effect size 0.325), independent of antidepressant effects, for cognitive impairment associated with depression, compared with other antidepressants(Baune B et al. Int J Neuropsychopharm 2018).  FDA psychopharm advisory committee voted a special indication. Full FDA said no

### Slide 45
Something New!  Esketamine

Approved in 2019. Administered by nasal spray
Glutamate antagonist at the N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor - and this is a NEW mechanism
It's the s-enantiomer of ketamine, an anesthetic approved in 1970, which later gained notoriety as "Special K" due to dissociative effects
Use as an augmentation of antidepressants - approved for treatment resistant cases.
Experts find more questions than answers about it and recommend many good trials before trying it (Schatzberg Am J Psychiatry 2019; Dubovsky, Journal Watch Psychiatry, Dec. 2019)
Costs $7-10,000 for the labeled two month course

### Slide 46
Remission vs. Partial Response

The goal of therapy is remission.*  Rates are about 35-45%.  "Response" is 60-70%.  Prognosis is worse for partial responders.
Partial response is often placebo response. Evaluate carefully.  Rapid early response that does not improve further or loses steam is often placebo response.
 "Augmenting" a placebo response with another drug is not best practice.
             *Keller MB.  JAMA 2003 June 18:3152-3160

### Slide 48
"Poop-out."  How often is it due to                             loss of placebo response?  from Zimmerman and Thongy, J Clin Psychiatry 2007;68:1271-1276

Patients who initially improve include drug responders and placebo responders.
When patients "relapse," some of these relapses are in patients who never experienced a true drug response in the first place.
Zimmerman's meta-analysis involved studies of acute and continuation treatment with SSRIs or placebo.
Using a formula by Quitkin et al (1993), he concluded that most relapses during continuation treatment occurred in patients who were initial placebo responders.

### Slide 49
Management of Selected Side Effects

### Slide 50
Sexual Dysfunction (SD)

A big problem in primary care:(JAMA 2003 July 2:57-65) 
Meta-analysis (Serretti and Chiesa 2009) found treatment-emergent SD in 40-80% for most SSRIs, SNRIs.  Placebo:  14%.  
First Choice: switch, to bupropion, mirtazapine (weight gain), nefazodone (liver risk).  Trazodone?
Add-ons: 
Cochrane Review (2004,5) found sildenafil was clearly better than placebo (in men only). There also was support for tadalafil.

### Slide 51
Sexual Dysfunction - 2

Add-Ons (cont): 
No other options are supported by adequate evidence.  
Bupropion helped sexual desire (only) in one RCT, but had no benefit in another, when used at a lower dose. 
Post-SSRI Sexual Dysfunction (Ben-Sheetrit 2015; Patacchini and Cosci 2019)
Survey of 532 pts with normal pre-treatment functioning claiming their impairment did not resolve after d/c at least one month later.
23 "high probability" cases, 183 possible cases
Reporting bias a major limitation of these data
Should prescribers mention this risk??

### Slide 52
Insomnia/Nightmares

Highest insomnia incidence with bupropion and SNRIs: 2-2.3 odds ratio vs. placebo1
Trazodone 25-100 mg has efficacy, is cost-effective,2 no dependence, short half life: "in many ways an ideal hypnotic."3  
Benzodiazepine, for patient with no substance abuse history. ? In PTSD.
Antihistamines: problem of tolerance
Nightmares/disturbed awakenings due to PTSD: prazosin 1-20+ mg hs
Quetiapine: problem of the "munchies" and metabolic consequences4
1Alberti JCP 2015 2Sleep Med 2004:5(1):7-8 3Stahl SM. CNS Spectr 2009:14(10):536-46. 4Cates ME Comm MH J 2009

### Slide 53
Sweating

Research needed.  The following could help:
Benztropine 0.5 mg bid
Clonidine 0.1 mg bid
Alpha -1 adrenergic blocker, e.g. terazosin 1-5 mg/d

### Slide 54
SUICIDAL IDEATION AS A SIDE EFFECT

Children and adolescents:  FDA warning, which was extended to young adults through age 24.
Ideation increase is up 2 fold. Actual suicide: no incr. 
Effect confirmed in large review by Sharma, BMJ '16
Antidepressants are not very effective for depression in children and adolescents.  They are better for non-OCD anxiety disorders. (JAMA April 18, 2007; 1683-96)
After the warning, antidepressant prescription rates initially went down and suicide rates went up.  Recently, though, usage and suicide rates seem to have returned to baseline (Scandinavia)
Adults >24:  No FDA warning.  Some studies show small suicide ideation effect (Arch GS Dec. 2006:1358-67) and some do not (Am J Psych Jan. 2006:41-47). ?Paroxetine?

### Slide 55
DECREASED BONE MINERAL DENSITY & INCREASED FRACTURE RISK w. SSRIs(Richards JB et al.  Arch Intern Med 2007;167:188-194Rivelli SK, Muzyk AJ.  Psychopharm Review 2009;44(8):1-8.)

Observational study of 5000 adults over age 50, of whom 137 were on SSRIs:
Risk of Fragility Fracture increased 2.1 fold in the SSRI group, after adjustment for covariates. Effects dose-dependent
SSRI patients also had increased falls, decreased bone mineral density.
Some studies did not find bone loss from SSRIs, but one found HR 1.75: (Diem S 2013, Sheu 2015) 
Recommendation: adequate Ca++ intake, weight-bearing exercise, smoking cessation, and bone mineral density screening.

### Slide 56
Diabetes Risk with Antidepressants(Andersohn et al.  Am J Psychiatry 2009;166:591-8)

Case-controlled observational study from U.K. General Practice examined 2,243 cases of diabetes.  
Two year use of moderate or greater dose of antidepressants increased risk of diabetes by 1.84 incidence ratio.
Paroxetine associated with the most diabetes among the commonly prescribed SSRIs (1.33 risk ratio)

### Slide 57
New Observational Report on Long Term Weight Gain From Antidepressants

Uguz et al 2015 monitored weight gain on 362 patients for up to 3 years
55% gained an average of 11 lb
41% gained > 7% body weight
Only fluoxetine = no significant weight gain.  Others:
Mirtazapine 72%
Paroxetine 55%
Venlafaxine 52%
Escitalopram 49%
Limitations: it was patient self-report

### Slide 58
From:  Fava M et al.  J Clin Psychiatry 2000;61:863-7.

### Slide 59
Upper GI Bleeding and SSRIs

Risk is increased by an odds ratio of 1.3 x to 6.3 x in different observational studies. NNH ~ 100.
Risk increased to 10x or more by concomitant NSAIDs and antiplatelet agents like clopidogrel (Plavix), alcohol excess, ginkgo, & previous hx bleeding. 
Risk reduced by taking omeprazole and others
SNRIs and vilazodone have same or higher risk 
Mirtazapine and bupropion risk similar? - OR 1.2
Recommend: Avoid SSRIs in high risk pts., avoid NSAIDS, instruct patients, be alert 
Opatrny L.  Br J Clin Pharm.  May, 2008
deAbajo et al.  Arch Gen Psych 2008;65(7):795-803
Loke et al.  Aliment Pharmacol Ther 2008;27:31-40
Kyoung-Sae Na  et al. J Affect Disord 2018 Jan 1;225:221-226

### Slide 60
Venous Thromboembolism (VTE)

Unclear why an anticoagulant could also cause increased coagulation in some patients
Large observational study in UK found risk of hospitalization or death from VTE increased 40%
Deep vein thrombosis and pulmonary embolism
All antidepressants did it. 
Depressed control group - no increase (but small number of subjects)
Recommendation: warn patients who have risk factors such as age >60, h/o VTE, cancer, surgery, immobility, long air travel
Parkin L et al.  J Am Heart Assoc 2017;6(5):e005316

### Slide 61
Antidepressants in Pregnancy/Lactation

High risk of recurrence when antidepressants are stopped, (Cohen LS et al 2006) 
Severely depressed pregnant women have higher suicide risk.  Also ? low birthweight and preterm delivery of fetus.
SSRIs may affect risks of postpartum hemorrhage, premature delivery, and other postnatal complications (but hard to tell because of 'confounding by indication')
paroxetine has D safety rating since 2005 for cardiac septal defects. Some controversy (Malm et al 2011, Huybrechts et al 2014) but latest, largest observational study (Reefhuis et al 2015) strongly confirmed earlier findings.

### Slide 62
Antidepressants in Pregnancy/Lactation - 2

Another concern: 6 fold increased risk of pulmonary hypertension in newborn. (Chambers et al NEJM 2/9/06)
Mothers: Study of 5700 pregnancies found that SSRI associated with gestational hypertension in 19%, pre-eclampsia in 15%, vs. 9% and 5% in pregnant control women.(Toh et al, 2009)  Confounding by indication? 
Breast feeding: lowest infant serum levels appear to be with sertraline and paroxetine 
Consider psychotherapy for mild-moderate depression.  Severe, psychotic, bipolar, suicidal depression: collaborative decision-making. (Yonkers KA et al.  Gen Hosp Psychiatry 2009;31:403-)

### Slide 63
Augmentations:  Evidence-Base* and Costs**

*Thase ME.  CNS Spectrums 2004;9(11):808-821.(updated)
A= >1 RCTs
B= 1 RCT, plus c
C= Case series, anecdotal report, expert opinion

Mohamed S: JAMA 2017:318:132-145

**US Dept. of Veterans Affairs procurement cost Sept, 2017

### Slide 64
VAST-D Trial: Comparison of switching to bupropion or augmenting with bupropion or aripiprazole: Remission Rates in 1500 patients

VA study.  47% also had PTSD.  85% men.
Number of previous trials - mean 2.5
Aripiprazole augmentation (10 mg) - 29%
Bupropion augmentation (to 400 mg) - 27%
Bupropion switch - 22%
NNT for aripiprazole aug over bupropion switch was 14
PTSD group had poorer overall outcome but same ratios
25% gained significant weight on aripiprazole (> 7%)
5% significant weight gain on the other options, and 12% lost significant weight on bupropion (5% on aripiprazole)
Mohamed S et al:JAMA 2017:318:132-145; J Clin Ps 2020;81(4)

### Slide 65
Some Question if Antidepressants Are Effective.*Kirsch I et al. PloS Med 2008:5e45; Turner et al.  NEJM 2008;358:252-60Fournier JC et al. JAMA 2010;303(1):47-53; Khin NA. J Clin Psy 2011:72;464

32 randomized trials (published and unpublished) submitted to the FDA for approval of the antidepressants fluoxetine, nefazodone, paroxetine and venlafaxine produced an effect size of 0.32 and a drug-placebo difference of 1.8 points on the Hamilton Depression Rating Scale.  This is of doubtful clinical significance.*
About one third of all antidepressant trials were never published.  33 of 36 of these studies produced either negative or questionable results.
In 6 placebo-controlled trials (imipramine - 3, paroxetine - 3), there was minimal to non-existent antidepressant effect in mild to moderately ill subjects

### Slide 66
Do they work? Answer Parker G.  British Journl of Psychiatry 2009;194, 1-3

Antidepressants often do not work - but...
Many of the studies are of extremely poor quality
But most importantly, there are problems with the diagnostic criteria for major depression used in studies and by clinicians
The more "biological" depressions that respond best to drugs are those with melancholia, especially with psychomotor retardation
Milder, briefer, and personality-based depressions spontaneously remit or respond well to attention.

### Slide 67
Lecture Conclusions and Recommendations

Prescribe antidepressants - when indicated and likely to be effective.
Knowledge of drug costs and cost-effective hierarchies will increase flexibility to deal with formulary issues and benefit patients
Consider consulting evidence-based practice guidelines and algorithms to assist with clinical decision-making

### Slide 26
DSM-5Mixed Features Specifier

Replaces Mixed Episode Criteria
Applies to Mania, Hypomania, and Major Depression
Specifier added when Subthreshold symptoms of the opposite polarity present.
Symptoms present concurrently or waxing/waning juxtaposition.

Major Depression
(plus 3 of the following)

elevated, expansive mood
inflated self-esteem
overtalkativeness
racing thoughts
excessive goal-directed activity
Impulsivity
decreased need for sleep

Hypomania or Mania
(plus 3 of the following)

depressed or dysphoric mood
anhedonia
worthlessness or guilt
fatigue
psychomotor retardation
suicidal thoughts

Mania + Depression = 
Mania with Mixed Features

http://www.dsm5.org/ProposedRevisions

### Slide 27
DSM-5 Mixed Features SpecifierExcluded symptoms

Potentially overlapping symptoms are excluded.

Major Depression Symptoms

sleep disturbance
appetite disturbance / weight change
psychomotor agitation
poor concentration

Mania Symptoms

irritability
psychomotor agitation
distractibility

### Slide 2
Things to Know about these Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 10/20/2020.

### Slide 3
Pre-Lecture Exam	Question 1

Which of the following are true regarding the known risks associated with antidepressant use in pregnancy?
SSRI neonatal adaptation syndrome occurs in 20-30% of infants exposed in the last trimester of pregnancy
Paroxetine has been associated with congenital heart defects and is not recommended in pregnancy
SSRIs are not associated with an increased risk for spontaneous abortion 
All of the above

### Slide 4
Pre-Lecture Exam	Question 2

Which of the following are true regarding antidepressants and breastfeeding?
Sertraline is considered a "first line" antidepressant choice since it has very low levels in breast milk
Buproprion is contraindicated since it has been repeatedly associated with infant seizures in multiple studies
It is recommended that infants have antidepressant blood levels taken when they are exposed to SNRIs through breastfeeding
All of the above

Pre-Lecture ExamQuestion 3

Lamotrigine 1.3% at <100 mg day and 10% at > 200 mg day
10% in Lithium exposed infants
5% in valproic acid exposed infants
Atypical antipsychotics have been studied extensively and have been shown to be risk free, and are category A
None of the above
