### Slide 8
Risks of Untreated MDD in Pregnancy

Significant increased risk in meta-analysis of: 
	Preterm birth
	Problems with breastfeeding 
	Peripartum depression

Increase in most studies:
	Low birth weight

Grigordias S et al. J Clin Psychiatry. 2013; 74(4)321-41

### Slide 9
Risks Associated With Pharmacotherapy During Pregnancy

Teratogenicity: gross evidence of organ dysgenesis (eg, Ebstein's anomaly with lithium)
Occurs 2-8 weeks after conception, but can extend into 2nd trimester (craniofacial)
"Behavioral teratogenicity": subtle functional disturbances (eg, developmental delays, neurologic deficits)
Occurs throughout pregnancy
Perinatal complications: effect of drug on labor and delivery and immediate neonatal outcomes

### Slide 10
Risks for Spontaneous Abortion

Meta-analysis and systematic review did not find significant association between antidepressant exposure and spontaneous abortion (Ross et al. JAMA Psychiatry. 2013; 70(4): 436-43)
Estimated risk in general population: 12% of pregnancies
Estimated risk is less in patients with previous history of successful pregnancy (4-5%) and greatest in a history of recurrent miscarriage (24-50%)
			(Regan et al, BMJ, 1989; 26: 54-58)

### Slide 11
SSRI group and Malformations:

(Kallen and Olausson, 2007; Alwan S, et al. NEJM 2007; 356:2684-2692);
Louik C, et al. NEJM  2007; 356:2675-2683

### Slide 12
Specific Malformation RisksBerard A., et al. Br Med J Open 2017;7:e013372

### Slide 13
Persistent Pulmonary Hypertension (PPHN)

Rare condition in the general population: estimated 1/1000 births
Possible Causes:
Hypoxia and hypercarbia at birth (meconium aspiration, complicated deliveries)
Increased medial muscle thickness of pulmonary arteries
Vasoactive mediator abnormalities (nitrous oxide, leukotrienes, platelet activating factor)

SSRIs have been associated with an increased risk of PPHN in some, but not all studies-->increase from 1/1000 to 2/1000  (Jong et al. Reprod Toxicol 2012; 34(3) 293-7)

### Slide 14
Conclusions: Antidepressants and Congenital Malformations

Majority of studies do not report an association between antidepressant use and increased risk for major congenital malformation
Specific medications are associated with particular malformation risks
Several studies have shown risk for cardiac malformations for paroxetine
Paroxetine is not considered a first line treatment choice for pregnant women and should be avoided

### Slide 15
Neonatal Adaptation Syndrome (NAS)Neonatal SSRI "Adaptation" Syndrome

Clinical characteristics:
Respiratory distress
Autonomic instability
Poor feeding
Neurologic symptoms: tremor, myoclonus, seizures
Neonatal adaptation syndrome occurs in < 30% of neonates exposed to SSRIs in utero, leading to NICU and SCN admits
Etiology controversial: SSRI withdrawal or serotonergic toxicity?
Self limited, supportive treatment
Severe symptoms rare  (Forsberg L et al. PLoS One,  2014)

### Slide 16
Depression During Pregnancy: Treatment Implications

To switch antidepressant before or during pregnancy
During pregnancy: avoid switching compounds without previous history of response 
To decrease or discontinue antidepressant prior to delivery
SSRIs and TCAs have been associated with neonatal complications, including lower Apgar scores and increased rates of admission to special care nurseries
Reducing the dose does not increase risk of relapse
Discontinuing antidepressant substantially increases risk of relapse
Decision based on severity of depression, consultation with OBGYN/perinatologist

Casper, RC et al. J Pediatr. 2003; 142: 402-408; Center for Evaluation of Risks to Human Reproduction, 2004; Cohen LS, et al. JAMA. 2006;295:499-50

Cox JL, et al. Br J Psychiatry. 1993;163:27.

Major Depressive Disorder with Peripartum Onset

Onset 1st month Peripartum 
Often identified after 1st Peripartum month
 Depression risk:
Past mood disorder
Past Peripartum disorder
Depression during pregnancy
Anxiety during pregnancy
Poor support system

### Slide 20
Important Medical Differential Peripartum Depression

Peripartum thyroiditis (PPT)
Increased risk in women with history of PPT;  preexisting autoimmune disorders, high thyroid peroxidase antibody (TPO-Ab) titers in pregnancy
Stagnaro-Green J Clin Endocrinol Metab. 2012. 97: 334-342
Anemia 
SSRI and SNRI use in pregnancy is associated with an increased risk of peripartum hemorrhage in some but not all studies
Hanley GE. et al. Obstet Gynecol. 2016; 127(3): 553-61' Bruning AH et al. Eur J Obstet Gynecol Reprod Bio 2015; 189: 38-47
B12 and/or folic acid deficiency

### Slide 21
Can Peripartum Depression Be Prevented?

Estimated risk for peripartum depression in a woman with a previous history of depression: unknown 
Estimated risk for recurrence of peripartum depression: 25%
Limited research available for guiding treatment recommendations
Post-partum education and home visits: Variable resultts
Peripartum antidepressant "prophylaxis" trials:
   Nortriptyline no more effective than placebo
   in a RDB study (Wisner et al, 2001)
   Sertraline more effective than placebo (7% vs.
   50%) (Wisner et al, 2004) in RDB study

### Slide 22
Evidence Based Treatments for Perinatal Depression

Psychotherapy is effective treatment for mild-moderate perinatal depressions
Types of psychotherapy:
Interpersonal therapy
Cognitive Therapy 
Marital Therapy 
Brief dynamic Therapy

(Sockol LE.  J Affective Dis.  2015; 177: 7-21)    
Sockol LE et al. Clin Psychol Rev. 2011;31(5):839-49.

### Slide 23
Antidepressants for the Treatment of Peripartum Depression

Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitors (SSRIs) are the most frequently studied antidepressants in perinatal mood disorders
SSRIs have higher depression response and remission rates than placebo or "care as usual"  in meta-analysis
To date, few trials comparing antidepressants and psychotherapy
Molyneaux E et al.  JAMA 2015; 313: 19:196-1966.

### Slide 24
Brexanolone (Allopregnanolone) for Peripartum Depression

Allopregnanolone is a metabolite of progesterone
Allopregnanolone is a GABA receptor modulator and regulates mood
Progesterone and allopregnanolone increase during pregnancy and decrease rapidly after delivery
Thought to precipitate depression in some women
Allopregnanolone is formulated as the medication Brexanolone (ZulressoTM)
FDA approved for peripartum depression in 2019

### Slide 25
Brexanolone (Allopregnanolone) for Peripartum Depression

Brexanolone (ZulressoTM) treatment
Single 60-hour IV infusion
Typical response within 2 days
Dosing:
0 to 4 hours: 30 mcg/kg/hour
4 to 24 hours: 60 mcg/kg/hour
24 to 52 hours: 90 mcg/kg/hour 
52 to 56 hours: Decrease dosage to 60 mcg/kg/hour
56 to 60 hours: Decrease dosage to 30 mcg/kg/hour
FDA mandates a Risk Evaluation and Migatation Strategy (REMS) because of a risk for excessive sedation or sudden loss of consciousness during administration
Remission rates in 3 phase 3 trials ranged were about 60% at the end of the 60-hour infusion
However, the cost is extremely high (about $34,000 for drug only)

Meltzer-Brody S, et al. Lancet 2018; 392(10152):1058-1070

### Slide 26
"Treatment Resistant" PPD

No studies to inform treatment decisions
Algorithm similar to MDD w/non-Peripartum onset
Consider breastfeeding issues (e.g., lithium augmentation)
Always double check thyroid status
Always evaluate family and marital dynamics--marital problems have been strongly associated with persistent PPD
Strongly consider bipolar differential
Options:
ECT
Robakis T and Williams KE. Arch Womens Ment Health 2013; 16(5): 343-51
Brexanolone
Meltzer-Brody S, et al. Lancet 2018; 392(10152):1058-1070
Unclear if effective in treatment-resistant MDD with peripartum onset

### Slide 27
Ugus and Arpaci. Breastfeeding Medicine. 2016; 11: 9:487-489; Weisner KL. Am J Psychiatry. 1996;153:1132-1137.; Sriaman N. et al.  Breastfeeding Med. 2015; 10: 290-299; Llewellyn A, Stowe ZN. J Clin Psychiatry. 1998;59:4

Breastfeeding and Psychotropic Drug Use

All psychotropic medications found in breast milk
Concentrations of medications in breast milk vary: milk/plasma ratio poor indicator of exposure
Majority of clinical practice guided by case reports and clinical impression vs systematic data
Neurodevelopmental follow-up data limited to case reports that examine children in first year of life
American Academy of Pediatrics recommends that Relative Infant Dose (RID) of a drug be <10% and most antidepressants are < 10% 
RID is the weight adjusted infant dose relative to weight adjusted maternal dose (dose in infant (mg/kg/day)/dose in mother (mg/kg/day)
Absolute drug exposure is very low

### Slide 28
Estimation of Daily Infant Dose (DID)
Average drug concentration in milk (mg/mL) X volume of 
   milk (estimated to be 150 mL/kg/d) 
Relative Infant Dose (RID): Percent of maternal dose in the milk
Daily Infant Dose (mg/kg/d) / Maternal Dose (mg/kg/day)
RID < 10% of maternal dose is recommended by AAP

Principles:

### Slide 29
All SSRIs in general are compatible with breastfeeding
Paroxetine and Escitalopram: low or undetectable levels in infant 
  serum
Fluoxetine and Citalopram have been found to have RID >10% 
  in some studies so are not the treatment of choice if initiating
  antidepressant, but compatible with breastfeeding if woman already 
  taking (don't switch if effective!)
FDA warning regarding Fluoxetine---but if a woman used it in 
  pregnancy and it is effective, it is not recommended to switch to 
  another drug Peripartum

Which SSRI to Choose If Not Sertraline?

### Slide 30
Premature Infants, Lactation and Antidepressants

All studies investigating plasma concentrations in infants exposed to antidepressants through breastfeeding have included only term infants
No studies of premature infants
Liver metabolic enzymes (CP450) immature until approximately 2 months of age 
For very premature infants, it is recommended that pediatrician is consulted

### Slide 31
Risk of Recurrence In Bipolar Women During Pregnancy Off Lithium

Viguera et al, Am J Psychiatry,
 2000

### Slide 32
Teratogenicity Time Table

Days		Organ System		Associated Defects
10-32		CNS				Neural Tube
20-56		Cardiac			Ebsteins Anomaly
42-63		Lips and palate		Cleft lip and palate
24-56		Limbs	
60-140	Craniofacial		Craniofacial

### Slide 33
Pregnancy and Bipolar Disorder:Peripartum Period

Peripartum Psychosis: usually occurs within six weeks of childbirth, usually presents with delusions

BP women have 100-fold higher risk than women without a psychiatric illness history of experiencing Peripartum psychosis (1)

40% of the female BP subject population experienced Peripartum mania or depression (2)

Freeman et al (2002): 67% of 50 BP women with children experienced a Peripartum mood episode within one month of delivery

Pariser SF, Ann Clin Psychiatry 1993   
Jefferson et al, 1987

### Slide 34
Prevention Peripartum Relapse/Recurrence

Lithium prophylaxis in acute peripartum in women who have discontinued in pregnancy associated with decreased relapse risk (Bergink V. et al. Am J Psych 2012; 169: 609-15)
No consensus on best time to reintroduce prophylaxis but some experts recommend commencing in the second or third trimester to minimize teratogenic risk
Safety and effectiveness of newer medications and alternative treatments for prophylaxis requires further investigation

### Slide 35
Adverse Pregnancy Outcomes with Anticonvulsants

Adverse outcome = major congenital malformations (which could be attributable to drug) or fetal death
Rates:
Lamotrigine = 1.0%
Carbamazepine = 8.2%
Phenytoin = 10.7%
Valproate = 20.3%

Meador KJ, et al. Neurology 2006; 67:407-412

### Slide 36
Lamotrigine and Pregnancy

Lamotrigine not associated with increased risk of congenital malformations at doses <325 mg/d
Lamotrigine drug metabolism increases during 2nd and 3rd trimester of pregnancy, so doses may need to be raised
For patients on a high pre-pregnancy dose, recommend obtaining a pre-pregnancy blood level and checking it again during pregnancy, esp if dose increase is needed
If patient is stable, decrease of the dose to prepregnancy level Peripartum
Variable transfer of Lamotrigine into breast milk
Breastfeeding is not contraindicated, but infant should be monitored 
and pediatrician consulted
            Kahn et al. Curr Psychiatry Rep. 2016 18: 12-23

### Slide 37
Lithium and Pregnancy

Lithium 
Ebstein's anomaly in general population 1/20,000
Reanalyzed rate in Lithium exposed infants is
   1/1000 or 2/1000 
Counsel that risk is very low,  (20-40 times the rate in general population, but very low absolute risk)
Cardiac risk is in first trimester exposure to lithium
Limited studies of follow up of children exposed to lithium in utero

Yonkers et al. American J Psychiatry, 2004

### Slide 38
Treatment with Lithium During Pregnancy

Levels in umbilical cord blood=maternal blood levels
Fetal Ultrasound and Level II Ultrasound at 20 weeks recommended
Avoid toxicity at delivery by discontinuing the dose for approx. 48 hours
Neonatal toxicity is directly related to maternal blood levels

Newport et al., Am J Psychiatry, 2005

### Slide 39
Lithium and Breastfeeding

Secretion of lithium in breastmilk is very variable and metabolism by infant is very variable
Premature, dehydrated, febrile infants are at high risk  for Lithium toxicity when breastfeeding
Lethargy, EKG changes and thyroid abnormalities have been reported in infants exposed to Lithium through breastfeeding
Healthy infants receive 22-42% of maternal dose 
Monitor both infant and maternal serum Li levels frequently
https://toxnet.nlm.nih.gov/newtoxnet/lactmed.htm

### Slide 40
Atypical Antipsychotic Use During Pregnancy

Conventional, but not atypical, antipsychotics linked with hyperprolactinemia and amenorrhea
Exception: risperidone and paliperidone associated with increased prolactin levels
Animal studies: no teratogenic or embryotoxic effects
Most atypical APs linked to weight gain
Exceptions:
Ziprasidone weight neutral
Modest weight gain: aripiprazole, brexpiprazole, lurasidone, cariprazine
Antipsychotics have been associated with gestational diabetes (Cohen LS et al. 2018)
Quietapine has lowest rate of placental passage in small comparative study (Newport et al. Am J Psych 2007; 164: 1214-20.

Volavka et al. J Clin Psychiatry 2004 . Allison et al. Am J Psychiatry 1999; Cohen LS, et al, Am J Psychiatry. 2018; Huhn M, et al. Lancet 2019, 394:939-951

### Slide 41
Preconception Counseling In Bipolar Patients

Careful review of previous affective episodes--look for modifiable precipitants, including seasonal relationship, sleep disruptions, travel, medications
Careful review of menstrual cycle history
Careful review of family history:  Peripartum psychosis may cluster in families!
Recommend folic acid 400 micrograms/day and 3-4 mg/day if continuing a mood stabilizer
Recommend BBT +/- LH kit monitoring to confirm ovulation  as goal is  Minimum Time Off Mood Stabilizer if patient decides to discontinue!!
Aim for mono therapy if possible

### Slide 42
Post-Lecture Exam	Question 1

Which of the following are true regarding the known risks to antidepressant use in pregnancy?
SSRI neonatal adaptation syndrome in 20-30% of infants exposed in the last trimester of pregnancy
Paroxetine has been associated with congenital heart defects
SSRIs are not associated with an increased risk for spontaneous abortion 
All of the above
Answer: D

### Slide 43
Pre-Lecture Exam	Question 1

Which of the following are true regarding antidepressants and breastfeeding?
Sertraline is considered a "first line" antidepressant choice since it has very low levels in breast milk
Buproprion is contraindicated since it has been repeatedly associated with infant seizures in multiple studies
It is recommended that infants have antidepressant blood levels taken when they are exposed to SNRIs through breastfeeding
All of the above

### Slide 44
Pre-Lecture ExamQuestion 3

Rates of congenital malformations with mood stabilizers in the treatment of bipolar women have been estimated to be the following:
Lamotrigine 1.3% at <100 mg day and 10% at > 200 mg day
10% in Lithium exposed infants
5% in valproic acid exposed infants
Atypical antipsychotics have been studied extensively and have been shown to be risk free, and are category A
None of the above

### Slide 1
Short Term Interventions with Potentially Long-Term Benefits in MDD

Charles DeBattista, M.D.Department of PsychiatryStanford University

### Slide 2
Things to Know about these Slides

The content is over-inclusive by design.

The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 9/18/2020.

### Slide 3
Disclosures

Grant support; Assurex, Jannsen, Brain Resources, St. Jude, PCORI, NIMH

### Slide 4
Novel Interventions for MDD

Ketamine
Rapastinel
Esketamine
Psilocybin
Mifepristone
Botox
Brexanolone
Theta Burst TMS

### Slide 5
Ketamine

Anesthetic agent
Used intravenously primarily
Used for chronic pain
N-methyl-D-aspartate antagonist;
Mu opiod agonist; stimulant (?)
Psychotomimetic; dissociation
Acute antidepressant efficacy not sustained

### Slide 6
Putative signaling pathways involved in ketamine's antidepressant effect

Scheuing et al Frontier in Neurosc, 2015.

### Slide 7
Zarate, CA et al. Arch Gen Psychiatry 2006; 63:856-864.

Change in the 21-item Hamilton Depression Rating Scale28 (HDRS) over 1 week (n = 18)

### Slide 8
Rationale for OPT KET NIMH Trial in Treatment-Resistant Depression

Several single-site studies supported the rapid antidepressant efficacy of ketamine in TRD; however, uncertainties remained 
small sample size
crossover design
saline as control condition
Response persistence
Safety and tolerability
Would a single infusion of ketamine prove superior to an "active" placebo in a parallel-arm randomized controlled trial?
Ketamine (N=47); midazolam (N=25)

### Slide 9
Primary Efficacy Outcome: MADRS at 24 hr

Murrough JW et al. Am J Psych, 2013.

### Slide 10
Response to Repeated Ketamine Infusions

*p<.05

Murrough JW et al. Biol Psych, 2013.

### Slide 11
Memantine for Late-Life Depression and Apathy After Disabling Medical Event: HDRS Effect

Lenze et al. Int J. Geriatr Psychiatry, 2012.

memantine (N-17)
placebo (N=19)
low-risk comparisons (N=19)

Week

Hamilton Depression Score

### Slide 12
NIMH Ketamine Trial

N=99
Single infusion over 40 minutes
Doses 0.1,0.2,0.5.1.0 mg/kg or Midazolam
Assessments day 0,1,3,5,7,14,30
Results; both low dose (0.1 mg/kg)and higher dose (0.5, 1.0 mg/kg) > plb on HamD-6 by day 1
Some sustained benefit to day 7 but not after

### Slide 13
AZD 6765

Synaptic glutamate binder
Recent multicenter trials failed to demonstrate efficacy in multiple doses per week protocols over several weeks.
Program reportedly canceled

### Slide 14
Rapastinel (Glyx-13)

Selective partial agonist of glycine site of NMDA receptor
IV administration: not orally active
Animal models show sustained changes in neural plasticity for >2 weeks after single dose
Animal models suggest potential benefits in depression, stress tolerance, cognitive enhancement
Human studies in OCD and Depression

### Slide 15
Rapastinel GLYX-13 in Major Depression

U shaped dose response in rat models and in Phase 2A study
No ketamine-like side effects
Phase 2A study - 1,5,10 or 30 mg or placebo; i.v.
N=114 MDD pts; Add on to standard AD vs plb
5 mg. and 10 mg. separated from placebo at day 7 but not at day 14; other doses did not
Reduced symptoms within 2 hours and lasted 7 days
Effect size for single dose 0.58

Burch RM: ACNP Annual Meeting, Dec. 2012.

### Slide 16
Ketamine and Morphine in OCD

IV ketamine significantly more effective than placebo in refracratory OCD; effects last one week in some patients (Rodriguez C et al Neuropsychopharm 38: 2475-2483, 2013).
Oral morphine significantly more effective than placebo in refractory OCD; effects seen the next day and last for 5 days (Koran L et al, J Clin Psychopharm 66: 353-359, 2005).

### Slide 17
Esketamine

S enantiomer of ketamine
About twice a potent as racemic ketamine
Similar level of disassociation
Available in IV and Intranasal form
Fast tracked by the FDA for treatment of TRD and acute suicidality in MDD

### Slide 18
Esketamine in TRD

N=30 (
Add on for patient who had failed current and at least one previous
0.2 vs 0.4 mg/kg IV vs placebo
Results: 0.2 = 0.4 mg/kg> placebo
Significant benefit in 2 hours
67% of 0.2 mg and 64% of 0.4 mg/kg dose responded by day one vs 0 % of plb
Response often sustained to day 35
Most common AEs HA, nausea, disassociation

### Slide 19
Figure 1

Biological Psychiatry 2016 80, 424-431DOI: (10.1016/j.biopsych.2015.10.018)

Copyright  2016 Society of Biological Psychiatry Terms and Conditions

### Slide 20
Implications for other MoA's

Combination of NMDA antagonism, mu opioid agonism, and dopamine release/stimulant properties could be problematic

Have we found an "iatrogenic, one compound speedball?"

### Slide 21
Possible Studies to Clarify Key MoA's

Assess abuse liability in patients using rating scales
Use of PET mu opioid ligand in ketamine treated subjects
Naltrexone to block antidepressant effects in ketamine responders
PET studies using raclopride to assess dopamine binding

### Slide 22
Psilocybin

5HT2a Agonist
Mushroom/mescaline/psychedelic 
Low abuse liability in animal models
Used for centuries by indigenous people 
Studied as an adjunct to psychotherapy in the 1950s and 1960s
Research largely halted in 1970 after Nixon era criminalization of psychedelics

### Slide 23
Open Label Psilocybin in Refractory Major Depression

12 treatment refractory depressives
10 mg. on day 1 and 25 mg. on day 8
8 of 12 patients responded at one week; 7 of the 12 maintained response at 3 months

R. Carhart-Harris et al, Lancet Psychiatry 3: 619-627, 2016.

### Slide 24
Double-blind Studies of Psilocybin in Cancer Patients with Comorbid Depression and Anxiety

Two positive double-blind studies 
Niacin or low dose psilocybin as controls
Full doses of psilocybin were 0.3 mg./Kg or 22 to or 30 mg./70 kg
Both studies demonstrated sustained responses at full doses

R. Griffiths et al, Psychopharm 30: 1181-1197, 2016; S. Ross et al, Psychopharm 30: 1165-1180, 2016.

### Slide 25
Low Dose Buprenorphine Reduces Suicidal Ideation

88 patients with clinically significant suicidal ideation
Buprenorphine 0.1-0.8 mg/day (mean dose 0.44 mg/day)
    or placebo for 4 weeks
Buprenorphine superior to PBO for reducing suicidal ideation at 2 and 4 weeks
No withdrawal symptoms after treatment discontinuation





Yovell Y et.al Am J Psych in Advance 10.1176/aapi.ajp 2015.155040535

### Slide 26
Fava et al. Am J Psychiatry, 2016.

ALKS-5461 as Adjunct in MDD

FORWARD-3 and FORWARD-4
814 patients in DB, PBO controlled 11 week trials in antidepressant non-responders
Doses of buprenorphine/samidorphan (0.5/0.5 mg and 2/2 mg)
Both doses not superior to PBO
FORWARD-5 (1/1 mg and 2/2 mg): 2/2 mg. superior to placebo

### Slide 27
RCT of ALKS 5461 (buprenorphine plus the mu antagonist ALKS 33) in SSRI non-responders

Fava et al. Am J Psychiatry, 2016.

### Slide 28
Psychotic Major Depression

15-19% of community-based subjects with major depression (e.g., Ohayon and Schatzberg, Am J Psychiatry, 2002)
Marked neuropsychological impairments (e.g. Schatzberg et al., Am J Psychiatry, 2000; Jeste et al, Am J Psychiatry, 1996)
Excessive HPA axis activity (e.g., Nelson and Davis, Am J Psychiatry, 1997); proposed as a cause of psychosis and/or cognitive impairment and target for treatment (e.g. Schatzberg et al., J Psych Research, 1985)
Mifepristone - GR antagonist 7 day treatment with longer term relief out to 8 weeks

### Slide 50
Summary

There are a variety of novel approaches to MDD that may have long term benefits
While some treatments like ketamine/esketamine, rapastinel may require repeated treatment others like botox, mifepristone and psilocybin, TBS may be effective  for months 
Phase 3 studies of esketamine, rapastinel, and botox will clarify utility

## Source: `03_Core_Topics/Mood/_source/605 - Depression in the Elderly.pptx`

### Slide 1
Depression in Later Life:Epidemiology, Assessment, and Treatment

James M. Ellison, M.D., MPH
Professor of Psychiatry and Human Behavior
Sidney Kimmel College of Medicine
Thomas Jefferson University
Swank Foundation Endowed Chair in Memory Care and Geriatrics
Christiana Care Health System
Wilmington, DE

Gary W. Small, M.D.Parlow-Solomon Professor on AgingDavid Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA 
Professor of Psychiatry & Biobehavioral SciencesDirector, Geriatric Psychiatry Division 
Director, UCLA Longevity Center
University of California, Los Angeles

*

### Slide 2
Things to Know About These Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated on: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 12/22/2020

### Slide 3
3

Self-Assessment Question 1:Which of the following statements is most correct?

A. Major depressive disorder is less prevalent in older than in younger adults.
B. Major depressive disorder in late life is associated with increased morbidity and mortality from medical illnesses and suicide.
C. Major depression is not a normal concomitant of aging.
D. Numerous published randomized controlled treatment trials are available to help guide the treatment choices for older adults with major depressive disorder.
E. All of the above are true

*

### Slide 4
4

Self-Assessment Question 2:Which of the following statements is most correct?

A. White matter hyperintensities are the most replicated neuroimaging abnormality in late life depression.
B. White matter hyperintensities and late-life depression have a direct cause-effect relationship.
C. White matter hyperintensities represent deposition of beta amyloid plaques in the prefrontal white matter. 
D. The most common location of white matter hyper intensities in vascular depression is occipital.
E. None of the above

*

### Slide 5
5

Self-Assessment Question 3:Which of the following forms of psychotherapy has/have been empirically validated for the treatment of depression in older adults?

A. Cognitive Behavior Therapy
B. Problem Solving Therapy
C. Interpersonal Therapy
D. All of the above
E. None of the above.

*

### Slide 6
6

Self-Assessment Question 4:Which of the following statements is most correct?

Efficacy of serotonin reuptake inhibitors in treating late life depression is similar to that of TCAs, though TCA side effects may be less tolerable. 
For nonpsychotic late life depression, the combination of psychotherapy and medication is recommended.
Older adults are more vulnerable than younger adults to anticholinergic side effects of antidepressants.
Older adults typically take more concurrently prescribed medications than younger adults, necessitating careful attention to drug/drug interaction possibilities when an antidepressant is prescribed.
All of the above

*

### Slide 7
7

Self-Assessment Question 5:Which of the following statements is not correct?

ECT is usually less efficacious than antidepressants in treating late life depression with psychotic features.
Intracranial mass lesion, recent CVA, or recent MI can complicate the safe administration of ECT.
Informing patient and family about potential memory disturbance associated with ECT will help them understand and tolerate this usually transient aspect of treatment.
Demented patients may experience transient cognitive worsening during the course of a series of ECT treatments.
Compared to bilateral electrode placement, unilateral nondominant ECT is associated with a lower prevalence of cognitive side effects.

*

### Slide 8
8

Major Teaching Points

Depression in later life is an important, under-recognized illness with severe consequences    in function and mortality.
Psychotherapy, medication, and ECT have     each been shown to be effective treatments and enough information is available to individualize treatment approach to a specific patient's needs.

*

### Slide 9
9

Outline

Prevalence and significance of depressive symptoms and depressive syndromes                 in later life
Characteristic presentations and assessment process
Treatment approaches
Psychotherapy
Medications
ECT
Treatment resistance
Maintenance treatment issues

*

### Slide 10
10

The High Prevalence of Depressive Symptoms      in Later Life

Minor depressive disorders are more common than major depressive disorders among older adults
Minor depression ~ 8-10%
Major depression ~ 3-4% 1
Depression is common in primary care settings 2
Depression or depressive sx in 17% to 37% 
35% to 50% reporting sx were not diagnosed       by PCP
Depression is highly prevalent and undertreated in LTC settings 3

1. Steffens et al. 2000; 2. Garrard JL et al. J Gerontology Medical Sciences. 1998;  3.  Brown et al.                      J Am Geriatr Soc. 2002;50(1):69-76.

*

### Slide 11
11

Failure to Treat DepressionPromotes Adverse Outcomes

Functional Decline / Increased disability1
Increased use of non-mental health services1
Increased mortality rate
Overall2:	>4x rate of death over 15 months 
Cardiac3:	4x rate of death within 4 mo after MI
Suicide4:	13% of population, 18% of suicides are >= 65 years old

Beekman et al. 1997; 2. Bruce and Leaf 1989; 3. Romanelli et al. 2000; 
4.       http://www.cdc.gov/ncipc/wisqars/default.htm

9th leading cause of death in US population 
12/100,000

### Slide 14
Definition: DSM 5 MDD

SIG 
      E CAPS
  Sleep
  Interest
  Guilt/worthlessness
  Energy
  Concentration
  Appetite/weight
  Psychomotor
  Suicidal

MDD = "Major Depressive Disorder"
American Psychiatric Association: Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition.
Arlington, VA, American Psychiatric Association, 2013.

Depressed mood OR loss of interest/pleasure, plus
At least 4 other SIGECAPS
Present at least during the same 2 week period
Distress or functional impairment
Medical/Substance/Psychiatric exclusions 
There has not been a manic/hypomanic episode
Depression resembles but differs from "responses
	to a significant loss" - NO BEREAVEMENT EXCLUSION IN DSM 5

*

### Slide 15
New in DSM 5: Persistent Depressive Disorder: 300.4

Summarized from American Psychiatric Association: Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of 
Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition. Arlington, VA, American Psychiatric Association, 2013.

Consolidates two earlier disorders: Chronic Major Depressive Disorder and Dysthymic Disorder
Depressed mood more days than not, most of day, at least 2 years
Two or more are disturbed: appetite, sleep, energy, self-esteem, concentration, hopelessness
No remission more than 2 months at a time in 2 year period
Major Depressive Disorder may also be present
No manic, hypomanic, cyclothymic, other psychiatric, substance, medical
Causes significant distress (social, occupational, other)

Cerebrovascular Risk Factors Are Common Among Depressed Elderly

Diabetes mellitus
Hypertension
History of cardiovascular disease

History of stroke
Cigarette smoking

*

### Slide 21
Cerebrovascular Disease Is Associated with Increased Late Life Depression

Vascular disease predicts onset of depression and depression predicts onset of CV disease/CVA1
Depressive symptoms are common with hypertension2 or with vascular dementia3
Depressed elders have an increased risk for ischemic stroke4
Depression's pathophysiologic effects on inflammatory cytokines, platelet aggregation, and lifestyle may increase risk for cerebrovascular disease5

1. Thomas et al. J Affect Disord 2004;79:81-95; 2. Rabkin et al. Am J Psychiatry 1983;140:1072-1074.  
3. Sultzer et al. Am J Psychiatry 1993;150:1806-1812; 4. Arbelaez et al. J Am Geriatr Soc 2007;5:1825-1830;
5. Maletic V, in Ellison et al (eds). Mood Disorders in Later Life. Informa Healthcare 2008.

### Slide 22
Vascular Depression

A hypothetical late-onset depressive syndrome with a distinct symptomatology linked with vascular disease
Defined by 1 :
First onset of depression >= 60 yrs old
Presence of HT and/or TIA or surgery for vascular disease
Overlaps with 
Subcortical ischemic depression
Depression Executive Dysfunction Syndrome

1. Kelly, Jr. and Alexopoulos, in  Ellison et al (eds): Mood Disorders in Later Life. New York, Informa  2008.

### Slide 24
Neuropsychological Correlations1

Presence of moderate to severe white matter hyperintensities in depressed patients has been linked with: 
Decreased agitation/guilt
Increased psychomotor retardation and disability
Poorer Executive Functioning
Poorer response to citalopram treatment
Greater relapse risk

1. Kelly Jr and Alexopoulos. In Ellison et al (eds). Mood Disorders in Later Life. 
Informa Healthcare 2008.

*

### Slide 25
Post-Stroke depression

600,000 CVAs per year in US
9-34% show MDD at 3-6 months after CVA
Minor depression is also common
Location of lesion is important:
Depression associated with left anterior and left basal ganglia lesions
Apathetic depression associated with basal ganglia damage
Emotional neglect/indifference/euphoria associated with right-sided lesions
Treatment 
No antidepressant shown clearly superior 
Stimulants, ECT also used

Whyte and Mulsant Biol Psychiatry 2002;52:253-64. Hama et al.  Post-stroke affective or apathetic depression and lesion location: left frontal lobe and bilateral basal ganglia. Eur Arch Psychiatry Clin Neurosci 2007; Santos et al. The neuroanatomical model of post-stroke depression: towards a change of focus? J Neurol Sci 2009;283:158-62.

### Slide 29
GDS 15 (Geriatric Depression Scale 15 Item)

1. Are you basically satisfied with your life ? 
2. Have you dropped many of your activities and interests ? 
3. Do you feel that your life is empty ? 
4. Do you often get bored ? 
5. Are you in good spirits most of the time ? 
6. Are you afraid that something bad is going to happen to you ? 
7. Do you feel happy most of the time ? 
8. Do you often feel helpless ?  
9. Do you prefer to stay at home, rather than going out and doing  new things ?

10. Do you feel you have more problems with memory than  most? 
11. Do you think it is wonderful to be alive now ? 
12. Do you feel pretty worthless the way you are now?
13. Do you feel full of energy ? 
14. Do you feel that your situation is hopeless ? 
15.  Do you think that  most people are better off than you are ?

29

GDS is in the Public Domain, can be freely reproduced and used. Score 1 pt for each "Yes" on 2,3,4,6,8,9,10,12,14,15 or "No" on 1,5,7,11,13. A score of 6 or higher suggests need for definitive diagnostic evaluation. (http://www.stanford.edu/~yesavage/GDS.html)

*

### Slide 30
30

Cornell Scale for Depression in Dementia

Scoring System 
A = unable to evaluate 0 = absent 1 = mild or intermittent 2 = severe 
Ratings should be based on symptoms and signs occurring during the week prior to interview. 
No score should be given in symptoms result from physical disability or illness. 
A. Mood-Related Signs 
1. Anxiety: anxious expression, ruminations, worrying 		a 0 1 2 
2. Sadness: sad expression, sad voice, tearfulness 			a 0 1 2 
3. Lack of reactivity to pleasant events 				a 0 1 2 
4. Irritability: easily annoyed, short-tempered 			a 0 1 2 
B. Behavioral Disturbance 
5. Agitation: restlessness, handwringing, hairpulling 			a 0 1 2 
6. Retardation: slow movement, slow speech, slow reactions 		a 0 1 2 
7. Multiple physical complaints (score 0 if GI symptoms only) 		a 0 1 2 
8. Loss of interest: less involved in usual activities 			a 0 1 2 
(score only if change occurred acutely, i.e. in less than 1 month)

*

### Slide 31
31

Cornell Scale for Depression in Dementia (2)

C. Physical Signs 
9. Appetite loss: eating less than usual 				a 0 1 2 
10. Weight loss (score 2 if greater than 5 lb. in 1 month) 		a 0 1 2 
11. Lack of energy: fatigues easily, unable to sustain activities 		a 0 1 2 
(score only if change occurred acutely, i.e., in less than 1 month) 
D. Cyclic Functions 
12. Diurnal variation of mood: symptoms worse in the morning 	a 0 1 2 
13. Difficulty falling asleep: later than usual for this individual 	     	a 0 1 2 
14. Multiple awakenings during sleep 				a 0 1 2 
15. Early morning awakening: earlier than usual for this individual    	a 0 1 2 
E. Ideational Disturbance 
16. Suicide: feels life is not worth living, has suicidal wishes, 	      	a 0 1 2 
or makes suicide attempt 
17. Poor self esteem: self-blame, self-depreciation, feelings of failure  	a 0 1 2 
18. Pessimism: anticipation of the worst a 0 1 2 
19. Mood congruent delusions: delusions of poverty, illness, or loss     	a 0 1 2

Alexopoulos et al. 1988

### Slide 42
42

Antidepressant Treatment of Late Life Depression Is Evidence-Based

Over 70 Randomized Controlled Trials of pharmacotherapy of geriatric depression
>25 comparisons of 1 AD to placebo, >50 comparisons of two active antidepressants to each other or to placebo
Many trials use older antidepressants, optimal rather than typical populations        and settings
Efficacy supported, with limited EBM support for greater side-effect superiority of newer agents

### Slide 45
Choosing An Optimal Antidepressant: 1. Efficacy

FDA-indicated antidepressants are effective in treating late-life depression1
Response rate (50% symptom decrease)2
50 - 65% in trials with ITT analyses
25 - 30% respond to placebo
Number Needed to Treat (NNT): 2.5 to 5
Remission (>=90% symptom decrease)2
Typically 30 - 40% with medication vs 15% for placebo
NNT: 4 to 7

1. Ellison et al. Mood Disorders in Later Life. Informa Health Care 2008; 2. Shanmugham et al. 
Psychiatr Clin North Am. 2005;28:821-35.

ITT: Intention to Treat
NNT: Number Needed to Treat

*

### Slide 46
Choosing An Optimal Antidepressant: 2. Potential Adverse Effects in the Elderly

Adapted/modified from http://www.cpgnews.org/DEP/tools.cfm

*

### Slide 47
Antidepressant Treatment is Safe and May Be Medically Beneficial in Cardiac Patients

SADHART1 showed that treatment is well-tolerated 
Sertraline 50-200 mg/d x 24 weeks
No change with sertraline vs placebo in:
Left ventricular ejection fraction
Heart rate, blood pressure, ECG
Trend with sertraline vs placebo toward lower incidence of death, recurrent MI, CVA, heart failure, hospitalization for angina
ENRICHD2 study found SSRI decreased life threatening outcomes among highly at risk cardiac depressed patients

1. Shapiro et al. Am Heart J. 1999;137:1100-6; 2. Taylor et al. Arch Gen Psychiatry. 2005;62:792-8

*

### Slide 48
Age exacerbates potential for adverse reactions and interactions at five levels
Changes in gastrointestinal absorption
Changes in protein binding
Changes in hepatic metabolism
Changes in renal excretion
Receptor site competition associated with polypharmacy

Choosing An Optimal Antidepressant: 3. Drug-Drug Interactions

*

### Slide 49
49

Choosing An Antidepressant:Consider Age-Related Changes in Pharmacokinetics and Pharmacodynamics

Reduced GI, renal and liver function
Lower albumin levels
Increased fat/muscle ratio
Increased receptor-site sensitivity for many drugs (decreased b-adrenergic)
Polypharmacy leading to drug-drug and drug-disease interactions

*

### Slide 50
50

Choosing An Antidepressant:Consider CYP 450 Drug/Drug Interactions

Some CYP 2D6 substrates
TCA, fluoxetine, paroxetine, trazodone, venlafaxine
selegiline
donepezil
morphine, dextromethorphan, codeine, meperidine, oxycodone, tramadol
encainide, flecainide, lidocaine, mexiletine
metoprolol, bisoprolol, propranolol, timolol, labetolol
Two CYP1A2 substrates
clozapine
warfarin

Some CYP 3A4 substrates
alprazolam, midazolam, triazolam, clonazepam
carbamazepine, lamotrigine
donepezil
acetaminophen
codeine
clarithromycin, erythromycin
ketoconazole
tamoxifen, vinblastine, doxorubicin
amiodarone, quinidine
calcium channel blockers
lovastatin, simvastatin, atorvastatin, fluvastatin, pravastatin
estradiol, cortisol, prednisone, testosterone
omeprazole

Zubenko and Sunderland 2000; Cadieux 1999

*

### Slide 51
Choosing An Optimal Antidepressant: 4. Cost

Affordability affects adherence 
Stick with generically available antidepressants when possible
Not available generically: 
Emsam (selegiline)
Vybriid (vilazodone)
Trintellix (vortioxetine)
Fetzima (levomilnacipran)
Spravato (esketamine)

*

### Slide 52
52

Antidepressant Choices:  Tricyclic Antidepressants (TCAs)

Advantages:  proven efficacy, availability of blood levels for selected agents, low cost
Disadvantages:  sedation, cardiovascular effects, autonomic side effects (hypotension), toxicity.
Examples:  nortriptyline 10 to 150 mg daily (guide treatment by plasma level of 50 - 10 ng/ml); desipramine 10 to 150 mg daily

*

### Slide 53
SSRIs - A Rational First Choice in LLD

Several choices for well-tested, generic agents with relatively benign side effect profiles, limited drug interactions, appropriate elimination half-lives:
Sertraline
Citalopram (Note FDA dosage warning related to QTc)
Escitalopram
What is different about vortioxetine? 
Agonist at 5-HT1A, partial agonist 5HT1B
Antagonist at 5HT3A, 5HT7
Blocks Serotonin transporter strongly, NE transporter less

Ellison et al., in Ellison et al (eds). Mood Disorders in Later Life. Informa HealthCare 2008.

*

### Slide 54
54

Antidepressant Choices:  Serotonin Reuptake Inhibitors (SRIs)

SSRIs and SNRIs
Advantages:  Effective with minimal toxicity, avoidance of autonomic side effects, less sedation, ease of administration
Disadvantages:  Overstimulation/insomnia, G.I. symptoms, sexual side effects, hyponatremia, drug interactions, cost
Examples:  escitalopram 10 to 20 mg/d; sertraline 25 to 200 m/day; citalopram 10 to 20 mg/day; venlafaxine XR 37.5 to 225 mg/d; duloxetine 20-90 mg/d

*

### Slide 55
55

Antidepressant Choices:  Ungrouped Agents

Mirtazapine (start:  15 mg/day) may cause sedation and increased appetite.
Bupropion (start:  37.5 mg BID or bupropion SR 100 mg q d) may increase risk for seizures at higher doses, is not approved for treating anxiety disorders, and is contraindicated with comorbid eating disorder.
Trazodone: rarely used as primary therapeutic agent because of limited potency and significant side effects (sedation, hypotension, priapism) but can be used as an hypnotic in conjunction with SSRI.

*

### Slide 56
56

Antidepressant Choices:  Monoamine Oxidase (MAO) Inhibitors

Advantages:  MAO levels increase with age, low cardiac effects, effectiveness for atypical depression.
Disadvantages:  Dietary restriction, potential hypertensive crisis, orthostatic hypotension, drug interactions.
Examples:  phenelzine 15 mg bid to tid, tranylcypromine 10 mg bid to tid, selegiline transdermal 6 to 12 mg/d

*

### Slide 58
58

In Treatment Resistant Patients:                                              To Switch or To Augment?

Simpler, less costly
Avoids drug-drug interactions
Reduces side effects 
Introduces "different mechanism"
Implementation is slower
Outpatient setting

Switch

### Slide 59
59

Antidepressant "Augmenters"

+ signifies presence of credible evidence base for use

*

### Slide 60
60

Small, mostly open label, studies support atypicals as SRI augmenters in depressed nongeriatric nonpsychotic adults1
Risperidone: 0.5 to 1 mg/d
Olanzapine: 1 RCT at 5-20 mg/d
Ziprasidone: 20-80 mg bid
Aripiprazole: 5-10 mg/d 
Quetiapine:  Anecdotal support for augmentation
Clozapine: Anecdotal support for augmentation
Quetiapine has been studied as a monotherapy in late life depression with positive results. 
In elderly, controlled trial for aripiprazole2, open trials for risperidone, brexpiprazole3

1. Papakostas GI. Essent Psychopharmacol. 2005;6(4):209-20. Review - not specifically geriatric. 
2. Lenze EJ  et al. Lancet. 2015 Dec 12;386(10011):2404-12; 3. Lepola et al. Int J Geriatr Psychiatry. 
2018 Jul 23. doi: 10.1002/gps.4952.

Augmenters: Atypical Antipsychotics

### Slide 61
61

Prescribe Atypicals with Caution                     in Elderly Patients

FDA warnings re use in psychosis/dementia geriatric patients raise concerns for older adults with depression as well.
CVA risk
Overall mortality risk
Concerns re metabolic effects
Weight gain
Glucose
Lipids
Safety and risk-management considerations

### Slide 62
62

Augmenters: Testosterone

Low total testosterone level predicts higher incidence of depressive illness among older men (enhanced with comorbid medical morbidity).1
One RCT found testosterone +SSRI = placebo + SSRI in older hypogonadal men2, while others have supported testosterone coadministration3 including in SSRI-resistant hypogonadal4 or eugonadal5 men
Prescreen: Contraindicated with prostate cancer, may cause hepatotoxicity and other adverse effects.

1. Shores et al. 2005; 2. Seidman et al 2001; 3. Pope HG Jr et al .2003; 4. Seidman and Rabkin 1998; 5. Seidman et al. 2005

### Slide 63
63

Hormone Treatments for Late-Life Mood and Cognitive Disorders: Estrogen

Estrogen, historically used as an antidepressant augmenter, was supported by limited evidence but is used infrequently to treat depression following the WHI and other studies raising concern about the safety of estrogen treatment.

### Slide 64
64

Antidepressant "Co-Prescribing"

+ signifies presence of credible evidence base for use

Dew et al. 2007; 2. Lavretsky et al. Am J Psychiatry. 2015 Jun;172:561-9.

### Slide 65
65

Delusional Depression:An Important Source of Treatment Resistance in Late Life Depression

More prevalent among older than younger depressives
Associated with:
Hypochondriacal and nihilistic delusions
Worse response to antidepressant monotherapy
Longer hospitalizations
High relapse rate
Delusional relapses

### Slide 66
66

Treatment of Late Life Delusional Depression

ECT may be more rapidly effective
No RCTs guide choice of agent (antipsychotic or antidepressant) in treatment of geriatric psychotic depression
Antipsychotic adjunctive treatment shown important in younger adults
Expert consensus: APD + AD is first line treatment for geriatric psychotic major depression1
Atypicals vs typicals?  Two positive delusional depression treatment studies support use of olanzapine in younger adults - no evidence base in older adults.

1. Alexopoulos et al: J Clin Psychiatry 2004;65 Suppl 2:5-99

*

*

### Slide 67
67

Electroconvulsive Therapy

Underused modality, especially suitable with:
Antidepressant intolerance or non-response
Prior positive response to ECT
Delusions 
Catatonia
Mania
Emergency
High response rates documented1

Seiner S, Henry M: Chapter 10.  Electroconvulsive therapy for the treatment of late life depression, 
In Ellison JM et al (eds): Mood Disorders in Later Life. New York, New York. Informa, 2008.

*

### Slide 68
ECT Efficacy

Greater in older adults1
RUL: for >=60 yr old, 70.4% remission vs 46%  in <60
BT: for >=60 yr old, 75% remission vs 58.3%  in  <60
Better than meds in recent comparison:* 
3.1 +/- 1.1 wk to ECT remission vs 4.0 +/- 1 wk with meds2
Remission rate: 63.8% at 6 wk vs 33.3% at 12 wk in med group2
Cognitive effects: stable or improved  in recent study3, mixed findings in earlier studies attributed to technique and/or underlying disease.4

1. Sanghani et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry 2014;22:S114. 2. Spanns et al. Br J Psychiatry 
2015;206:67-71; 3. Verwijk et al. Int Psychogeriatr 2014;26:315-24. 4.Galvez et al. 
Curr Psychiatry Rep 2015;17:59-74
*This study contrasted results from two possibly noncomparable RCTs

*

### Slide 69
69

ECT and Medical Status Concerns

Rarely contraindicated in appropriate setting
Cardiac: Recent MI, unstable angina, arrhythmias, severe valvular diseases, CHF, hypertension
Pulmonary: COPD, asthma, infections 
Gastrointestinal: Aspiration or laryngospasm risk factors
Musculoskeletal: Stress to bones, joints, vertebrae during treatment or in subsequent falls
Neurologic: Intracranial lesions "substantially increase" risk1

Seiner S, Henry M: Chapter 10.  Electroconvulsive therapy for the treatment of late life depression, 
In Ellison JM et al (eds): Mood Disorders in Later Life. New York, New York. Informa, 2008.

*

### Slide 70
70

ECT and Memory Loss

A major concern of patients and families
ECT may improve depression-impaired cognition but exacerbate impaired cognition of dementia
Preparation:
Education of patient/family
Pre-screen to establish baseline
Monitor of memory throughout treatment course
Decrease treatment frequency when pronounced
Treat unilaterally when possible

Seiner S, Henry M: Chapter 10.  Electroconvulsive therapy for the treatment of late life depression, 
In Ellison JM et al (eds): Mood Disorders in Later Life. New York, New York. Informa, 2008.

*

### Slide 71
Treatment of Vascular Depression Requires:

Specialized assessment/awareness:
Lifestyle and illness issues, vascular risk factors
Attention to different spectrum of depressive symptoms (e.g. apathy, executive dysfunction) 
Monitoring of medical parameters 
Interactions: Medical status and psychiatric treatments
Possibly a different treatment selection:
Problem Solving Psychotherapy?1
Range of antidepressant choices
Agents under discussion: D3 agonists, modafinil, stimulants, nimodipine2, other novel agents, ECT, rTMS

Alexopoulos et al. Am J Geriatr Psychiatry 2003;11:46-52; 
Taragano et al. Int Psychogeriatr. 2005;17:487-98.

*

### Slide 72
72

Anxiety Symptoms Are Highly Prevalent in Late Life

10-20% of adults over 65 show clinically significant new or recurring anxiety symptoms1
Symptom picture may confusingly emphasize agitation rather than worry; physical/cognitive rather than affective symptoms, such as sweating, restlessness, pacing, palpitations, poor concentration, fatigue, dizziness, dry mouth, GI symptoms, tremor, aches, pains
Anxiety even more prevalent among the medically ill elderly

1Himmelfarb, Murrell. J Psychol. 1984;159-167.

### Slide 73
73

Anxiety Symptoms are Commonly Associated with:

Conditions
Post-CVA
Coronary artery disease
IBS
Endocrine
Dementia
Depression

Medications / Drugs of abuse
Stimulants or sympathomimetics
Withdrawal of CNS depressants
Thyroid hormones
Nicotine
Antidepressants
Antipsychotics
Corticosteroids

### Slide 74
74

Differentiating Anxiety from Depression

Difficult, because they share disturbances of sleep, appetite, and cognitive functioning1
Initial insomnia, fear, loss of confidence more typical of anxiety2
Anxiety is often associated with depression in the elderly3

Colenda and Smith: AJGP 1993;1:327-338; 2 Salzman C: Conclusion. In: Salzman C, 
Liebowitz BD, eds: Anxiety in the Elderly: Treatment and Research 1991; 3. Fernandez et al. 
J Clin Psychiatry. 1995;56(suppl 2):20-29

### Slide 75
75

Depression with Anxiety: Treatment Recommendations

The claim for superiority of sedating antidepressants with anxious, insomniac depressed patients has limited evidence base.
These antidepressants are FDA-Indicated for GAD:
Paroxetine (but note the pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamics concerns for older patients)
Venlafaxine
Escitalopram
Other useful agents may include sertraline, citalopram, mirtazapine, tricyclic antidepressant, buspirone as adjunct
Avoid chronic benzodiazepines when possible

### Slide 76
76

Maintenance Treatment:What do Experts Recommend?

1 episode: 	Continue for 	1 year
2 episodes:	Continue for 	1-3 years
3 episodes	Continue for 	>3 years

Alexopoulos GS, Katz IR, Reynolds CF III, et al. Postgrad Med Special Report. 2001 (October):1-86.

### Slide 77
77

Citalopram Prevents Depression Recurrence in Elderly

N=121 outpatients > 65 yr
20-40 mg citalopram vs placebo for up to 104 weeks after 2 periods of open-label treatment (up to 24 weeks total) to establish and continue remission
No treatment-related serious AEs

Estimated cumulative proportion recurrence-free

Klysner R, et al. Br J Psychiatry. 2002;181:29-35.

Week in double-blind period

### Slide 78
78

Recurrence after Recovery from Geriatric Depression

Up to 15 years observational follow up (n=380)
Recurrence in
85% of those who had recovered
58% of those who had remained well at least 5 years
Risk factors (not in subgroup with 5 years recovery)
more prior episodes
longer depressive episode before intake
Low levels of antidepressant treatment 
never marrying
female

Mueller et al. Am J Psychiatry 1999;156:1000-6.

### Slide 79
79

Adherence in the Elderly

Among outpatients given an antidepressant:
Nearly 50% discontinue in 1st month
68% discontinue during 1st 3 months
Adherence correlates with positive attitude toward medication.
Nonadherence correlated with:
Higher perception of risk and presence of side effects

De las Cuevas et al. Eur J Clin Pharmacol 2014;70:89-98.

### Slide 80
80

Suggestions to Improve Adherence for Treatment of Geriatric Depression/Anxiety

Assess for cognitive impairment 
Assume adherence will be a problem
Ask about non-adherence 
Encourage patient to monitor self-adherence 
Include family members/other supportive individuals as monitors/helpers
Explore patient's conception of illness/treatment
Provide clear, easy-to-understand info (repeat frequently, oral/written)
Maintain appropriately frequent patient contact
Accept limited adherence in some patients but maintain dialog

Wetherell JL, Unutzer J. CNS Spectr. 2003;8(12 suppl 3):48-59.

### Slide 81
81

Importance of Recognizing Significant Psychosocial Factors:Effect of Severe Life Event on Time to Response in Elderly Depressed Patients

Fraction NOT Responding

Adapted from Karp et al.  Depression 1993;1:250-156, 1993.

Weeks in Treatment

### Slide 82
82

Summary of General Principles of Pharmacotherapy in Late Life Depression

Differential diagnosis and comprehensive treatment planning
Consider psychosocial and medical factors
Psychotherapy may be important treatment ingredient
Individualized consideration of treatment agents' properties
Begin with low doses
Monitor closely for response, side effects, and compliance 
Increase dose slowly and carefully
Avoid underdosing and premature discontinuation

### Slide 83
83

Self-Assessment Question 1:Which of the following statements is most correct?

A. Major depressive disorder is less prevalent in older than in younger adults.
B. Major depressive disorder in late life is associated with increased morbidity and mortality from medical illnesses and suicide.
C. Major depression is not a normal concomitant of ageing.
D. Numerous published randomized controlled treatment trials are available to help guide the treatment choices for older adults with major depressive disorder.
E. All of the above are true

### Slide 84
84

Self-Assessment Question 2:Which of the following statements is most correct?

A. White matter hyperintensities are the most replicated neuroimaging abnormality in late life depression.
B. White matter hyperintensities and late-life depression have a direct cause-effect relationship.
C. White matter hyperintensities represent deposition of beta amyloid plaques in the prefrontal white matter. 
D. The most common location of white matter hyper intensities in vascular depression is occipital.
E. None of the above

### Slide 85
85

Self-Assessment Question 3:Which of the following forms of psychotherapy has/have been empirically validated for the treatment of depression in older adults?

A. Cognitive Behavior Therapy
B. Problem Solving Therapy
C. Interpersonal Therapy
D. All of the above
E. None of the above.

### Slide 86
86

Self-Assessment Question 4:Which of the following statements is most correct?

Efficacy of serotonin reuptake inhibitors in treating late life depression is similar to that of TCAs, though TCA side effects may be less tolerable. 
For nonpsychotic late life depression, the combination of psychotherapy and medication is recommended.
Older adults are more vulnerable than younger adults to anticholinergic side effects of antidepressants.
Older adults typically take more concurrently prescribed medications than younger adults, necessitating careful attention to drug/drug interaction possibilities when an antidepressant is prescribed.
All of the above

### Slide 87
87

Self-Assessment Question 5:Which of the following statements is not correct?

ECT is usually less efficacious than antidepressants in treating late life depression with psychotic features.
Intracranial mass lesion, recent CVA, or recent MI can complicate the safe administration of ECT.
Informing patient and family about potential memory disturbance associated with ECT will help them understand and tolerate this usually transient aspect of treatment.
Demented patients may experience transient cognitive worsening during the course of a series of ECT treatments.
Compared to bilateral electrode placement, unilateral nondominant ECT is associated with a lower prevalence of cognitive side effects.

### Slide 88
88

Self-Assessment Question Answers

1. 	E
2. 	A
3. 	D
4. 	E
5. 	A
