## Source: `03_Core_Topics/Mood/_source/803 - Bipolar Depression.pptx`

### Slide 1
BIPOLAR DEPRESSION - 2020

### Slide 2
Things to Know about these Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 10/20/2020.

### Slide 3
Authors

David N. Osser MD, Associate Professor of Psychiatry, Harvard Medical School 
Dana Wang, MD, Formerly, Clinical Fellow in Psychiatry, Harvard Medical School

### Slide 4
Disclosures

The authors have no relationships with any pharmaceutical companies.

### Slide 5
Pre-Lecture ExamQuestion 1

Characteristics of the "pre-bipolar" presentation of major depression include all but
	A.  Family history of bipolar disorder
	B.   History of good response to antidepressants
	C.  History of antidepressant-emergent irritability, agitation or suicidality
	D.  Family history of suicide
	E.  Young age of onset

### Slide 6
Question 2

2. All of the following medications are FDA-approved for use in bipolar depression except
	A.  Aripiprazole
	B.   Lurasidone
	C.  Quetiapine
	D.  Lamotrigine
	E.   Olanzapine and fluoxetine combined (OFC)

### Slide 7
Question 3

3.  All of the following have low weight gain and metabolic side effects except
	A.  Lurasidone
	B.   Lamotrigine
	C.  Cariprazine
	D.   Quetiapine

### Slide 8
Question 4

All of the following are true of lithium except
	A.  Efficacy for acute bipolar depression
	B.   Strong antisuicide effect
	C.  Neuroprotective effects - increases cortical gray matter that is depleted in bipolar disorder
	D.  More likely to be maintained as a monotherapy in bipolar patients than any other medication

### Slide 9
Question 5

The International Society for Bipolar Disorders Task Force on Antidepressant Use in Bipolar Disorder recommended (Am J Psychiatry, November, 2013) all of the following except
	A. Avoid antidepressants if any history of antidepressant-emergent hypomania, mixed states, or mania
	B.  Avoid antidepressants if present or past history of rapid cycling
	C. Avoid antidepressants during any mixed states or if history of predominant mixed states in the past
	D. OK to use antidepressants in bipolar I depression only if a past history of positive response (but never as monotherapy)
	E.  OK to use antidepressants as a monotherapy in bipolar II depression if there are no more than 2 manic symptoms

### Slide 10
BIPOLAR DEPRESSION

From the Psychopharmacology Algorithm Project at the Harvard South Shore Program (PAPHSS)
Updated From Bipolar Disorders, 2019; Oct 25. doi: 10.1111/bdi.12860 epub ahead of print. 

ASCP Model Curriculum for Psychopharmacology, 11th Edition 2020

### Slide 11
BIPOLAR DEPRESSION

Interactive version at the PAPHSS website:
www.psychopharm.mobi

### Slide 13
PAPHSS Algorithms

Designed to approximate the cognitive process of a consultant psychopharmacologist who is an expert on the evidence-base.
Prioritize using the fewest medications to maximize efficacy, emphasize long-term safety and tolerability, and minimize adverse reactions.

### Slide 14
*BIPOLAR DEPRESSION

Although the DSM diagnostic criteria for bipolar and unipolar depressive episodes are the same, they are different in:
Natural history, onset, biological correlates, suicide risk, associated comorbidities, and:
The medications that are effective

### Slide 15
*Diagnosis

Suspect "pre-bipolar" presentation or bipolar vulnerability if patient has a:
 Family hx of bipolar disorder; a younger age of onset; a family hx of completed suicide; past poor response to antidepressants; a history of treatment-emergent irritability, agitation, or suicidality after antidepressants; postpartum psychosis.

### Slide 16
*Diagnosis

Before initiating pharmacotherapy:
Screen for medical or neurological contributors
Treat any comorbid conditions, e.g., thyroid disease, substance abuse, and address special considerations (e.g., pregnancy)
Make criteria-based diagnosis of Bipolar Depression: Major depressive episode with hx of hypomania or mania

### Slide 17
*Comorbidity and special patient populations in bipolar depression and how they affect the algorithm

Consider at each node of the algorithm whether one of these is present: the standard recommendations may then change
Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD)
Posttraumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD)
DSM-5 Anxiety Disorders
Substance Use Disorders
Women of Child Bearing Potential/Pregnancy
Cardiac/QTc issues and other Medical Comorbidities

### Slide 18
*ADHD - a 20% comorbidity

Observational study in 2,307 Swedish adults with bipolar given methylphenidate (MPH) for ADHD (Viktorin et al, AJP 2017): 
7 fold increased risk of mania if the patient was not on a mood stabilizing medication over next 3 months.  And again, 3-6 months
0.6 hazard ratio for mania if the patient WAS on a mood stabilizer: So MPH was safe and even had some protective effect
Emphasizes the importance of diagnosing bipolar in ADHD patients before treating.

### Slide 19
*PTSD

20% comorbidity lifetime (Hernandez 2013)
Do not recommend SSRIs (two are FDA-approved for PTSD) in bipolar patients.
Use prazosin for PTSD symptoms rather than antidepressants (Bajor 2011, Raskind 2013) despite some negative data in PTSD (Raskind 2018)
Lamotrigine has some efficacy in avoidance and re-experiencing symptoms (Berlin, 2007)

### Slide 20
*DSM-5 Anxiety Disorders

Incidence of up to 75% (Provencher 2012)
Avoid antidepressants
Consider buspirone, hydroxyzine, pregabalin, gabapentin depending on the anxiety disorder
Valproate sometimes helpful in treatment-resistant panic disorder
Quetiapine may have a role but it was ineffective in one study of bipolar depression comorbid with GAD (Gao 2014)

### Slide 21
*Substance Use Disorders

Prevalence up to 60% in Bipolar I (Pettinati 2013)
Most bipolar clinical trials exclude patients who are actively abusing substances
Priority is to treat the substance problems to remission
Cannabis is very harmful to bipolar patients in all its forms.(Zorilla 2015, Mammen, 2018).  This is a tough sell but clinicians should be firm about it

### Slide 22
*Women of Childbearing Potential/Pregnancy

Avoid valproate and carbamazepine cat. X/D (Balon & Riba 2016) 
Lamotrigine an option, but associated with cleft palate.
Lithium OK. New meta-analysis of 13 studies found the odds ratio for any cardiac abnormality to be 1.86. The risk was limited to fetuses exposed in the first trimester.  The absolute risk was 1.2% for any cardiac abnormality.(Fornaro et al 2020)
Atypicals seem to have low risk, but avoid depots and
 CAUTION: anticholinergic add-on 
Olanzapine has gestational diabetes risk, change in birth weight
Prescribe as few drugs as possible - ideally, one. 
ECT is not as safe as once thought. 7% fetal mortality (Leiknes 2013)

### Slide 23
Medical Comorbidities

QTc - Least prolongation: lurasidone, cariprazine.  Most: quetiapine.(Kuhn et al, Lancet 2019)  Monitor for risk factors for Torsades e.g., bradycardia and electrolyte abnormalities
Liver problems - quetiapine, olanzapine, valproate, and carbamazepine can elevate LFTs
Renal filtration problems - avoid lithium. Lamotrigine is also renally excreted
Obesity - the best options are lamotrigine, lurasidone, and carbamazepine

### Slide 25
ECT may be considered first-line
severe suicidality, catatonia, poor oral intake, medical conditions that may limit use of psychotropics.

*INITIAL TREATMENTNode 1: Is ECT Urgently Indicated?

### Slide 26
*Is ECT Urgently Indicated? (Cont.)

Highly effective for bipolar depression (Goodwin 1990; Zornberg 1993; Devanand 2000), 65-85% response rate (Daly 2001, Medda 2009)
Response in patients with treatment refractory bipolar depression may be closer to 50% (Ciapparelli 2001, Grunhaus 2002)
Fewer ECT may be needed for Bipolar Depressed patient than Unipolar Depressed patients (Agarkar 2012)

### Slide 27
Is ECT Urgently Indicated (Cont.)?

ECT (R unilateral) vs. algorithm-based pharmacological treatment (Goodwin and Jamison 2007 algorithm), n=73, (Schoeyen 2015), in treatment resistant BD
ECT: 74% response by 6 weeks
Medication algorithm: 35% response
Critique:  algorithm was outdated.

### Slide 28
Ketamine IV infusion

Ketamine is an N-methyl-D aspartate antagonist 
Positive studies in bipolar depression (50-80% responded) (Diazgranados 2010, Zarate 2012)
Use 0.5 mg/kg over 40 minutes added to mood stabilizer - lasted 7-10 days. Can repeat
Off label but reasonable for hospitalized, treatment-resistant patients who refuse or do not tolerate ECT (Sanacora 2017, Zorumski 2017

### Slide 30
*Node 2: Patient not on any mood stabilizer

Five options: lithium, quetiapine, lurasidone, lamotrigine, cariprazine.  
Select the one which seems most suitable given patient's side effect vulnerability and preferences. 
Will discuss the evidence, advantages and disadvantages of each

### Slide 31
*Lithium:  Advantages

Modest acute efficacy - at best, (Zornberg 1993, Frances 1998, Baldessarini 2000, Nemeroff 2001) or maybe none (Young 2010)
Strongest antisuicidal effects (Baldessarini 2006, 2009, Griffiths 2014, Tondo 2016, Smith 2017)
Lithium more likely to be maintained as a monotherapy over time than any other bipolar medication regimen in 3 large surveys, one from the US and two from Europe (Baldessarini 2007, Kessing 2011, Hayes 2016)
Unique neuroprotective effects (Bearden 2007; Nunes 2007; Fornai 2008) and increases cortical grey matter (Moore 2009, Lyoo 2010, Gildengers 2015, Giakoumatos 2015)

### Slide 32
*Lithium Disadvantages:

Many patients do not tolerate relatively minor side effects such as tremor, nausea, loose stools, blunted high moods (Cipriani 2011) but these can be managed (Gitlin 2016)
Abrupt discontinuations can destabilize (Goodwin 1994)
Risks of end stage renal disease NNH=300. (Bendz 2010) but risk may be lower due to bias in studies (Kessing 2015, Tondo 2017)
Works best and most likely to be adhered-to when started early in course.  Work less well in rapid cyclers but so do all others. (Kessing 2014)

### Slide 33
Lithium (Cont.):

Target serum level >0.8 mEq/L for acute depression if lower levels ineffective (Nemeroff 2001)
Proposed target maintenance level of 0.6-0.8 mEq/L (Severus 2008, Nolen 2019)

### Slide 34
*Quetiapine at Node 2

Quetiapine efficacy in Bipolar Depression, BOLDER I, n=511, 8 wk, placebo-controlled, industry supported, 58% of patients responded compared to 36% with placebo, separation from placebo in one week (Calabrese 2005)
Effects in bipolar I > II. Rapid cyclers responded

### Slide 35
Node 3: Quetiapine (Cont.):

BOLDER II replicated BOLDER I but with higher placebo response, better response in Bipolar II depression, but sleep and appetite with more impressive improvement than mood and suicidality (Thase 2006)
FDA indication for bipolar depression because of these two studies

### Slide 36
Node 2: Quetiapine (Cont.):

EMBOLDEN I, n=802, 8 wk, double-blind, placebo-controlled, quetiapine more effective than placebo (which was not different from low dose lithium at 0.6 mEq/L) (Young 2010)
EMBOLDEN II, n=740, 8 wk, double-blind, placebo-controlled, quetiapine more effective than placebo (which was equal to paroxetine) (McElroy 2010)

### Slide 37
*Quetiapine (Cont.):

Quetiapine may have maintenance efficacy (Weisler 2011) but the study was flawed by having a 100% enriched sample of quetiapine responders. (Goodwin 2011)
Lithium and quetiapine were equally effective in the Weisler study and better than placebo.  That result is more impressive for lithium, actually

### Slide 38
Weisler et al 2008

### Slide 39
*Node 2: Quetiapine (Cont.):

Weight gain, metabolic side effects including insulin resistance (Ngai 2014), and sedation are liabilities. 
No antisuicidal or neuroprotective effects have been found.

### Slide 40
*Node 2: Lamotrigine

Evidence in acute bipolar depression is not strong. 
First study, n= 195, double-blind, placebo controlled, on 200 mg/day 51% of patients showed improvement, 41% with 50 mg and 26% with placebo (Calabrese 1999).

### Slide 41
*Lamotrigine (Cont.):

But then the next 4 studies did not show efficacy.  No FDA-approval for acute BD.
Pooled data of the 5 studies showed modest efficacy and greater separation from placebo in the more severely depressed (Geddes 2004, Calabrese 2008). Effect size of d=0.27.  Small.
But in the More Severely Ill (Hamilton > 23) d=0.47 because placebo response lower.

### Slide 42
*Lamotrigine (Cont.):

However, FDA approval for maintenance to reduce risk of recurrent depressive episodes, because of two positive studies (Bowden 2003, Calabrese 2003)
No efficacy to prevent manias was found
But, it is well-tolerated, dangerous rash is rare, no weight gain, no sedation. For these reasons many may prefer to try it at Node 2 over the other 4 options.

### Slide 43
*Node 2: Lurasidone

PREVAIL-2, monotherapy, placebo-controlled, 6-wk,  n=505, significant reduction in depressive symptoms and improved quality of life (Loebel 2013).  Study used a special method of enhancing efficacy signal (Sachs 2014).
Minimal metabolic changes, in terms of lipids, weight, glycemic control, low QTc effect
FDA indication for Bipolar Depression in 2013
No studies in mania.  No maintenance studies.

### Slide 44
*Node 2: Cariprazine

FDA-approved in 2019 for bipolar depression in doses of 1.5 mg or 3 mg.  NNT in 4 studies = 10 compared with NNT of 5 for lurasidone. (Citrome 2019) 
Also was approved for mania in 2015 at 3-6 mg daily.
Thus it joins quetiapine as one of two medications approved for both bipolar depression and mania.
Minimal metabolic changes, in terms of lipids, weight, glycemic control, low QTc effect - all advantages over quetiapine
Nausea, akathisia, restlessness, EPS are dose-related

### Slide 46
*Not Recommended at Node 2, but      FDA-Approved  - Olanzapine+Fluoxetine Combination (OFC)

Large industry supported study, n=788, compared olanzapine, OFC, placebo, with significant response by OFC, non-clinically significant response with olanzapine (Tohen 2003)
Not recommended at this Node because of the severe metabolic side effects of olanzapine (Ostracher 2016)

### Slide 47
Olanzapine alone for bipolar depression?

Olanzapine monotherapy had a small bipolar depression effect in a trial in Japan (d=0.22).(Tohen 2012)
Olanzapine then was approved in Japan for this
Chinese study (Wang 2013) n=68 also showed significant benefit but with large metabolic effects.
But even single doses of olanzapine cause increases in insulin resistance (Hahn 2013)

### Slide 48
*Node 2: What about an antidepressant, especially if it's Bipolar II?

No antidepressant is FDA-approved explicitly for bipolar depression (I or II)
Some short-term studies without placebo control have found efficacy in bipolar II (Amsterdam 2003, Altshuler 2017). STEP-BD found no efficacy in bipolar depression. (Sachs 2007)
Antidepressants can have fewer side effects than the 5 more evidenced treatments at Node 2, so many clinicians want to consider them
In the STEP-BD study, antidepressant use (added to mood stabilizer) in rapid cyclers was associated with triple the rate of depressions per year compared with those not on an antidepressant. (El-Mallakh 2015)

### Slide 49
*Node 2:  Any other options?

Valproate: Might be effective in bipolar depression (Bond 2010, Smith 2010; Wang 2010) but it was a modest response in these few small studies.  No maintenance efficacy, weak antimanic effects (Tamayo 2010, Cipriani 2011), and many side effects including weight gain and fetal harm.
Insufficient evidence of efficacy of  carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine (Keinares 2013, Vasudev A 2011)

### Slide 50
*Node 2:  Summary

Lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, and lurasidone, and cariprazine are the five most evidenced options with acceptable safety
Choose whatever seems best for the patient's side effect tolerance and need for an agent that will prevent switching to mania
If it fails or is not tolerated, try another until you have tried all five, if acceptable.

### Slide 51
Bipolar Depression Algorithm (Cont.)

PATIENT ALREADY ON A MEDICATION
AND IS REFRACTORY TO CURRENT MEDICATION

### Slide 53
*Nodes 3,4,5: Is the patient currently on lithium, an anticonvulsant, quetiapine, lurasidone, or cariprazine?

If patient presents with acute bipolar depression despite taking one of these medications, you have to decide whether to optimize dose, add something, or switch. 
General Principle: You want to keep something on board that could prevent switching to mania, unless they only have mild hypomanias.

### Slide 54
*Node 3: Is the Patient Taking Lithium?

Optimize lithium level: If serum level is less than 0.8 mEq/L then increase dose (Nemeroff 2001)
If already higher than 0.8 mEq/L then go to Node 7 where the remaining four options of quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, and cariprazine can be considered for treating this depression.  
If lamotrigine or lurasidone is picked you should probably keep the lithium to prevent mania.  This is less clear for quetiapine and cariprazine - both are FDA-approved for mania but at higher doses than used for depression.

### Slide 55
*Node 4: Is Patient taking Lamotrigine, Valproate, or Carbamazepine?

All three might work (Selle 2014), so optimize dose: lamotrigine to 200-400 mg/day (level ~4-11 mcg/mL - Kesefir 2013, Katayama 2014); valproate or CBZ to usually-employed levels for mania (50-125; 4-12 mcg/mL)
If doses are already optimized, then add/switch to lithium, lurasidone, lamotrigine, cariprazine, or quetiapine for reasons discussed at Node 2)  
Some would add antidepressants here, but the evidence support is minimal.(Sachs 2007)  More on that option later.

### Slide 56
*Node 5: Is the Patient on Quetiapine, Lurasidone, or Cariprazine?

Optimize dose to 300-600 mg/day of quetiapine, 40-120 mg/day of lurasidone, or 1.5-3 mg cariprazine for up to 6 weeks (longer if the patient is still gradually improving).
If still no response, then start or add lithium, lamotrigine, quetiapine, lurasidone, or cariprazine as discussed in Node 2

### Slide 58
*Node 6: Is the Patient on an Antidepressant, Olanzapine, or OFC?

Because these agents have no known efficacy or unacceptable toxicity for first-line use, the recommendation is the same as Node 2 (as when patient not currently taking a mood stabilizer).
Taper off these agents and start lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, or cariprazine. 
Taper gradually so as not to further destabilize mood (over 2 weeks).

### Slide 60
*Node 7:  Summary

Lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone and cariprazine are the 5 preferred initial options with the best evidence and safety
Choose whatever seems best for the patient's side effect tolerance
If your first choice fails or is not tolerated, try another until you have tried all 5 unless the expected side effects of one or more are unacceptable.
Any can be added to lithium, but quetiapine and cariprazine have antimanic effects so you might try to remove the lithium later.

### Slide 62
Is the Patient in a Mixed State, a Rapid Cycler, or Have a History of Either of those after an Antidepressant?

If NOT, an antidepressant can be considered now.
Patient should be on an adequate dose of one of the mood stabilizers. Even so, efficacy to be expected is small (McGirr 2016 meta-analysis of 6 studies found effect size 0.165, and no difference in response or remission rates)
First choice, for lowest risk of precipitating mania: bupropion.  Second choice SSRIs.
Avoid adrenergic antidepressants, TCAs, SNRIs, associated with more severe switches (Boerlin 1998)
Valproate could possibly be effective (Selle 2014)

### Slide 63
MORE ABOUT TREATMENT WITH ANTIDEPRESSANTS

Controversial in bipolar depression, historically mostly used in Bipolar II.
Are they effective? (McGirr 2016, as noted)
If effective, are they safe? (McGirr also found significant risk of switch to mania over 1 year, 1.8 OR compared to placebo)
Do risks of treatment-emergent mania or long-term mood destabilization outweigh the small potential for acute benefits?

### Slide 64
Antidepressant Studies (Cont.):

Another meta-analysis of 19 studies found antidepressants better than placebo (p<0.001, RR 1.257) but results in non-bipolar depression better (196 trials, p=0.008 compared to bipolar) (Papakostas 2016)

### Slide 65
*The International Society for Bipolar Disorders Task Force Report on Antidepressant Use in Bipolar Disorder

Pacchiarotti I, Bond DJ, Baldessarini RJ et al. American Journal of Psychiatry, November 2013;170:1249-62
67 Co-Authors (published experts)
If 80% of panel found an item "essential" or "important," it was included in the recommendations.

### Slide 66
*International Society of Bipolar Disorders (ISBD), Re Antidepressants:

"...the available evidence concerning both the value and risks of antidepressant treatment in bipolar disorder is remarkably limited, and much of it is methodologically weak. Therefore, it is not currently possible to make firm clinical recommendations that are soundly evidence based." (Pacchiarotti 2013)

### Slide 67
*ISBD Re: Antidepressants

"There is a striking incongruity between the wide use of and the weak evidence base for the efficacy and safety of antidepressant drugs in bipolar disorder. Few well-designed, long-term trials of prophylactic benefits have been conducted, and there is insufficient evidence for treatment benefits with antidepressants combined with mood stabilizers.  A major concern is the risk for mood switch to hypomania, mania, and mixed states."

### Slide 68
*ISBD (Cont.):

"......the use of antidepressants to treat depressive phases or components of bipolar disorder can neither be condemned nor endorsed without carefully evaluating individual clinical cases and circumstances."

### Slide 69
*ISBD  12 Recommendations (condensed) - When To Use

May use antidepressants if a history of previous positive response but never as monotherapy in Bipolar I depression. 
In Bipolar II, could use monotherapy but not if 2 or more manic symptoms.
If used, observe closely for any agitation, manic/mixed symptoms and stop the AD

### Slide 70
*ISBD  12 Recommendations (condensed) - When Not To Use

Avoid antidepressants if any history of antidepressant-emergent hypomania, mixed states, or mania.
Avoid antidepressants if rapid cycling now or in the past.
Avoid antidepressants during any mixed states or if history of predominant mixed. Discontinue antidepressants if mixed.

### Slide 71
*Post-ISBD:

Study of patients treated according to ISBD recommendations, n=101, treated with antidepressants, but high risk groups excluded, 75% responded to antidepressant, only 2.9% switch rate, so effective and relatively safe. But not a controlled study-observational, un-blinded. (Tundo 2015)

### Slide 72
*Post-ISBD: venlafaxine vs. lithium in Bipolar II Depression

Lorenzo-Luaces 2016 studied 129 patients with bipolar II depression, 60 were rapid cyclers (stratified for this)
Venlafaxine max of 375, Lithium max 1200 mg.
12 weeks

### Slide 73
*Post-ISBD: venlafaxine vs. lithium

Results:  40 of 44 patients responded to ven and 15 of 22 to lithium.  (many more dropouts on Li+) No difference in sustained response in rapid vs. non-rapid cyclers.
They were continued another 6 months open label.  3 ven patients relapsed and 4 on lithium
Surprise: no difference in emerging hypomania in the rapid cyclers! (43% on lithium, 18% on venlafaxine, p=0.31)
More randomized trials needed

### Slide 74
*Node 8: Using Antidepressants in Non-Rapid-Cycling Bipolar Depression

Our conclusion, again, about antidepressants at Node 8: After lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone and cariprazine, you can consider antidepressants (in combination with mood stabilizer) in bipolar depressed patients (Bipolar II>I) who are not rapid-cycling. We'd still wait on rapid cyclers despite Lorenzo-Luaces (2016)

### Slide 76
*Node 9: If Patient DOES have mixed or Rapid Cycling, we would still Avoid Antidepressants

Even minimal coexisting manic symptoms predicts bad results with antidepressants (Frye 2009)
History of recent, frequent, severe or dangerous hypomanic/manic switches (Boerlin 1998)
History of antidepressant-induced mania or hypomania (Goldberg 2003)
History of substance abuse (Goldberg 2002)

### Slide 77
*Node 9 (Cont.) : Rapid-Cycling Bipolar Disorder

Definition: 4 or more mood cycles per year, associated with high risk of mood destabilization 
Recommendation is to try combinations, not already tried, involving lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, or cariprazine

### Slide 78
*Node 9: Evidence Supporting Combinations

Quetiapine effective as add-on to lithium for maintenance (Vieta 2008; Suppes  2009)
Lamotrigine positive response if added to lithium (52% over 32% with placebo) (Van Der Loos 2009)
Lurasidone effective and FDA-approved for addition to lithium. PREVAIL-1, n=346, 6 weeks, double-blind, placebo-controlled, improved depressive symptoms and functioning, well-tolerated (Loebel 2013)

### Slide 80
*Node 10: REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION

After monotherapy with lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, cariprazine and combinations, or combination of mood stabilizer with antidepressant, reconsider ECT.  Other possible options: clozapine, ketamine, modafinil, omega-3 fatty acids, pramipexole, vagus nerve stimulation, etc.

### Slide 81
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION Other remote options.  Stimulants

Sparse data, open-label studies and case studies, ?mild symptomatic improvement, low switching, mostly methylphenidate studied
Lisdexamfetamine, adjunctive, RCT, n=25, showed decrease in self-reported depressed mood, decreased fatigue and sleepiness on days, decreased binge eating, but not different from placebo on MADRAS (McElroy 2014)

### Slide 82
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION Modafinil, Armodafinil

Modafinil, adjunctive, placebo controlled RCT, n=85, sig positive effect on depression (but primarily on energy sxs), no manic switches (Frye 2007)
R-modafinil (armodafinil), adjunctive, 3 large placebo controlled RCTs: One favorable (NNT=9), the other two negative.  Drug company decided not to file for an FDA indication. (Ketter 2015)

### Slide 83
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION, Pramipexole

Pramipexole, D3 agonist, 2 small positive short-term placebo controlled studies (n=21, n=22), added to mood stabilizer. (Goldberg 2004; Zarate 2004) Rapid cyclers excluded in one study.  Well tolerated though nausea in > 60% (i.e., they didn't drop out because of it)
Other reports of positive clinical experience (Fawcett 2016) at higher doses (2.5 mg vs 1.5 in the controlled studies). 
Adjunctive pramipexole may have continued benefits after acute response (El-Mallakh 2010; Dell'Osso 2013)

### Slide 84
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSIONOmega 3 fatty acids

RCT, n=116, showed no difference between ethyleicosapentaenoate and placebo in patients taking mood stabilizers (Keck 2006)
However, a smaller controlled study, n=75, showed improvement in mild to moderately depressed patients (Frangou 2006)
Meta-analysis of adjunctive omega 3 fatty acids, n=291, showed efficacy for bipolar depression, low-moderate effect size 0.34 (Sarris 2012)
Most tolerable bipolar depression tx available

### Slide 85
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSIONAripiprazole

Aripiprazole failed to show efficacy as monotherapy in controlled studies (Thase 2005); post-hoc analysis showed some improvement in more severely depressed patients (Thase 2012)
However multiple small open-label and uncontrolled studies showed some efficacy for aripiprazole as an adjunctive agent in bipolar depressed patients (Ketter 2006; McElroy 2007; Dunn 2008; Satajovic 2008; Kemp 2007; Sokolski 2007) 
Might work best at low doses, 5 mg (Kelly 2017)

### Slide 86
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSIONClozapine

Clozapine (effective for refractory schizophrenia), no controlled studies targeting bipolar depression. 
However small studies that have included refractory bipolar disorder showed possible mood stabilizing effect (Suppes 1999; Fehr 2005)
48 month follow-up study showed improvement in patients with refractory psychotic bipolar disorder treated with clozapine (Ciapparelli 2003)
Long-term study showing decrease rate of hospitalization from bipolar depressive episodes if clozapine was added (Chang 2006)

### Slide 87
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION, Sleep Deprivation and Light Therapy

Two studies of "chronotherapy" for bipolar depression involved keeping patients up all night and then administering a strong dose of fluorescent light.(Benedetti 2014)
Perhaps more impressive, a study of 7000 lux white light for an hour at Noon was compared with dim red light, in an RCT in 46 patients for 6 weeks.(Sik 2018)  Remission seen in 68%, vs. 22% in controls.  
Needs replication with more proof the "blind" was successful, and need maintenance study.

### Slide 88
*REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION Esketamine

Much discussion about this new product which has had little study in bipolar depression.  In unipolar it is effective and might even work as a maintenance treatment (Daly 2019) though these patients were not very treatment-resistant
Concerns about efficacy, side effects, opiate receptor agonism and potential for abuse - all suggest caution is required before this should enter the mainstream of algorithm recommendations. (Schatzberg 2019, McShane 2019)

### Slide 89
REFRACTORY BIPOLAR DEPRESSION Other

Possible value but not adequately studied or not ready to replace items earlier in the algorithm:
Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation (TMS)
Transcranial Direct Stimulation (Sampaio-Junior 2018)
Vagus Nerve Stimulation (VNS)
Deep-brain Stimulation (DBS)

### Slide 90
*BRIEF SUMMARY:

For both Bipolar I and II: First-line lithium, quetiapine, lamotrigine, lurasidone, or cariprazine - may be combined
Low priority of Olanzapine + fluoxetine because of long-term metabolic risks
Antidepressants (only with mood stabilizers) in low risk patients (i.e., not in patients with mixed syndromes or rapid cyclers.)
Combinations of the first-line agents not already tried

### Slide 91
Pre-Lecture ExamQuestion 1

Characteristics of the "pre-bipolar" presentation of major depression include all but
	A.  Family history of bipolar disorder
	B.   History of good response to antidepressants
	C.  History of antidepressant-emergent irritability, agitation or suicidality
	D.  Family history of suicide
	E.  Young age of onset

### Slide 92
Question 2

2. All of the following medications are FDA-approved for use in bipolar depression except
	A.  Aripiprazole
	B.   Lurasidone
	C.  Quetiapine
	D.  Lamotrigine
	E.   Olanzapine and fluoxetine combined (OFC)

### Slide 93
Question 3

3.  All of the following have low weight gain and metabolic side effects except
	A.  Lurasidone
	B.   Lamotrigine
	C.  Cariprazine
	D.   Quetiapine

### Slide 94
Question 4

All of the following are true of lithium except
	A.  Efficacy for acute bipolar depression
	B.   Strong antisuicide effect
	C.  Neuroprotective effects - increases cortical gray matter that is depleted in bipolar disorder
	D.  More likely to be maintained as a monotherapy in bipolar patients than any other medication

### Slide 95
Question 5

The International Society for Bipolar Disorders Task Force on Antidepressant Use in Bipolar Disorder recommended (Am J Psychiatry, November, 2013) all of the following except
	A. Avoid antidepressants if any history of antidepressant-emergent hypomania, mixed states, or mania
	B.  Avoid antidepressants if present or past history of rapid cycling
	C. Avoid antidepressants during any mixed states or if history of predominant mixed states in the past
	D. OK to use antidepressants in bipolar I depression only if a past history of positive response (but never as monotherapy)
	E.  OK to use antidepressants as a monotherapy in bipolar II depression if there are no more than 2 manic symptoms

	3. D
	4. A

### Slide 1
Brandon c. yarns, md, ms1, and David l. Sultzer, MD1,2



1VA Greater Los Angeles Healthcare System 
Department of psychiatry and BIObehavioral sciences,
David geffen school of medicine AT ucla

2DEPARTMENT OF psychiatry and human behavior
Institute for memory impairments and neurological disorders
UC irvine

2020 version

Psychosis and schizophrenia in later life

### Slide 2
Things to know about these slide presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated in: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 9/18/2020.

### Slide 3
Self-Assessment Question 1Which of the following is true regarding early- and late-onset schizophrenia?

Late-onset schizophrenia typically requires much higher doses of antipsychotics to treat compared to early-onset schizophrenia.
Male gender is over-represented among patients with late-onset schizophrenia compared to early-onset schizophrenia.
Positive symptoms in late-onset schizophrenia are much more prevalent than in early-onset schizophrenia.
Rate of age-related cognitive decline in late-onset schizophrenia does not differ from that in normal subjects.
Remission of schizophrenia in late life is wholly dependent on age and chronicity of illness.

### Slide 4
Self-Assessment Question 2Which of the following is true regarding treatment of older adults with schizophrenia?

Antipsychotics should always be tapered to discontinuation in patients with late-onset schizophrenia.
Cognitive Behavioral Social Skills Training has been shown to improve cognitive insight and socialization in older people with schizophrenia.
Cumulative incidence of tardive dyskinesia with conventional antipsychotics is low in elderly patients.
Nonpharmacologic treatments should be avoided in late-onset schizophrenia.
Risperidone has been shown to be superior to olanzapine in treating positive and negative symptoms in older adults with schizophrenia.

### Slide 5
Self-Assessment Question 3Which of the following is true regarding adverse effects of antipsychotics in the elderly?

Adverse effects such as postural hypotension/falls, sedation, and anticholinergic toxicity are much lower in older adults compared to younger adults.
Antipsychotic polypharmacy never produces an increased risk of adverse effects.
Black box warnings regarding stroke and increased mortality do not apply to the use of antipsychotics in elderly patients with dementia.
Risperidone produces the greatest risk of weight gain in the elderly compared to other atypical antipsychotics. 
Typical antipsychotics carry a much greater risk of tardive dyskinesia in the elderly compared to atypical antipsychotics.

### Slide 6
Self-Assessment Question 4Which of the following statements is true Regarding psychosis in Alzheimer's disease?

Alzheimer's disease patients with psychosis are less likely to be institutionalized than patients without psychosis. 
Neurobiological correlates of psychosis in Alzheimer's disease show no overlap with brain areas involving memory. 
Psychosis in Alzheimer's disease is associated with increased agitation and aggression and more rapid cognitive decline.
Psychosis will occur in around 1% of patients with Alzheimer's disease. 
There is no association between greater cognitive impairment and psychosis in Alzheimer's disease.

### Slide 7
Self-Assessment Question 5Which of the following is true Regarding treatment of psychosis in DEMENTIA?

Antipsychotics should always be avoided in patients with dementia.
Studies show that antipsychotics have powerful effects for prevention of psychosis in dementia.
The ADAD trial showed that for Alzheimer's patients with psychosis, relapse rates were not at all higher in patients whose antipsychotics had been discontinued. 
Visual hallucinations in Parkinson's disease should always be treated aggressively with antipsychotics.
When considering antipsychotics for patients with dementia and psychosis, risk of adverse effects, including mortality, should be balanced with the level impairment from symptoms.

### Slide 8
Major Teaching points

As many as 23% of older adults may develop psychotic symptoms; most of these are due to incident dementia (40%) and other secondary causes (18%).
Treatment of psychosis in the elderly should be aimed at improving quality-of-life and allowing for the patient to remain in the least restrictive setting possible. 
In clinical practice, the substantial risks of antipsychotic use in the elderly should be considered in relation to the distress and impairment resulting from ongoing symptoms.

### Slide 9
Outline

Differential Diagnosis
Primary Psychoses:
Schizophrenia in Later Life
Schizoaffective Disorder & Delusional Disorder in Later Life
Psychosis Due to Mood Disorders in Later Life
Secondary Psychoses: 
Psychosis Due to Dementia
Psychosis Due to Other Medical Conditions and Medications
Delirium

### Slide 10
Differential diagnosis

First, determine whether psychosis is primary or secondary
Primary psychosis may be due to mood disorders (38% of incident psychosis in later life) or schizophrenia-spectrum disorders (3% of incident psychosis in later life)
Most cases of new-onset psychosis in later life are secondary to dementia (40% of incident psychosis in later life), followed by other medical illness/drugs/alcohol/toxins (11%), and psychosis due to delirium (7%)

Holroyd 1999
Manepalli 2007

### Slide 11
Schizophrenia in later life

Prevalence in older adults is estimated at 0.1-0.5%, compared to 1% in younger adults
Persons with schizophrenia are living longer, with 25% of those in developed countries expected to be 55 years and older by 2025
The International Late-Onset Schizophrenia Group proposed subdividing schizophrenia into 3 groups based on age of onset:
Early-onset schizophrenia (EOS) -  before 40 years old
Late-onset schizophrenia (LOS) - between 40-60 years old
Very-late-onset schizophrenia-like psychosis (VLOS) - after 60 years old

Castle 1993
Copeland 1998
Cohen 2008
Howard 2000

### Slide 12
Age of schizophrenia onset, by gender

Lindamer 1997

### Slide 13
Early-onset Schizophrenia (EOS) in later life

Comprises the majority (75-80%) of cases of schizophrenia in later life
Older adults with EOS & the "paradox of aging":
Early/magnified physical comorbidity/mortality with accelerated physiologic aging, and ...
Initial incidence of neurocognitive disorder at illness-onset but with similar rate of cognitive decline compared healthy peers, resulting in overall poorer cognition, but ...
Improved psychosocial functioning, well-being, and self-management with diminished psychosis and fewer hospitalizations compared to younger persons with EOS.

Harris 1988
Jeste 2011

### Slide 14
Late-onset Schizophrenia (LOS)

Different characteristics of LOS compared to EOS in later life:
More likely to be female,
Lower burden of positive symptoms,
Increased organization of delusions,
More persecutory-type delusions,
May require lower doses of antipsychotics,
BUT equal family history and childhood history of maladjustment with EOS.
Cognition similar to patients with EOS.
Persons with LOS also have higher rates of marital and occupational success compared to EOS.

Palmer 2001
Vahia 2010
Rajji 2008

### Slide 15
VERy-Late-onset Schizophrenia-like psychosis (VLOS)

Incidence is low: 7.5 per 100,000 person years at risk
"Schizophrenia-like" because of higher rates of brain abnormalities compared to EOS and LOS, suggestive of a neurodegenerative condition.
VLOS is further differentiated from EOS and LOS by the following:
MUCH more likely to be female,
Increased partition and persecutory delusions,
Higher incidence of visual, tactile, and olfactory hallucinations,
Typically, an absence of negative symptoms and thought disorders,
PLUS less family history and history of childhood maladjustment.

Stafford 2017
Howard 2000

### Slide 16
treatment principles for schizophrenia later in life

The greatest benefits are likely from a combination of pharmacotherapy and psychosocial treatments.
If starting a new antipsychotic, initial dosing should be approximately half the normal adult starting dose and increased gradually to the minimally effective dose.
For patients on long-term antipsychotics, taper and even complete discontinuation of antipsychotics in older adults should be attempted at regular intervals to limit associated morbidity and mortality.
Typical antipsychotics should be avoided in older adults, given the substantially increased risk of newly incident tardive dyskinesia compared to atypicals.

Jeste 1999
Lehman 2004
Takeuchi 2013

### Slide 17
Schizophrenia in Older AdultsAntipsychotic Treatment

Very few double-blind RCT's in older adults (Krause 2018)

Paliperidone ER vs. placebo (Tzimos 2008)
n=114;   No between group differences for efficacy or tolerability
Olanzapine vs. haloperidol (Kennedy 2003)
n=117;  OLZ mean dose 12mg, HAL 9mg; Response: OLZ 51%, HAL 29%
OLZ: fewer motor side effects
Olanzapine vs. risperidone (Jeste 2003)
n=174;  OLZ mean dose 11.1mg, RIS 1.9mg;  Response: OLZ 59%, RIS 58%
Adverse events: OLZ 16%, RIS 9%
Improvement on some cognitive measures in both groups
Crossover: traditional antipsychotic to OLZ or RIS (Ritchie 2003)
n=66, open; iImproved symptoms and parkinsonism in both groups
No between group differences

### Slide 18
Risperidone vs Olanzapine in late life Schizophrenia

Largest RCT to date.  International, double-blind, 8-week RCT
176 patients, aged >60 years with schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder
Randomly assigned to flexible doses of Risperidone (1-3; median 2 mg/d) or Olanzapine (5-20; median 10 mg/d)
Both antipsychotics produced significant improvement from baseline scores on PANSS
No significant difference between the two drugs on psychopathology ratings, cognitive skills, QTc, EPS  reports, or anticholinergic side effect reports
Greater weight gain with olanzapine (p=.05)

Jeste 2003

### Slide 19
Recommended Dosagesin Older Patients with Schizophrenia

*Clozapine, though gold-standard for treatment-resistant schizophrenia, is infrequently prescribed due to side effect limitations (hypotension, anticholinergic effects, agranulocytosis).

*Long-acting injectable antipsychotic drugs are generally avoided in older adults with psychosis due to side effect and titration concerns.  However, a recent study of hospitalized elderly patients with schizophrenia suggested that long-acting drugs may reduce subsequent rehospitalizations.

Alexopoulos 2004, Rothenberg 2014, Lin 2020

### Slide 20
Side Effects of Atypical Antipsychotics in OLDER Patients

Adverse effects are more common in older adults, and risk increases with higher doses:
Sedation/somnolence
Anticholinergic toxicity
Postural hypotension and falls
Extrapyramidal symptoms and gait abnormality
Tardive dyskinesia
FDA Warnings:
In all age groups: weight gain, diabetes, hyperlipidemia
In dementia patients: strokes, mortality

Jeste 1999

### Slide 21
TD Incidence in Older Patients:Haloperidol versus Risperidone (1mg/d)

Jeste et al., JAGS, 1999

### Slide 22
Psychosocial THERAPIES- Late-Life Schizophrenia

Cognitive Behavior Social Skills Training - CBSST 
>45yo with schizophrenia or schizoaffective dx
Helping Older People Experience Success - HOPES  
>50yo with serious mental illness, more than half with schizophrenia or schizoaffective dx
Functional Adaptation Skills Training - FAST
>40yo with schizophrenia or schizoaffective dx

All demonstrated benefit  
None specifically studied late-onset schizophrenia

Granholm 2005
Meuser 2010
Patterson 2006

### Slide 23
Remission of Schizophrenia

Symptomatic remission defined and adapted for later life schizophrenia from the 2005 Remission in Schizophrenia Working Group
Previously met DSM-IV criteria for schizophrenia
No hospitalization for 1 year
Score of 3 or less on 8 items of PANSS
About 49% remission rate in those older than 55 with schizophrenia onset before 45yo, consistent with rates reported in younger adults with schizophrenia

Bankole 2008

### Slide 24
Remission of schizophrenia

Remission may lead to 'Recovery' (or persistent symptomatic remission) in a smaller proportion of older persons with schizophrenia, about 8%. 
Remitted patients had somewhat impaired cognition & functioning, suggesting that remission in schizophrenia may reflect a return to pre-morbid functioning, rather than to the level of those without schizophrenia.
Potentially remediable factors (social networks, lifetime trauma) were associated with remission, supporting the opportunity for greater levels of remission.

Bankole 2008

### Slide 25
Predictors of Sustained Remission

Greater social support
Greater cognitive / personality reserve
Early initiation of treatment 
NOT age or duration of illness

Owens 2010

### Slide 26
Schizoaffective disorder in later life

12-month prevalence of schizoaffective disorder in adults over age 60 years is 0.14%

Epidemiologically and clinically, more like schizophrenia than bipolar disorder in later life: similar rates of mortality, negative symptoms, clinical global impression of impairment, likelihood of marriage, and independent living as schizophrenia

Compared to younger adults with schizoaffective disorder, greater severity of illness, more treatment resistance, and higher risk of suicide

Rolin 2017
Meesters 2012
Post 1971
Gupta 2007

### Slide 27
Delusional disorder

Mean age of onset is 40

Mean age in a large sample was 55 years old 

Delusions are more circumscribed than in schizophrenia

Higher frequency of somatic/hypochondriasis and nihilistic delusions than younger adults

Only case reports for delusional disorder in the elderly

dePortugal 2008

### Slide 28
Psychosis in mood disorders

Psychotic features in major depressive disorder are associated with a worse prognosis than depression without psychosis.

An antidepressant plus an atypical antipsychotic is currently the first-line recommendation for geriatric psychotic depression.

Geriatric depressed patients with psychotic symptoms have higher response rates to ECT than do geriatric depressed patients without psychosis.

High placebo response seen in geriatric studies may be explained by the social support and nonspecific therapeutic factors offered by study staff.

Oudega 2014
Wijkstra 2015
Alexopoulos 2011
Alexopoulos 2001

### Slide 29
Alzheimer (Alzheimer's disease) and Kraepelin (schizophrenia) were buddies in the early 20th century.

They viewed psychiatric symptoms in the two disorders as consequences of specific neuropathologies.

Psychosis in Alzheimer's Disease

### Slide 30
Neuropsychiatric symptoms  in NEUROCOGNITIVE DISORDERS

Includes apathy, depression, irritability, agitation/aggression, and psychosis
80-100% of people with Alzheimer's disease will develop a neuropsychiatric symptom

Psychosis, in the form of delusions and hallucinations, occurs in 30% - 50% of AD patients
Psychosis is more prevalent in advanced AD
Psychosis is associated with agitated and aggressive behaviors

Geda 2013
Lyketsos 2000
Craig 2005

### Slide 31
Psychosis of AD: Public Health Significance

1) High incidence and prevalence
2) May be chronic or recurrent
3) Contributes to agitation and aggression
4) More rapid cognitive decline, and possibly death
5) Greater caregiver distress
6) Earlier institutionalization
7) Higher cost of care

Song 2018
Feast 2016
Maust 2017

### Slide 32
NEUROBIOLOGY OF PSYCHOSIS IN AD

Neuropathology, genetic, and 	neuroimaging  studies show links 	between regional neurobiology and 	psychosis and agitation in AD
  An FDG-PET neuroimaging example:
  n=88, probable AD
  Mean MMSE 19.3
  Delusions (n=28) vs. No delusions (n=60)
  Results unchanged when  controlled for               	overall cognitive deficits
  Some overlap with memory, both clinical and 	neurobiological
  Results indicate that psychosis and 	agitation are fundamental expressions 	of the neurodegenerative process

Sultzer 2014
Weissberger 2017
Victoroff 2018

Brain regions where metabolic activity is lower in AD patients with delusions

### Slide 33
MANAGEMENT CONSIDERATIONSPSYCHOSIS AND AGITATION IN AD

Behavioral disturbances that accompany psychosis should first be managed non-pharmacologically whenever possible.  
A thorough review of medications, both prescribed and OTC, can identify psychotropic agents that may inadvertently exacerbate neuropsychiatric symptoms.  
Medical conditions such as pain, dehydration, urinary retention, and delirium can contribute to behavioral disturbance and warrant attention.  
Environmental circumstances that foster neuropsychiatric symptoms or disruptive behaviors should be addressed.

### Slide 34
Psychosocial Interventions for Behavioral Disturbances in Dementia(Behaviors That May Accompany Psychosis)

Psychosocial/behavioral interventions are first-line treatments, before or along with pharmacotherapy.
The majority of randomized controlled trials were small in size and methods were inconsistent across trials.
Interventions can be targeted to the person with dementia, the caregiver, and/or the environment.
Need to tailor psychosocial interventions to the individual symptoms and circumstances  
The evidence best supports caregiver-focused interventions:
Education
Problem-solving
Support

Kales 2018
Livingston 2005

### Slide 35
ANTIPSYCHOTIC MedicationS DEMENTIA WITH PSYCHOSIS OR AGITATION

No drug, antipsychotic or other class, is approved currently for treatment of psychosis or agitation in Alzheimer's disease.
Off-label use of drugs is not uncommon in practice, but requires clear justification in individual patients.
Atypical antipsychotics show benefit over placebo in some trials for treatment of psychosis, agitation, aggression, and overall behavioral problems in patients with AD.  
The magnitude of benefit is modest, on average, and the placebo response rate is high.
In controlled trials, the effect size for improvement in psychosis was smaller than for agitation, suggesting that psychosis in dementia is paradoxically a poor prognostic factor for antipsychotic treatment. 
Efficacy of low-dose quetiapine for agitation or psychosis in AD was not apparent in most controlled trials.

Yunusa 2020
Maher  2011
Schneider 2006
Herrmann 2007

### Slide 36
Atypical Antipsychotic EfficacyFOR PSYCHOSIS IN AD

Aripiprazole

Olanzapine

Quetiapine

Risperidone

Overall
Effect Size=.11

Maher  2011

### Slide 37
Atypical Antipsychotics in DementiaDosage and Short-term Adverse Effects

* data predominantly from younger patients

### Slide 38
Double-blind, placebo-controlled trial of 421 outpatients with Alzheimer's disease and psychosis, aggression, or agitation.  
Randomly assigned to receive flexible dosing of olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone, or placebo for up to  36 weeks.  The initially assigned medication could be switched to an alternative treatment for insufficient efficacy, adverse effects, or other reasons.  
Time to discontinuation for any reason was the primary outcome.  There was no difference between antipsychotic and placebo treatment groups.  
An analysis of clinical symptoms found that those treated initially with olanzapine or risperidone had improved more than those treated with placebo on global behavioral measures.  
The magnitude of improvement was greatest on the BPRS hostile suspiciousness factor, a measure of anger, aggression, and paranoia.  
On the BPRS psychosis factor, only risperidone showed benefit compared to placebo.

Schneider 2006
Sultzer 2008

CATIE-AD  StudyATYPICAL ANTIPSYCHOTIC EFFECTIVENESS

### Slide 39
CATIE-AD  StudyIMPROVEMENT ON BPRS Psychosis Factor Score

-0.8

-0.7

-0.6

-0.5

-0.4

-0.3

-0.2

-0.1

0

Olanzapine

Quetiapine

Risperidone

Placebo

Score Change

*

Overall treatment group comparison, last observation in Phase 1, ANCOVA: p=.078
LS Pairwise vs placebo:   *p<.05

Significant benefit for psychosis in AD: risperidone only

Sultzer 2008

### Slide 40
Antipsychotic Discontinuation IN ADADAD TRIAL

Phase A 		Phase B
Open Treatment 	Randomized Trial
16 weeks 	             16 weeks 	     16 weeks
	         I---Risperidone---I---Risperidone---I
	
I-Risperidone--I   I---Risperidone---I------Placebo-----I

	         I------Placebo------I------Placebo-----I

N=180
 Mean dose 0.97mg/d
 110 responders randomized
 Relapse, first 16 weeks:
 60% P, 33% Risperidone
 Relapse, second 16 weeks:
 48% P, 15% Risperidone
 Adverse effects: No substantial difference

Devanand 2012

Among those with known behavioral symptoms that responded to initial risperidone treatment, the relapse rate was 60% when crossed over to placebo vs 33% when continued on risperidone for 4 months

### Slide 41
Summary and ImplicationsANTIPSYCHOTICS FOR PSYCHOSIS AND AGITATION/AGGRESSION IN AD

Benefits are modest
Mean improvement occurs over weeks
Doses are low, but not always micro
Effect size is small, on average
Some individuals benefit more, some less
The meaning and value of a given change in symptoms is quite variable across patients
Specific symptoms matter
Presence of psychosis does not predict behavioral benefit
Patients with advanced dementia can benefit
Hostile, paranoid, aggressive behaviors may preferentially respond

### Slide 42
Parkinson's Disease with Psychosis

30-40% of those with PD have delusions or hallucinations
Visual hallucinations are most common
50% have "presence" or "passage" visual hallucinations
Multiple etiologies
Possible delirium
Dopaminergic drugs likely contribute
Clinical approach
Review medical circumstances and medications
Consider discontinuing anticholinergic drugs
Reduce antiparkinsonism med doses to the lowest effective dose
Can improve psychosis but often exacerbates motor signs

Sultzer and Ross 2007
Ffytche 2017

### Slide 43
Parkinson's Disease With PsychosisMedication Management

Quetiapine
Clinical experience: usual dose 50-75mg/day
Limited support for efficacy in controlled trials
Lower response and more motor decline, if dementia
Risperidone and olanzapine
EPS - High risk for exacerbating tremor, rigidity, or bradykinesia 
Clozapine
Data from controlled trials, but impractical in usual clinical care
Start 6.25mg/day, go slow, usual dose 20-40mg/day
Monitor WBC

Desmarais 2016

### Slide 44
Parkinson's Disease psychosis - pimavanserin

Pimavanserin is a 5HT2A inverse agonist
6-week placebo-controlled study
SAPS-PD: 9 items, delusions/hallucinations
Responders on Clinical Global Impression
49%  Pimavanserin
26%  Placebo
Adverse events
Edema 7%
Fall 11%
Hallucination 7%
 QTc
Usual dose: 34 mg/day
Studies in those with psychosis due to other neurocognitive disorders are ongoing

Cummings 2014
Combs 2017

### Slide 45
DEMENTIA WITH LEWY BODIES

5-10% of major neurocognitive disorders (dementias)
Core clinical criteria:
Fluctuating cognition
Recurrent visual hallucinations
Occur in up to 80% of those with DLB
Usually well-formed images - people, children, animals
REM sleep behavior disorder
Parkinsonism
Management of hallucinations
Cholinesterase inhibitors may be helpful
Treatment approach is similar to Parkinson's disease with psychosis
Uncertain efficacy and common side effects
Med doses are relatively low

McKeith 2017

### Slide 46
Side Effects Atypical Antipsychotics in Elderly Patients

Adverse effects are more common in the elderly and risk increases with higher doses
Sedation/somnolence
Extrapyramidal symptoms and gait abnormality
Edema
Metabolic syndrome, including weight gain
Seen in studies of Alzheimer's disease with agitation/psychosis
Tardive dyskinesia
Other adverse effects in those with dementia:
Cognitive decline
Cerebrovascular events
Mortality

FDA Warnings (in Dementia)

### Slide 47
Mortality IN DEMENTIAAntipsychotics

Maust 2015
Pharmacy database, n= 90,000
Diagnosis of dementia and new start of an antipsychotic med
6-month mortality, compared to non-treated control
Increased mortality risk for antipsychotics
FDA review, 2005
Placebo-controlled trials of atypical antipsychotics in dementia
Treatment period: 10 to 12 weeks
Mortality rate with antipsychotics was 1.6 to 1.7x  placebo
Mortality rate: 4.5% antipsychotic, 2.6% placebo

### Slide 48
MEDS OTHER THAN ANTIPSYCHOTICSagitation in dementia

Cognitive enhancers
May reduce likelihood of behavioral symptoms emerging over time
No clear benefit for active behavioral problems
Citalopram (or possibly other SSRI's)
Benefit seen in some controlled trials
Dose-related QT interval changes  
Divalproex sodium
Little support for efficacy in controlled trials; side effects are common
Carbamazepine 
Drug interactions
Trazodone 
Some evidence for benefit at higher doses
Benzodiazepines (e.g. lorazepam)
Risk for falls and cognitive decline
Drugs with alternative mechanisms - in development

### Slide 49
IF MEDS ARE PRESCRIBED FOR PSYCHOSIS OR AGITATION IN DEMENTIA. . .

Address underlying causes first
Superimposed infections, complicating medications
Environmental and behavioral techniques in all cases
Lowest effective dose
Monitor closely for adverse effects
Regularly re-assess medication efficacy and safety
Prescribe no longer than necessary

Rabins 2010

### Slide 50
Secondary psychosis: Other Medical Conditions and Medications

Medications
Dopamine agonists, e.g., pramipexole
Other meds for parkinsonism or restless legs syndrome, e.g., pramiprexole, bromocriptine
Steroids, stimulants, anticholinergics, sedative/hynpotics
There is an increasing prevalence of substance use/abuse in the elderly.
Other medical conditions: CNS, other organs, metabolic
Primary focus is addressing the underlying cause
Medication treatment for psychosis is symptomatic and time-limited.

Martins 2012

### Slide 51
Secondary psychosis: DELIRIUM

Delirium is often prolonged or persistent in older adults.
Recovery to "baseline" can be up to 1 year post-hospitalization.
May reflect an emerging dementia syndrome
30% of patients with delirium experience delusions:
Usually paranoid or persecutory
Often with perceptual disturbances, frequently at night
Determine and treat the underlying cause.

Cole 2010

### Slide 52
Self-Assessment Question 1Which of the following is true regarding early- and late-onset schizophrenia?

Late-onset schizophrenia typically requires much higher doses of antipsychotics to treat compared to early-onset schizophrenia.
Male gender is over-represented among patients with late-onset schizophrenia compared to early-onset schizophrenia.
Positive symptoms in late-onset schizophrenia are much more prevalent than in early-onset schizophrenia.
Rate of age-related cognitive decline in late-onset schizophrenia does not differ from that in normal subjects.
Remission of schizophrenia in late life is wholly dependent on age and chronicity of illness.

### Slide 53
Self-Assessment Question 2Which of the following is true regarding treatment of older adults with schizophrenia?

Antipsychotics should always be tapered to discontinuation in patients with late-onset schizophrenia.
Cognitive Behavioral Social Skills Training has been shown to improve cognitive insight and socialization in older people with schizophrenia.
Cumulative incidence of tardive dyskinesia with conventional antipsychotics is low in elderly patients.
Nonpharmacologic treatments should be avoided in late-onset schizophrenia.
Risperidone has been shown to be superior to olanzapine in treating positive and negative symptoms in older adults with schizophrenia.

### Slide 54
Self-Assessment Question 3Which of the following is true regarding adverse effects of antipsychotics in the elderly?

Adverse effects such as postural hypotension/falls, sedation, and anticholinergic toxicity are much lower in older adults compared to younger adults.
Antipsychotic polypharmacy never produces an increased risk of adverse effects.
Black box warnings regarding stroke and increased mortality do not apply to the use of antipsychotics in elderly patients with dementia.
Risperidone produces the greatest risk of weight gain in the elderly compared to other atypical antipsychotics. 
Typical antipsychotics carry a much greater risk of tardive dyskinesia in the elderly compared to atypical antipsychotics.

### Slide 55
Self-Assessment Question 4Which of the following statements is true Regarding psychosis in Alzheimer's disease?

Alzheimer's disease patients with psychosis are less likely to be institutionalized than patients without psychosis. 
Neurobiological correlates of psychosis in Alzheimer's disease show no overlap with brain areas involving memory. 
Psychosis in Alzheimer's disease is associated with increased agitation and aggression and more rapid cognitive decline.
Psychosis will occur in around 1% of patients with Alzheimer's disease. 
There is no association between greater cognitive impairment and psychosis in Alzheimer's disease.

### Slide 56
Self-Assessment Question 5Which of the following is true Regarding treatment of psychosis in DEMENTIA?

Antipsychotics should always be avoided in patients with dementia.
Studies show that antipsychotics have powerful effects for prevention of psychosis in dementia.
The ADAD trial showed that for Alzheimer's patients with psychosis, relapse rates were not at all higher in patients whose antipsychotics had been discontinued. 
Visual hallucinations in Parkinson's disease should always be treated aggressively with antipsychotics.
When considering antipsychotics for patients with dementia and psychosis, risk of adverse effects, including mortality, should be balanced with the level impairment from symptoms.

### Slide 57
Answers to Self-Assessment Questions

D
B
E
C
E

Preferred medications to try first for a first episode of schizophrenia include all but
	A.  Quetiapine
	B.  Risperidone
	C.  Ziprasidone
	D.  Aripiprazole

Question 2

2. Medications you could consider for your second antipsychotic trial include all but
	A.  Olanzapine
	B.  Clozapine
	C.  Risperidone
	D.  Perphenazine
   E.  A, B, C, and D are all possible choices

	A.  Risperidone

In this algorithm, the primary role of long-acting injectable antipsychotics is
	A.  Facilitating an adequate trial of a pre-clozapine antipsychotic in non-adherent patients
	B.  Improving long term adherence to antipsychotics
	C.  To improve efficacy in treatment-resistant patients

Question 5

After non-response to an adequate trial of clozapine, an evidence-supported promising option might be
	A. Switch to aripiprazole
