	D.  High dose olanzapine
      E.  None of the above

### Slide 12
12

* Overview of Lecture

Introduction and Goals of Treatment
Principles
Methods - how are these algorithms updated? 	
Adherence and the role of LAIs
Comorbidity and other features in schizophrenia and how they affect the standard algorithm
Review of nodes 1-5

### Slide 13
Introduction

Algorithm addresses positive symptoms of schizophrenia, consistent with DSM-5 which now requires positive symptoms in A criterion.
Primary negative and cognitive symptoms have few proven treatments
Secondary negative symptoms improve when extrapyramidal symptoms and sedation are managed successfully.

### Slide 14
Goals of Treatment

Recovery and normalized activity are the goals of antipsychotic trials. NOT JUST avoidance of hospitalization.
Response short of this should be considered unsatisfactory. 
If response is unsatisfactory, review diagnosis, psychosocial factors, and investigate behavioral toxicity

### Slide 15
Methods: How Do We Update Algorithms? - 1

Authors subscribe to the main psychopharm journals* and monitor for new RCTs,  effectiveness trials, reviews, guidelines, meta-analyses
We get Journal Watch Psychiatry, a publication of the Massachusetts Medical Society, reviews 25 articles monthly from more diverse sources
*Am J Psychiatry, JAMA Psychiatry, J Clin Psychopharmacol, J Clin Psych

Ps15

### Slide 16
How Do We Update Algorithms? - 2

New articles are sorted for relevance to first-onset, typical, and treatment-resistant patients
Our revised algorithms are qualitative, opinion-based analyses of this body of new information
When we submit for publication, the peer review process adds necessary modification and validation of the final reasoning

16

### Slide 17
17

### Slide 18
18

### Slide 19
19

Preliminary Node:  Consider Comorbidity and Other Features That May Affect the Algorithm

### Slide 20
Brief Overview of Comorbidities - 1

Suicidality: Use clozapine earlier
Aggressive behavior: clozapine > olanzapine > others
Primary negative symptoms: antidepressant
Depression: antidepressants if psychosis remitted
Agitation: best IM = haloperidol + lorazepam or other benzodiazepine

### Slide 21
IM Treatment of Agitation in ERMantovani C et al.  JCPsychopharmacol 2013;33:306-12

This is the largest and best controlled study but it confirms earlier smaller studies
100 patients with schizophrenia mostly, 36 with mania, randomly assigned to haloperidol 2.5 plus midazolam 7.5; haloperidol 2.5 plus promethazine 25; olanzapine 10; or ziprasidone 10.
In 60 minutes, efficacy was best was H + M or O, but odds ratio for side effects was 1.6x greater with olanzapine and 3.6x greater with H + P.
A recent study found midazolam 5 mg IM to be better than 4 other treatments in an ER setting. (Klein et al. Ann Emerg Med 2018; 72:374-85)

### Slide 22
Brief Overview of Comorbidities - 2

Pregnancy: try to use 1 drug, avoid LAIs 
Active drug abuse: all medications are less effective
QTc increase: avoid ziprasidone, amisulpride, iloperidone: best aripiprazole, lurasidone, paliperidone, cariprazine (Kuhn.  Lancet 2019)
Older patients: greater risk of TD, EPS, metabolic syndrome

### Slide 23
23

The Algorithm's Recommended Medication Trials

### Slide 24
First Trial: For First Episode Patients

Preferred choices in 2013: amisulpride, aripiprazole, risperidone, ziprasidone 
Added lurasidone in 2017 (FDA approval for adolescents with schizophrenia)
Avoid olanzapine, clozapine, quetiapine 
Use minimum effective dose at first, then adjust based on response, tolerability, bioavailability
Maintain trial at least 4-6 weeks.
(Supporting evidence will be discussed shortly...)

### Slide 25
25

If unable to complete an adequate trial of the selected medication, choose another from the first trial options until an adequate trial is completed.

### Slide 26
Why These Choices                    for the First Trial?

You want to start your new-onset patient with an antipsychotic with a lower side effect risk.
Because: whatever you choose, it is going to probably be needed long-term and the patient has to live with these side effects.
Also: you want a medication that prevents relapse
Therefore, you should choose from among the medications with lowest long-term side effects and reasonable (not necessarily the best) acute efficacy, but with good maintenance efficacy

### Slide 27
First Trial: Supporting Data - I

Why not First Generation Antipsychotics (FGAs) for Node 1?  Multiple studies show that in early psychosis patients, Second Generation Antipsychotics (SGAs) produce fewer dropouts and longer maintenance in remission than FGAs 
Emsley 1999, Lieberman 2003, Schooler 2005, Green 2006, Kahn 2008

### Slide 28
First Trial: Supporting Data - 2

SGAs have lower incidence of EPS and, most importantly, Tardive Dyskinesia  (though there may be bias in these data - see refs from previous slide: Lieberman, Tollefson 2003; Correll 2004; Gharabawi 2006; Novick 2010)
FGAs require more adjunctive anticholinergics which have adverse cognitive and peripheral side effects. (Minzenberg 2004)

### Slide 29
First Trial: Supporting Data - 3

An important study is Robinson DG et al 2015.
198 acutely psychotic patients (no more than 2 weeks exposure to antipsychotics) randomized to aripiprazole (A) or risperidone (R)
Positive symptoms:  A = 63% response, R = 57% 
Avolition/Apathy: A > R, p<0.03.
Metabolic side effects favored aripiprazole
Akathisia was less common with risperidone
Authors propose that aripiprazole is first-choice

### Slide 30
First Trial: Supporting Data - 4

Ziprasidone has the lowest weight gain and metabolic consequences - a strong positive.  It is slightly less effective than other SGAs (Komossa  2009) but this may be due to suboptimal dosing protocols (low dose, lack of adequate food intake).  Was equal to olanzapine in early-onset patients in one RCT.(Gootens 2011)
Amisulpride (not available in US) has good efficacy and low weight gain. (Komossa 2010)

### Slide 31
First Trial: Supporting Evidence - 5

Crespo-Facorro et al 2013: a randomized, flexible dose open label comparison of aripiprazole, ziprasidone, and quetiapine.
202 first-episode patients treated for 6 weeks
All cause discontinuation results:
Aripiprazole  20 mg - 15%
Ziprasidone  101 mg - 19%
Quetiapine 414 mg - 35%

### Slide 32
First Trial: Supporting Data - 6

Olanzapine is not recommended as a first-line agent due to severe weight/metabolic effects.  Even ONE DOSE causes insulin resistance and other endocrine/inflammatory dysfunction (Hahn 2013)
Quetiapine is not recommended.  Weight gain second only to olanzapine in the CAFE study of early psychosis (Patel 2009). In meta-analyses and a Finnish registry study quetiapine produces among the shortest times to rehospitalization (Kreyenbuhl 2011, Tiihonen J. JAMA Psychiatry 2017) after a first admission (Taipale 2017).

### Slide 33
Second Antipsychotic Trial

Consider risperidone, olanzapine, amisulpride, or an FGA*
If one of these had an adequate trial in Trial One, you can select any SGA or FGA for the Second Trial 
*Kuhn M et al.  Lancet 2019; 394:939-51

### Slide 34
Why These ChoicesFor the Second Trial?

Patient has already failed one adequate trial.  
For the second adequate trial, effectiveness is more important than side effects.  So pick from among those most effective

### Slide 35
Second Trial: Supporting Data

Meta-analyses (e.g., Leucht 2013, Kuhn 2019) of primarily industry-sponsored studies found small differences in efficacy among antipsychotics 
But, study populations are atypical
Important "Effectiveness Studies" in more "real-world" populations amplify those differences: risperidone, olanzapine, and FGAs seem to work better.

### Slide 36
2nd Trial:  Supporting EvidenceMcCue RE et al.  Br J Psychiatry 2006;189:433-440

327 acute inpatients randomized open-label to
   haloperidol 16 mg (mean maximum dose) 
    aripiprazole 22 mg
    olanzapine 19 mg
    quetiapine 650 mg
    risperidone 5.2 mg
    ziprasidone 150 mg.  
Response = % discharged in 3 weeks
Olanzapine 92%, haloperidol 89%, risperidone 88%, aripiprazole 64%, quetiapine 63%, ziprasidone 64%

### Slide 37
2nd Trial: Supporting Evidence - 2Suzuki T et al.  Psychopharmacology 2007;195:285-95Hatta K et al.  Schizophrenia Research 2009;113:49-55

78 patients randomized to olanzapine 18 mg, risperidone 5.5 mg, or quetiapine 560 mg, for up to 8 weeks.  
If no response (30% drop in PANSS) they were randomized to one of the other two for up to 8 more weeks. If still no response, they were given the third.  Results: 
Trial 1 - O=62%, R=54%, Q=35%. Q did less well. 
Trial 2 - 60% responded to quetiapine at the 2nd trial.  
Trial 3 - Only 2 patients responded to the third SGA.

### Slide 38
38

If unable to complete an adequate trial of the selected medication, choose another from the second trial options until an adequate trial is completed.

### Slide 39
A Very Common Problem Preventing Adequate First and Second Trials: Poor Adherence

Usually patients are treated with oral medication but non-adherence may be a reason for failure
May need to use long-acting injectables (LAIs) to ensure adequate trials
LAIs associated with good prevention of the next episode in observational studies (e.g., Taipale et al 2017) but selection bias may explain the data. 
i.e., patients who agree to an LAI after first or second episode may be patients who have better prognosis

39

### Slide 40
40

Role of LAIs vs. Oral SGAs

Mixed results in comparison studies 
California Medicaid (2007): see next 2 slides
Rosenheck et al study of Veterans (2011) found no LAI advantage in relapse prevention
Tiihonen (2011) found LAI decreased admission
"PROACTIVE" NIMH trial (2012): no LAI advantage in relapse, but better for psychotic symptoms throughout trial
Focus on mirror-image studies: LAIs better (Kishimoto 2013)
In Finland, fewer hospitalizations with LAIs compared with orals EXCEPT compared to CLOZAPINE  (Tiihonen 2017)

### Slide 41
"Real World" Comparison of First Generation LAIs and Risperidone LAI*

Observational study of indigent California Medicaid patients with schizophrenia initiated on one of the three LAIs in 2003-4.  N=2,695 patients
Most were taking < 80% of their oral medication in the 6 months prior to LAI. (mean: 60%) 
2/3 were on more than one oral antipsychotic and about half were on concomitant mood stabilizers, antidepressants, and anxiolytics.
      *Olfson M et al.  Schizophrenia Bull 2007;33(6):1379-87

### Slide 42
Olfson Results: Comparison of LAIs

Very few of these treatment-resistant, partly-compliant Medicaid patients stayed on their LAI for six months:
Haloperidol Decanoate:        9.7%
Fluphenazine Decanoate:     5.4%
Risperidone LAI:                  2.6%  (p<0.0001)
Speculate: LAIs more helpful in more routine, less treatment-resistant patients
Or, maybe structure helps: "LAI Clinics"

### Slide 43
Role of LAIs: Conclusions

LAI can enable you to give an adequate trial to determine effectiveness in the 1st or 2nd trial in a non-adherent patient. If not effective: clozapine
LAIs are no better than oral and ineffective for outpatients with inadequate structure or no family members to encourage adherence 
Use LAIs for patients who are excellent responders to oral but not very adherent. 
(Journal reviewers praised these recommendations for the role of LAIs.)

### Slide 44
Role of Newer SGAs: not in algorithm

Iloperidone (Fanapt) was rejected by FDA in July 2008 due to lack of efficacy.  Decision reversed in 2009.  Equivalent to ziprasidone in one study. Weight gain, QTc
Asenapine (Saphris) approved in Aug. 2009. Sublingual administration: practical? One negative and two positive published studies. Unpublished negative studies.
Paliperidone (Invega), metabolite of risperidone found abundantly in patients given risperidone. Not more effective than risperidone

### Slide 45
Why Not Combinations at 2nd Trial? Consider this: Kane et al.  J Clin Psychiatry 2009;70(10):1348-57

323 patients with unsatisfactory response to risperidone (4-8 mg) or quetiapine (400-800 mg) were randomized to combine current Rx with aripiprazole 2-15 mg daily (mean: 10) or placebo for 16 weeks.
70% completed the study
78% schizophrenia
The results strongly suggest that combinations should not have an early role.

### Slide 47
Meta-analysis of Antipsychotic Combination Studies vs. Monotherapy

Authors:  Galling B et al, 2016  
21 DB and 10 open label studies. 
Symptom reduction vs. placebo:  none in the DB studies, some in the open label
"Response":  no group differences in either
Conclusions:  AP combination in schizophrenia not supported by evidence.  
Treatment should be several monotherapies including clozapine before trying combinations.

### Slide 48
Giving Adequate Trials of Selected Antipsychotics

Dosing, Administration, and 
Side Effect Management - see Crash Course lecture on Antipsychotics

### Slide 49
Adequate Trials: Important Role of Antipsychotic Plasma Levels*

Helps confirm adherence
Consider effects of P450 drug interactions
Aripiprazole has well-delineated optimal range of 150 to 210 ng/ml (Sparshatt, 2010)
Risperidone: 20-60 ng/ml (Parker, 2009)
Amisulpride: 100-300 ng/ml*
Olanzapine: 20-80 ng/ml*
Ziprasidone 50-200 ng/ml*
*Heimke C et al.  Pharmacopsychiatry 2018; 51(1-2) : 9-62

### Slide 50
Important Role of Plasma Levels 2

While "individualization" or "personalization" of treatment is a top priority on the research agenda of most psychiatric scientific societies, therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) is indeed the only clinically proven approach for personalized treatment in psychiatry. This is in sharp contrast to the fact that - although cheap and widely available -TDM is not systematically established in routine patient care. (Grunder 2018 - editorial)

Grunder, 2018 Pharmacopsychiary 2018: 51: 5-6

### Slide 51
Important Role of Plasma Levels 3

In a recent study* 99 patients with apparent antipsychotic treatment resistance being considered for clozapine.
35% had subtherapeutic levels
Of these, 34% were undetectable
Lower dose was associated with low levels
More likely to be readmitted (P = 0.02)
Consider adherence and pharmacokinetic factors
*McCutcheon R et al.  Acta Psychiatr Scand 2018

### Slide 53
Mean Change in PANSS Positive Symptoms

### Slide 54
CGI Outcome

### Slide 55
Conclusions on Aripiprazole Dose

15 mg is superior to 30 mg, at all data points and even after 1 week
Probably better to use than risperidone for First Episode patients (Robinson et al 2015); Dose 15 mg
There is no advantage to a "loading dose"
Results develop slowly compared to haloperidol 10 mg, but patience is rewarded. There is no advantage to raising dose.
Six-month relapse rates are somewhat higher than other antipsychotics (27%, compared to 15-19%)*
      *Pigott TA J Clin Psychiatry 2003;64:1048-1056

### Slide 56
Other Aripiprazole Issues

75 hour half life
Substrate for Cytochrome P450 3A4 and 2D6.  Paroxetine and fluoxetine will raise levels (use 50% dose), carbamazepine will lower them 50%.
8% of population are poor metabolizers of 2D6 and will get 60% higher levels.  So, some patients need only 5 mg
Advantage: aripiprazole, for patients at cardiac risk. It had the least QTc elev. of any antipsychotic (Chung et al, 2011)

### Slide 57
Aripiprazole Side Effects-1

Dizziness
Insomnia
Akathisia, agitation
Headache
Sedation
Metabolic syndrome - low risk
Hypoprolactinemia - New finding (Sogawa 2016).  Decreases sperm motility/quantity and women may not lactate post-partum.

### Slide 58
Aripiprazole Side Effects-2

Some patients develop irritability, insomnia, excitement, and nervousness. Also (2016 FDA warning) urges to gamble, shop, binge, have sex
Is this a dopamine agonist effect?
These side effects may occur more often if the patient was recently on a strong dopamine blocker like a FGA or risperidone.* Also, avoid adding DA stimulants such as bupropion.
*Raja M.  Int J Neuropsychopharmacol 2007;10:107-110.  Jong-Yih L.  J Clin Psychopharmacol 2009;29(1):93-5

### Slide 59
Risperidone: Dosing

4 mg to less than 6 mg daily for 3-6 weeks = best*
Doses of 6 mg or more - many more dropouts**
A dose that produces parkinsonian side effects is probably too high a dose
Acute exacerbation: 1 mg bid, then 2 mg bid 
First episode/first treatment:  0.5 mg bid, then 1 mg bid (maximum 2 to less than 4 mg daily)***
Elderly:  Similar to first episode, or less
P450 Drug Interactions: 2D6 substrate                     *Li C et al.  Cochrane Reviews 2009(4):art.No. CD007474
    **Yoshimura Y et al. J Clin Psychopharm 2017;37(3):296-301 
  ***Robinson DG et al.  Schizophrenia Bulletin 2015;41:1227-36

### Slide 60
Risperidone dosing and D2 receptor occupancy

In first-episode and drug free patients, risperidone at 6 mg per day produced EPS in almost everyone and dopamine D2 receptor occupancy averaging 82%*
At risperidone 3 mg, EPS were usually not present and the average D2 occupancy was 72%.*
Previous studies have shown that the optimal D2 occupancy level for maximizing benefits and minimizing EPS is 70-80%.
*Nyberg S et al, 1999

### Slide 61
Risperidone dosing - III

Chinese and other East Asian ethnic individuals (and many Africans) usually need somewhat lower doses of antipsychotics metabolized by 2D6, probably because 35-50% have a less active form of the 2D6 enzyme, rendering them "Slow Metabolizers" (SM's).
Poor metabolizers (PM's) are comparatively rare among Asians, being found in 1-6% compared to 5-10% in Caucasians.  They are very prone to EPS

### Slide 62
Risperidone Side Effects

Prolactin elevation, probably greater than with first-generation antipsychotics (FGAs). 
Maudsley Guidelines (2015) recommend monitoring baseline and 3-month prolactin levels with risperidone, paliperidone, and FGAs
Monitor for sexual dysfunction, galactorrhea, amenorrhea
Warn about long term osteoporosis
Young women especially vulnerable
Three recent studies support Maudsley Guidelines (Osser 2017)
Agitation. This can look like akathisia, or it may present as hypomania or mania. 
Anxiety, insomnia, headache and nausea. 
Weight gain and the metabolic syndrome - low to medium risk

### Slide 63
Ziprasidone - QTc Issues

Avoid ziprasidone if EKG shows QTc is >500 ms 
Is patient on medications that might prolong the QTc since EKG was done? (citalopram, haloperidol, tricyclics, quetiapine, floxacins.) If so, repeat EKG 
Check pulse.  Low pulse risks Torsades.  
Is the patient on a drug that lowers pulse? (Beta-blocker - often; SSRI - infrequently)
Risk for electrolyte problems?  (alc dependent, purging bulimic)  If so, get K+, Mg++ and follow
History of arrhythmias?  Get medical clearance.

### Slide 64
Dosing of Ziprasidone - 1

Only 80 mg bid was better than placebo in All Cause Discontinuation in meta-analysis of 4 studies.*
Citrome et al also found NO differences in discontinuation rates due to side effects at 160 mg vs 40 mg daily dose
 *Citrome L et al.  Schizophrenia Research 2009;111:39-45

### Slide 65
Dosing of Ziprasidone - 2

Absorption is reduced by at least 50% if not taken with food, more at higher doses. 500 kcal needed - not even 250 is enough.
Fat content not important. 
Gandelman et al. J Clin Psychiatry. 2009 Jan;70(1):58-62

### Slide 66
Third antipsychotic Trial: Clozapine

Strong, consistent data show clozapine more effective than FGAs or other SGAs in Treatment Resistant Schizophrenia (i.e., after 2 trials).*
It also reduces mortality and self-harm compared to other treatments (Wimberley et al 2017) 
Gradually remove other antipsychotics to complete a monotherapy trial (Meltzer 1992)
Clozapine also best for suicidal and assaultive patients (Meltzer 2003; Volavka 2004)
*Chakos 2001; McEvoy 2006; Lewis 2006; Buchanan 2009

### Slide 67
Trial 3: Clozapine.                 Give Adequate Trial

Plasma levels optimal above 350-450 - 600 ng/ml
Levels > 600 ng/ml associated with more toxicity
CYP1A2 inhibitor fluvoxamine: watch out!
Citalopram also raises levels - ? Mechanism
CYP1A2 inducer: cigarette smoke
Avoid benzodiazepines if possible - respiratory depression

### Slide 68
Trial 4: Clozapine Augmentations

Best-studied options: risperidone and lamotrigine - each with 5 placebo-controlled RCTs, and topiramate. (Tiihonen '09; Weiner '10; Correll '16). 
Risperidone = or > 4 mg may work better
Topiramate also produced ~ 3 kg weight loss
Meta-analysis found none > controls (Correll C 2017)
Also consider ECT.  One controlled study (clozapine alone as control - Petrides 2015)) and many case reports. (Havaki-Kontazaki 2006)   A sham-controlled trial found no efficacy.(Melzer-Ribeiro '17)
Bone Marrow Transplantation?? (Miyaoka T, 2017)

### Slide 69
Trial 5: What To Do Next?(You might get here if not able to use clozapine)

Try another of the Node 4 augmentations
Keep clozapine:  add memantine or O3FA
No clozapine: Try another antipsychotic monotherapy, e.g., aripiprazole (see* & next slide)
No clozapine: Try combinations of FGA + mirtazapine (Joffe et al 2009), or SGA + celecoxib
No clozapine:  Try antipsychotic combinations, but not ones that have negative evidence, i.e., aripiprazole plus quetiapine or risperidone
DeLucena 2009; Peet 2002; *Raja 2007, Jong-Yih 2009, Feeley RJ et al 2017; Muller 2010; Terevnikov 2013

### Slide 70
Aripiprazole in Treatment-Resistant Schizophrenia               Kane JM et al.  J Clin Psychiatry 2007;68:213-223

426 Treatment resistant patients.
Failed 2 or > AP trials of 6 weeks (one = 3 mo.). Then open trial of 3 weeks of olanzapine or risperidone.  Out, if 20% PANSS improvement
Only 2% improved 20%.  
300 patients randomized to aripiprazole (93% at 30 mg) or perphenazine (mean dose 39 mg - about double the CATIE dose) 
Response defined as 30% PANSS improvement

### Slide 72
Summary and Conclusions2020 Schizophrenia Algorithm

Consider comorbidity
First Trial/First Onset:  amisulpride, aripiprazole, lurasidone, risperidone, or ziprasidone
Second Adequate Trial: amisulpride, olanzapine, risperidone, or first-generation 
Clozapine, after two adequate trials above
Clozapine augmentations, other monotherapies, combinations - but prognosis is guarded at best

72

### Slide 73
Post-Lecture ExamQuestion 1

Preferred medications to try first for a first episode of schizophrenia include all but
	A.  Quetiapine
	B.  Risperidone
	C.  Ziprasidone
	D.  Aripiprazole

### Slide 74
Question 2

2. Medications you could consider for your second antipsychotic trial include all but
	A.  Olanzapine
	B.  Clozapine
	C.  Risperidone
	D.  Perphenazine
   E.  A, B, C, and D are all possible choices

### Slide 75
Question 3

3. Other than clozapine, the next best antipsychotic targeting hostile/aggressive behavior is
	A.  Risperidone
	B.  Olanzapine
	C.  Haloperidol
	D.  Quetiapine

### Slide 76
Question 4

In this algorithm, the primary role of long-acting injectable antipsychotics is
	A.  Facilitating an adequate trial of a pre-clozapine antipsychotic in non-adherent patients
	B.  Improving long term adherence to antipsychotics
	C.  To improve efficacy in treatment-resistant patients

### Slide 77
Question 5

After non-response to an adequate trial of clozapine, an evidence-supported promising option might be
	A. Switch to aripiprazole 
	B.  Aripiprazole plus quetiapine
	C.  Electroconvulsive therapy
	D.  High dose olanzapine
      E.  None of the above

### Slide 78
Answers to Questions

1. A
	2.  E
	3.  B
	4.  A
      5.  E

## Source: `03_Core_Topics/SUD_Withdrawal/_source/703 - Substance Abuse-The Nation's Number One Health Problem_tlc.pptx`

### Slide 1
The Robert Wood Johnson Foundation has identified Substance Abuse as the Nation's Number One Health Problem

### Slide 2
Things to Know About These Slide Presentations

The content is over-inclusive by design.
The number of slides can be condensed or modified to suit your program's needs.
The slides can be combined with other educational materials.
The slides were peer reviewed by academic faculty in psychiatry. 
This presentation was updated on: 2020.
This presentation was peer reviewed on: 12/30/2020.

### Slide 3
Opioid Pharmacology

OPIOID: Any chemical compound with pharmacologic actions similar to those of morphine. The term "narcotic analgesic" is often used to refer to opioids.
Sumerians (6000 years ago) called poppy "joy plants." Morphine and codeine are alkaloids of the poppy plant, obtained as opium, referred to as OPIATE.

### Slide 4
CNS: analgesia, euphoria (dysphoria), sedation, 
	respiratory depression, release of prolactin, 
	nausea, anti-tussive effect
C-V: peripheral vasodilatation
EYE: pupil constriction
LUNGS: respiratory depression
GI: decrease in propulsive contractions in the 
	small intestine and colon. Spasms.

Opioid Actions

### Slide 5
Pain -- Opioids -- Analgesia

Primary medical use
	entire group - morphine, etc.
	acute and chronic severe pain
	cancer pain --- addiction
Non-medical use = euphoria

### Slide 6
Opiate Dependence

Nationally =
Heroin but also pharmaceutical opioids fentanyl, oxycodone (esp.. Oxycotin)
Male vs. female
Urban

### Slide 7
2.1 million with Opioid Use Disorder (2018)

117,000 new heroin users (2018) 

980,000 persons using at least weekly (1998)

828,000-1 million chronic users of heroin

Only 1,172,000 receiving treatment

(National Household Survey on Drug Abuse, 2018; Office of National Drug Control Policy, 1997; SAMHSA, Office of Applied Studies, National Household Survey on Drug Abuse, 2000 and 2001)

### Slide 8
Abuse of Prescription analgesics has risen
OxyContin, Vicodin, Demerol
Dramatic press coverage
Emergency Department visits 	1994-2001:
	41,687 to 90,232 (117% increase)

Significant diversion and abuse of methadone

DAWN, 2002

### Slide 9
What is the cost to society?

$20 billion per year total cost of heroin abuse

The economic cost of drug use and dependence estimated to be $98 billion (Harwood et al, 1998) 
Figures do not take into account social impact of drug addiction
Crime / legal costs 
Absenteeism from work / unemployment
Welfare / medical costs

### Slide 10
Abuse - culturally disapproved use
Addiction - meaning varies
Dependence - physiological changes,
		maladaptive behavior, neuroadaptation, 		repeat doses or withdrawal
		(see lecture notes for DSM-IV diagnosis)

Basic Terms 1

### Slide 11
Tolerance -
Withdrawal Symptoms - abrupt stop --- 			craving, dysphoria, 						nervous system over-activity
Cross-Tolerance - another drug substitutes
Rebound - abrupt stop --- exaggerated original 	symptoms

Basic Terms 2

### Slide 12
Detoxification - slow taper to prevent 			withdrawal
Withdrawal Symptoms - craving, dysphoria, 	nervous system over-activity
Relapse - return to abuse following full 		detoxification and 	stabilization
"slip" -

Basic Terms 3

### Slide 13
Agonist - stimulates receptor same as abused 	drug

Partial Agonist - stimulates but "ceiling" effect

Antagonist - blocks receptor and prevents 		abused drug effect

Basic Terms 4

### Slide 14
Substance Abuse signs -- General Physician

Medical:  infection, nasal/pulm, scars, drug requests
Behavior: poor school/work, marital, family discord
Laboratory: urine*, blood, (hair **, etc.)

	*    No info regarding tolerance/dependence
	**  huge issues re: privacy issues, validity

### Slide 15
Opioid

Detoxification - agonist, taper and/or clonidine
(transcrainal electro-stimulation -- inc. endorphin)
 Substitution - methadone, buprenorphine
Antagonist - naltrexone
 Relapse Prevention - naltrexone
 New - long-acting buprenorphine, naltrexone

### Slide 16
Withdrawal symptoms

Sweating			
Yawning
Anxiety
Increased BP and respiratory rate
Cravings 
Lacrimation 
Piloerection
Rhinitis
Gastrointestinal symptoms
Abdominal cramps, Diarrhea

### Slide 17
Methadone

Available since 1960's BUT confined to special 
  programs, under federal and state controls.

Primary care and other private physicians unable to
  treat patients with methadone

### Slide 18
Methadone

oral, long-acting and cheap
 "blocking dose"
 Eliminate injection risks
 Normalization of body; health
 Life not centered around heroin
 Reduced crime
 Employment

Several, valuable benefits:

### Slide 23

Combination tablet containing buprenorphine with naloxone - if taken under tongue, predominant buprenorphine effect

plus Naloxone

FDA approved in 1984
	pharmacologic effects

Indications
	opioid - relapse prevention, detoxification
	alcohol (1995) - relapse prevention 			'anticraving'

Specific antagonism of opiate mu 
Competitive antagonism but very tight
Very few AEs (HA, GI, dysphoria)
Oral, relatively long-acting
Non-addicting (no diversion)

Buprenorphine

	naltrexone before discharge

## Source: `04_Acute_and_Safety/Agitation_and_Restraint/_source/Crisis_After_Hours_Protocol.docx`

CRISIS & AFTER-HOURS PROTOCOL

ReConnect Psychiatry Practice LLC  |  Internal Policy & Patient-Facing Reference

Practice Hours & Availability

Regular office hours: [e.g., Monday-Friday, 9:00 AM - 5:00 PM EST]

Telehealth available: Statewide Maine via TherapyNotes Telehealth

After-hours coverage: This is a solo outpatient practice. Dr. Moss is not available for after-hours emergencies. Patients are directed to the resources below.

Patient-Facing Crisis Instructions

The following information is provided to all patients at intake, on the practice website, and via voicemail greeting:

IF YOU ARE IN IMMEDIATE DANGER OR EXPERIENCING A MEDICAL EMERGENCY, CALL 911.

Crisis Resources (Available 24/7)

Local Crisis Stabilization Units (Maine)

Community Health and Counseling - Bangor region

Crisis & Counseling Centers - Augusta, Lewiston, Portland

South Portland CTC (Crisis Triage Center)

Sweetser Crisis Services - Saco

Living Room Crisis Centers - various locations

For the most current list, visit 211maine.org or call 211.

Voicemail Greeting Protocol

The practice voicemail includes the following message during all hours:

"You have reached ReConnect Psychiatry and the office of Dr. Joshua Moss. If you are experiencing a psychiatric emergency, please call 911 or go to your nearest emergency room. You may also reach the 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline by calling or texting 988. For non-urgent matters, please leave a message and we will return your call within one business day."

Internal Protocol: Managing Crisis Contacts During Business Hours

If a patient expresses suicidal ideation during a session:

Conduct immediate risk assessment (intent, plan, means, protective factors)

If imminent risk: coordinate with emergency services (call 911), remain with patient virtually or in-person until handoff is complete

If elevated but not imminent risk: develop or update safety plan collaboratively, increase session frequency, consider medication adjustment, provide crisis resources

Document risk assessment, clinical decision-making, and safety plan in TherapyNotes

If a patient calls or messages with a crisis outside of session:

Voicemail directs to 988, Maine Crisis Line, or 911

If Dr. Moss becomes aware of an urgent message during business hours, return call as soon as possible for brief risk triage

This practice does not provide 24/7 on-call coverage; patients are informed of this at intake

Documentation Requirements

All crisis contacts documented in TherapyNotes within 24 hours

Safety plans saved in patient chart

Emergency contacts verified and updated at each visit

Signed crisis protocol acknowledgment in patient intake packet

Patient Acknowledgment

I understand that ReConnect Psychiatry Practice LLC is a solo outpatient practice that does not provide 24/7 crisis coverage. I have been provided with crisis resources and understand how to access emergency care.

Patient Name (Print): _______________________________________________

Patient Signature: __________________________________________________     Date: _______________

Resource | Contact

988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline | Call or text 988

Crisis Text Line | Text HOME to 741741

Maine Crisis Line | 1-888-568-1112

Maine Statewide Crisis Services | 211 Maine (dial 211)

SAMHSA National Helpline | 1-800-662-4357

Veterans Crisis Line | Call 988, press 1

Nearest Emergency Department | Call 911 or go to nearest ED

## Source: `04_Acute_and_Safety/Violence_Risk/_source/Technical_Review_Critique_2026-05-17.docx`

Technical Review - RSSM R34 Proposal (Track 1)

Received: 2026-05-17 evening Reviewer: External (third-party technical review of the original pre-read) Reviewing: Track 1 (RSSM-forward, 10-vignette pre-read packet) Saved by: Cowork session, 2026-05-17

TL;DR for the Tuesday call: The critique was written against Track 1 (the RSSM-forward 8-page packet, the version superseded tonight). Its scoring is Reviewer Interest: 8/10, Funding Feasibility: 6/10 - and its final verdict is "NOT yet R34-Ready, but 'QI-Pilot First' Ready." In strategic-tier language: this is a Tier C outcome prediction - exactly the contingency the Track 2 prep brief already anticipates. The Track 2 pivot you made tonight (gap-first, RSSM backstage, manual-still-in-draft framing) responds directly to most of the critique's recommendations. Bring the critique's key insights into Tuesday's call as honest acknowledgments, not as defensive responses.

Critique's six most actionable insights (and how to use them Tuesday)

1. Investigator dependence is the #1 scientific risk

Reviewer's exact framing: "A skeptical study section will view this as a 'boutique' intervention that works because of your personal clinical skills."

What to do Tuesday: If Acri raises fidelity or transportability, lead with: "My primary pre-submission concern is investigator dependence. The pilot must prioritize testing how non-developer clinician dyads implement the manual - proving the protocol is the active agent, not the developer." This is a sophisticated services-research move; volunteer it before she names it.

2. Strip to a "Core 6" strategy subset

Reviewer's pick (consistent with your own usage-frequency data): #1 (Joining), #2 (Normalization), #4 (Autonomy-deprivation contextualization), #10 (Three truths), #15 (Eliciting drivers), #28 (Aftercare menu).

What to do Tuesday: If Acri asks about the 28-strategy bank: "For the pilot, we are planning to fidelity-test a Core 6 subset - the highest-utilization strategies from the case series. The full 28 remain in the manual as the clinician's adaptive vocabulary, but the fidelity rating instrument tests the Core 6." This single move addresses the "complexity inflation" deadly sin.

3. Reframe the primary outcome

Reviewer's argument: 30-day all-cause readmission is "hard" but may be insufficiently sensitive to a brief 3-contact intervention confounded by socioeconomic and outpatient-access factors. Family Engagement Index and 90-day aftercare attendance are more directly downstream of what the intervention does.

What to do Tuesday: This is your Question #1 (outcome hierarchy). The critique reinforces your Track 2 framing exactly. Don't pre-commit - let Acri name the outcome. But have the redirect ready: "FAD-GF / FACES-IV as primary, 90-day aftercare attendance as secondary, 30-day readmission as tertiary - what would NIMH find non-controversial?"

4. Reframe the layer-coordination claim as a hypothesis

Reviewer's framing: "Present layer-coordination as a hypothesis to be tested, not a fact." This is the central correction to Track 1's RSSM-forward posture.

What to do Tuesday: This is precisely why we pivoted to Track 2. Hold RSSM nomenclature backstage. If she pulls on the theoretical scaffold, then you can describe the four-layer model - but explicitly as "the conceptual scaffolding the manual is built on; the testable hypothesis the R34 generates data on."

5. The QI-pilot-first contingency

Reviewer's path:

May 19: Consult with Acri -> confirm mechanism

June 15: Biostatistician Co-I committed

July-August: 5-10 case QI pilot under MaineHealth IRB-exempt status

September: Revise manual based on QI data

October: Finalize decision rules in Appendix L (renamed from I)

November: Submit R34 narrative

What to do Tuesday: This is your Tier C contingency. The reviewer is predicting it; don't be surprised if Acri suggests it. "If you advise QI-first, I have a parallel pathway already scoped - IRB-exempt at MaineHealth, 5-10 cases over six weeks, primary aim is clinician adherence to the Core 6." Volunteering this signals you're not married to a single path.

6. Operationalize the abstract language before any submission

The critique's three reframes are clinically tight:

What to do Tuesday: Don't use the abstract phrases in the call. Substitute the operational versions silently. The vignettes packet you're sending already does most of this work - these reframes are for your internal vocabulary discipline.

What the critique misses or gets wrong

"9,000-record ReConnect aftercare database" - the critique echoes the Track 1 framing's overstatement. This is a Maine resource/provider directory, not patient-outcome data. Even the critique mis-frames it as "feasibility backing"; for an R34 reviewer it's implementation infrastructure, not preliminary data. Don't perpetuate the framing in the call.

"Appendix I (Operationalization Framework)" - already renamed Appendix L per R-088 (2026-05-12). Minor.

Branch fit - the critique correctly names Child & Adolescent Services Research Program (Acri's program) but says "if pivot to adult, APPIRB is the appropriate alternative." Your Track 2 framing already leaves this open as Question #2. Good.

Vignette count - critique references "10 case vignettes" (Track 1 inline count). Your actual library is 12; Track 2 selects 3 for the packet.

Five "deadly sins" to avoid in the call

How this changes tonight's send

Nothing. The Track 2 materials (Pre-Read + Vignettes Packet) already align with the critique's recommendations - gap-first framing, manual-in-draft positioning, family functioning as primary outcome question, three crisp consultation questions, no defensive posturing toward existing models. The Gmail draft stays as-is.

What this changes for Tuesday morning

Add three things to your Acri_Meeting_Prep_Brief_2026-05-19.md mental rehearsal:

The investigator-dependence acknowledgment - volunteer it before she names it.

The Core 6 readiness - have the six numbers memorized (#1, #2, #4, #10, #15, #28).

The QI-pilot-first contingency - have the May 19 -> November timeline at instant recall as Tier C.

What this changes for the next 90 days (if Tier A or B outcome)

The critique's roadmap dovetails with the Track 2 prep brief's post-call plan:

Within 24h: thank-you email with three takeaways

Within 30 days: biostatistician verbal commitment (target June 15)

June-August: QI pilot if Tier C; otherwise refine Specific Aims

September-October: finalize decision rules and operationalized language

November: submit R34

This is consistent with the NIMH R34 PAR-25-284 June 16, 2026 deadline identified in tethering-project-grant-scan.md - though if you target the November cycle instead, you trade June for more preliminary data quality.

Sources

Technical Review and Strategic Consultation_ Relational Systems Stabilization Model (RSSM) R34 Proposal.md (uploaded 2026-05-17 evening)

tethering-project-grant-scan.md (2026-03-22, /Users/jm/claude-cowork-dispatch/desktop-recovered-mar2026/)

memory/people/mary-acri.md (/Users/jm/Documents/Claude/Projects/Claude Cowork Coworker/)

The Track 2 materials in ../Acri Meeting/

Abstract Track 1 language | Operationalized | Why it matters

"Clinician-as-container" | "Clinician adherence to role clarity and de-escalation protocols" | Reviewer-readable; fidelity-rateable

"Meaning-making" | "Identification of patient-centered functional goals" | Connects to validated outcome measures

"Signals of safety" | "Documented environmental modifications to reduce autonomic arousal" | Concrete and chart-auditable

Sin | What it sounds like | What to do instead

Investigator dependence | "I've been doing this for years" | "The pilot tests whether non-developer clinicians can deliver fidelity"

Complexity inflation | "We have 28 strategies indexed to 15 modality lineages" | "Core 6 for the pilot fidelity test"

Evidence-gap minimization | "The four-layer model is supported by clinical consensus" | "The four-layer scaffold organizes the manual; the layer-coordination claim is exactly what the R34 generates data on"

Defensive comparison | "Unlike NAVIGATE/MFGP/FFT, which take 6 months..." | "The closest temporal analog is Goldstein 1978; we're trying to replicate it under modern LOS"

Selling the manuscript | Mentioning RSSM by name | Stay backstage; let her pull on it if she's curious

## Source: `11_AI_and_Prompts/_source/Family_Therapy_Communication_Framework_Dr_Moss_Final.docx`

Family Therapy Communication Framework

From Dr. Moss - Adult Inpatient Psychiatry

1. Love, Conflict & Trust

You can't feel that level of anger unless you care. That's where we start. Love and conflict are tied together.
Trauma breaks trust, especially early trauma. Rebuilding trust takes time, consistency, and honest communication. You can't rush it.

2. No One Listens Unless They Feel Heard

The biggest thing-if there's one thing to take away-it's this: no one listens unless they feel heard.
Start conversations by listening. Let your partner feel heard before responding. That's the key to being understood.

3. Check-Ins and Permission

We don't just know how to do relationships-we learn from how we were raised. So we build systems:
- Check in with each other after time apart. Share how much emotional energy you have.
- Ask before sharing something heavy: "Can I vent for a minute?" That small step can prevent overload.

4. The Three Relationship Dances

All couples fall into patterns. I call them dances:
1. Push-Pull: One partner needs closeness, the other needs space. It escalates when no one gets what they need.
2. The Blame Dance: Arguing just to prove who's right. No one wins.
3. The Avoidance Dance: Both disconnect. Nothing gets resolved.
Recognize the dance. Name it. Use a safe word. Step away and come back later.

5. Respect Lines (Not Boundaries)

Boundaries sound cold-like a wall. I prefer 'respect lines'.
They're not about controlling the other person. They're about saying, 'When this happens, I feel this way.'
Use 'I' statements. Tell them how it lands on you. Let them decide what they do with it.

6. Anger is a Feeling. What Matters is How You Express It.

Feeling anger is okay. It's healthy. Most people only know dangerous or explosive anger.
Learn to name it early. Don't wait until it boils over. That's what couples work is for-helping each other feel safe with hard emotions.

7. Trauma and Triggers

PTSD means both of you have triggers. Learn your own, and learn each other's.
Triggers are reminders of fear. You can't avoid all of them-but you can learn to respect them.
And dissociation? It's like your brain temporarily leaves the driver's seat. That's normal in trauma.

8. Reconnect. Don't Just Survive.

It's not just about putting out fires. It's about finding joy again.
Do the things you loved doing together. Laugh. Share meals. Play.
Healing happens when you're safe and connected-not just when you're problem-solving.

9. Learn As You Go

You're not expected to have all the answers. Keep growing. Some resources:
- Esther Perel (relationships)
- Russell Barkley (ADHD & parenting)
- Gabor Mate (trauma & addiction)
- Codependent No More by Melody Beattie

10. Communication Exercise: The 24-Hour Reflection

This is a simple but powerful way to practice listening and talking openly:

- Set a timer for 5 to 10 minutes. One partner talks. The other listens.
- The listener may only ask clarifying questions-no follow-ups, comments, or reactions.
- After time is up, switch roles.

You can talk about anything: work, family, fears, relationship issues, or even the weather. There are no rules about the content.

- Once both have spoken, **do not talk about what was said for 24 hours**.
- This allows space for reflection, lets emotions settle, and gives each person time to process before discussing further.

It sounds easy, but five minutes of uninterrupted speaking-and listening-can feel long. The goal is to build space, trust, and calm into your communication.

Final Thought

Healing doesn't mean going back to who you were. It means building something more solid with what you've learned together.
Start small. Stay consistent. Be patient with the process-and with each other.

## Source: `11_AI_and_Prompts/_source/RSSM_Family_Handouts_v10.docx`

ReConnect Family Handouts (v10)

A print-ready companion for families and caregivers supporting psychiatric stabilization.

How To Use This Packet

 Use one handout at a time based on the current stress point.

 Keep language short, calm, and consistent.

 Repeat key messages; do not overload with explanation during escalation.

 Revisit handouts weekly and update actions based on what is actually working.

 Handouts 1-7 cover the foundation of stabilization and recovery. Handouts 8-15 address the longer arc: medication, burnout, relapse, and planning ahead.

Handout 1: Why Structure Comes Before Progress

Use when: family wants immediate insight work, major decisions, or rapid behavior change during instability.

Key message: recovery begins with stabilization, not motivation.

When sleep, routine, and emotional regulation are unstable, the brain cannot reliably learn, problem-solve, or tolerate stress.

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Protect sleep and wake-time consistency.

 [ ] Keep daily rhythm predictable.

 [ ] Reduce avoidable stimulation and conflict.

 [ ] Let the team prioritize foundation work first.

One-sentence script: "This is not delay - this is treatment."

Handout 2: Why Talking Is Not Always Helpful Right Away

Use when: conversations keep escalating, repeating, or ending in conflict.

Key message: less verbal intensity often helps more than more verbal effort.

When someone is highly anxious, agitated, or shut down, long conversations can increase distress instead of reducing it.

What Helps In The Moment

 Fewer words

 Slower pace

 Clear, simple statements

 Reassurance through consistency

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Pause when activation rises.

 [ ] Use brief statements and stop over-explaining.

 [ ] Return to problem-solving after regulation improves.

One-sentence script: "We can talk this through after everyone is calmer."

Handout 3: How Families Help Without Doing Everything

Use when: family support starts to become over-monitoring or over-correction.

Key message: stabilization needs calm structure, not total control.

Helping Does Not Mean

 Constant monitoring

 Repeated questioning

 Correcting all day

 Solving every problem

Helping Does Mean

 Keeping routines predictable

 Responding calmly and consistently

 Letting the treatment team hold medical decisions

 Stepping back when emotions are running high

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Choose one routine to protect daily.

 [ ] Reduce one high-frequency monitoring behavior.

 [ ] Identify one responsibility to shift back to patient capacity.

One-sentence script: "I will support your structure, not control every detail."

Handout 4: Understanding Family Stress And High Emotion

Use when: fear is showing up as criticism, overprotection, or urgency.

Key message: fear-driven reactions are understandable and can still destabilize care.

These responses often come from love and alarm. They can also increase stress and slow recovery if intensity stays high.

What Helps More Than Control

 Predictable check-ins

 Clear roles

 Space for independence once stability returns

 Trusting staged progress

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Set one predictable check-in time per day.

 [ ] Clarify who handles what in the home.

 [ ] Replace one urgency-based response with a calm limit.

One-sentence script: "Calm and consistency help more than pressure."

Handout 5: Why Progress Can Look Slow

Use when: family interprets setbacks as treatment failure.

Key message: non-linear progress is expected in psychiatric recovery.

Setbacks usually signal that demand exceeded foundation, not that progress is impossible.

Progress Often Looks Like

 Better sleep before better mood

 Fewer crises before more independence

 Consistency before confidence

 Small steps that hold before larger changes

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Track one "holding" gain (not just symptom change).

 [ ] Keep structure stable even when symptoms briefly improve.

 [ ] Avoid sudden demand increases after a good day.

One-sentence script: "Slow progress that holds is better than fast progress that collapses."

Handout 6: What Families Can Expect From The Treatment Team

Use when: family is unsure why the team is setting limits or sequencing care.

Key message: timing decisions are clinical, not dismissive.

Families can expect the team to:

 Prioritize safety and stability

 Explain current focus and rationale

 Communicate next steps clearly

 Set limits respectfully

 Involve families when clinically appropriate

You may hear:

 "Not yet"

 "Let's slow this down"

 "We need to stabilize sleep or routine first"

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Ask for the current layer focus.

 [ ] Ask what needs to stabilize before next-step goals.

 [ ] Write down the short-term plan in plain language.

One-sentence script: "Help us understand sequence, not just symptoms."

Handout 7: What To Do After Discharge

Use when: family is planning first 2-4 weeks post-discharge.

Key message: discharge is a transition phase, not an endpoint.

What Helps Recovery Hold

 Consistent wake times and routines

 No major decisions in early transition

 Early response to warning signs

 Clear contact/escalation plan

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Post a simple daily structure in the home.

 [ ] Define who to call for early warning signs.

 [ ] Schedule first follow-up supports before discharge week ends.

One-sentence script: "If instability returns, we check the foundation early and adjust fast."

Handout 8: Sleep Is The Foundation

Use when: family is focused on mood or behavior while sleep remains unstable.

Key message: sleep disruption is not a symptom - it is a driver. Protecting sleep is one of the most important things a family can do.

When sleep is disrupted, the brain's ability to regulate emotion, make decisions, and tolerate stress is impaired - regardless of medication, therapy, or willpower. In psychiatric recovery, sleep often improves before mood does, and protecting that improvement is critical.

What Families Can Do

 Protect a consistent wake time - this is more important than bedtime

 Keep the home quieter and dimmer in the last hour before bed

 Avoid late-evening conflict, difficult conversations, or high-stimulation activities

 Do not interpret daytime sleepiness as laziness - medication sedation and recovery fatigue are real

What To Avoid

 Changing sleep schedules after one good night

 Allowing screens and stimulation to override the wind-down period

 Assuming sleep will "fix itself" once mood improves - it usually works the other way around

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Agree on a consistent wake time and protect it for 7 days straight.

 [ ] Reduce household noise and light after 9 PM.

 [ ] Report any sleep changes (better or worse) to the treatment team.

One-sentence script: "If sleep is not stable, nothing else holds."

Handout 9: Medication And Your Relationship

Use when: medication side effects are affecting daily life, intimacy, or the family dynamic - and no one is talking about it.

Key message: medication happens to the whole family, not just the person taking it. Side effects that go unaddressed can quietly damage relationships.

Common Effects That Families Notice

 Energy and participation: Sedation may mean your loved one sleeps more or cannot participate in daily routines the way they used to. This is not laziness - it is a real physiological effect.

 Weight changes: Several medication classes cause significant weight gain. This affects self-image, confidence, and how your loved one feels in the relationship.

 Sexual side effects: Many psychiatric medications affect desire, arousal, or ability to reach orgasm. This is the effect couples are least likely to discuss and most likely to be harmed by.

 Emotional blunting: Some medications reduce the full range of emotional experience. Your loved one may seem "flat" - not depressed, but not fully present either.

What Helps

 Name the side effects out loud - to each other and to the prescriber

 Ask specifically whether alternatives exist if side effects are affecting daily life or intimacy

 Understand that non-adherence due to side effects is a clinical problem, not a relational betrayal

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Have one honest conversation about how medication is affecting both of you.

 [ ] Write down side effects to bring to the next prescriber appointment.

 [ ] If intimacy has been affected, name it without blame - and ask for clinical guidance.

One-sentence script: "The medication that creates stability should not destroy the relationship it is meant to protect."

Handout 10: The Contagion Of Calm

Use when: the household feels emotionally reactive - everyone's stress is feeding everyone else's.

Key message: calm is contagious. Your nervous system affects theirs, and theirs affects yours. When you regulate yourself, you are doing something directly therapeutic.

Nervous systems in close proximity influence each other. Research on physiological coupling shows that when one person in a dyad is regulated, the other's body tends to follow. This is not a metaphor - it is a measurable biological process.

This means that when a family member stays calm during a difficult moment, they are not just "keeping it together." They are providing co-regulation: their steady nervous system is helping their loved one's nervous system settle.

What Co-Regulation Looks Like In Practice

 Slowing your own breathing when your loved one is agitated

 Lowering your voice instead of matching their volume

 Staying physically present without rushing to fix or solve

 Tolerating silence instead of filling it with reassurance

What Works Against Co-Regulation

 Matching their intensity with your own urgency

 Trying to talk someone out of their feelings before the body has calmed

 Leaving the room in frustration (brief pauses are fine - abrupt departures are not)

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Practice one deliberate slow-down during a tense moment this week.

 [ ] Notice when your own body is escalating - tight chest, fast speech, clenched jaw - and pause before responding.

 [ ] After a calm moment that went well, name what you did: "I slowed down, and it helped."

One-sentence script: "I cannot regulate your emotions for you, but I can make it easier for your body to do it."

Handout 11: When Caring Becomes Your Whole Identity

Use when: the caregiver has lost sight of who they are outside the caregiving role.

Key message: caregiver role drift happens slowly and to almost everyone. It is not a failure of love. It is a predictable pattern - and understanding it is the first step to managing it.

When someone you love has a serious mental health condition, you step in during the crisis - managing medications, watching for warning signs, holding everything together. This is appropriate. The problem begins when these temporary crisis roles become permanent. Without anyone planning it, you stop being a partner, parent, or sibling and become something closer to a case manager.

Signs Of Role Drift

 You check on their medication, sleep, or mood multiple times a day - even when they are stable

 You feel anxious when away from them, as though something will go wrong without your supervision

 Your loved one has started to resist your help - not because they do not need it, but because it feels controlling

 You feel resentful, exhausted, or trapped - and then immediately guilty for feeling those things

How To Start Pulling Back

 Name it. Say out loud that role drift has happened. Naming it reduces shame for both of you.

 Identify what they can do for themselves. Imperfect self-management is better than perfect external management - because it builds capacity.

 Tolerate imperfection. When you step back, they may not do things the way you would. This is not relapse. This is recovery, which is messy by definition.

 Reclaim something that is yours. Restart one activity or friendship that existed before caregiving consumed everything.

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Circle three things on your daily caregiving list that your loved one could handle - even imperfectly.

 [ ] Hand one of those tasks back this week, with support but without taking it back.

 [ ] Do one thing for yourself that has nothing to do with caregiving.

One-sentence script: "I am going to support your recovery, not replace your autonomy."

Handout 12: When Relapse Hurts The Relationship

Use when: after a relapse, when trust has been damaged and the family is struggling with anger, grief, or withdrawal.

Key message: relapse is not just a medical event - it is a relational event. The damage to trust is real, and it deserves to be named and addressed, not ignored.

When your loved one relapses after a period of stability, something inside the relationship breaks - not just the medical progress, but the trust that had been carefully rebuilt. If relapse feels like betrayal, even though you know it is not their fault, that feeling is normal.

The Relapse-Trust Cycle

Relapse occurs -> Your trust erodes -> You withdraw emotionally to protect yourself -> Your loved one senses the withdrawal and feels shame -> Shame and reduced support increase the risk of another relapse -> The cycle repeats.

The key is interrupting the cycle, not assigning blame for it.

How To Rebuild

 Do a relapse review together with your clinician - what were the warning signs? What could each of you do differently?

 Re-establish small agreements. Small kept promises rebuild trust faster than large unkept ones. "I will take my medication every morning and tell you if I miss a dose" is better than "I will never let this happen again."

 Allow grief. You lost something in this relapse. Give yourself permission to grieve - the stability, the hope, the version of the future you had been building.

 Separate the person from the illness. Your loved one did not choose to relapse. When you can hold the anger at the illness and the love for the person at the same time, the relationship has space to recover.

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Name what trust was most damaged - stability, communication, or your own judgment.

 [ ] Agree on one small, specific commitment each of you will keep this week.

 [ ] Ask the treatment team for a structured relapse prevention plan that includes both of you.

One-sentence script: "Trust is rebuilt by structure, not by promises."

Handout 13: Recognizing Burnout In Yourself

Use when: the caregiver is running on empty - exhausted in a way that sleep does not fix, going through the motions, or feeling numb.

Key message: caregiver burnout is not weakness. It is what happens to the body and mind when they carry too much for too long without support. It deserves treatment in its own right.

The Warning Signs - By Stage

Early (months 1-6 of sustained stress):

 Difficulty sleeping even when you have time

 Increased irritability - snapping at small things

 Withdrawal from friends and activities

 Feeling like you are always "on alert"

Middle (months 6-18):

 Persistent fatigue that rest does not fix

 Loss of interest in things you used to enjoy

 Physical symptoms: headaches, stomach problems, muscle pain

 Resentment toward your loved one - followed by guilt about the resentment

Late (beyond 18 months):

 Emotional numbness - feeling nothing

 Fantasies about leaving or disappearing

 Neglecting your own health

 Depression, anxiety, or panic attacks

If you recognize yourself in the late signs, please talk to a clinician - for yourself, not just for your loved one.

What Helps

 Acknowledge it. Say the word "burnout" to yourself, to a friend, to your clinician.

 Get your own care. A therapist, a support group (NAMI Family-to-Family is an excellent option), even regular check-ins with your primary care doctor.

 Reclaim something small. One activity per week that is yours. This is a clinical recommendation, not optional self-care advice.

 Redistribute the load. With the treatment team, identify what you are doing that someone else could do.

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Identify where you fall on the burnout progression: early, middle, or late.

 [ ] Name one thing you could stop doing or delegate this week.

 [ ] Schedule one appointment or activity that is for your own well-being.

One-sentence script: "You cannot sustain this if you are breaking down. Getting help for yourself is not selfish - it is necessary."

Handout 14: When Partners Step Back

Use when: a partner or caregiver is considering what they can realistically sustain - and questioning whether they can continue at the current level.

Key message: stepping back is not abandonment. It is a thoughtful adjustment to what you can provide. Setting limits is what makes long-term presence possible.

If you are reading this, you may be asking questions that feel impossible: Can I keep doing this? Am I allowed to set limits? Does wanting to step back make me a terrible person?

These questions are legitimate. They are not signs of failure. They are signs that you have been carrying something heavy for a long time and are trying to figure out what is sustainable.

Stepping Back Can Mean

 Reducing daily caregiving tasks while maintaining emotional presence

 Establishing clear boundaries about what you will and will not do

 Asking others - family, friends, professionals - to carry more of the support

 Taking temporary distance while remaining connected

Before You Decide

 Is there still a partnership underneath the caregiving?

 Have you tried setting limits before reaching the breaking point?

 Are you carrying this alone, or have you used all available support?

 Is your physical or emotional safety at risk?

If You Stay

Staying does not mean staying the same way. Sustainable presence requires explicit boundaries, your own care, honest communication, and a crisis plan.

If You Leave

Tell them directly - not during a crisis. Involve the treatment team so support can be put in place. Ensure safety. Allow yourself to grieve.

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Identify where you are: "I need to restructure" versus "I need to leave."

 [ ] Name what you would need in order to stay sustainably - and say it out loud.

 [ ] Talk to a therapist or trusted person about what you are considering, before making a decision in crisis.

One-sentence script: "There is no decision here without cost. The question is which costs you can bear."

Handout 15: Planning Ahead - Psychiatric Advance Directives

Use when: the family is stable enough to plan for future crises - ideally during a good period, not during or immediately after a crisis.

Key message: a psychiatric advance directive gives your loved one a voice in their own treatment during their most vulnerable moments. It reduces fear, conflict, and guesswork for everyone.

A Psychiatric Advance Directive (PAD) is a legal document that allows your loved one to state their treatment preferences in advance - while they are well - in case a future crisis impairs their ability to make or communicate decisions. Think of it like a medical advance directive, but specifically for psychiatric care.

What A PAD Can Include

 Medications that have worked well and medications to avoid

 Preferred treatment settings and facilities

 De-escalation techniques that help when agitated

 Early warning signs that a crisis may be developing

 A designated decision-maker to act on their behalf

 Practical arrangements: childcare, pet care, employer notification, finances

How Families Can Help

 Start the conversation during a stable period: "Can we plan for what works best when things are hard?"

 Support without taking over - the PAD is the patient's document

 Keep a copy accessible - not locked away where no one can reach it at 2 AM

 Share it with the treatment team so it is in the medical record before it is needed

Family Actions This Week

 [ ] Ask the treatment team or social worker about PAD forms for your state.

 [ ] Begin a simple list of what has worked and what has not worked in past crises.

 [ ] Identify who your loved one would want as their healthcare decision-maker.

One-sentence script: "The best time to plan for a crisis is when you are not in one."

Companion Worksheets

Figure 45A. Family Anxiety Heat Map - role-by-time tracking of anxiety redistribution

Figure 45B. Vigilance Ledger - structured worksheet for sentinel monitoring and load transfer

Crisis Resources

If you or your loved one is in crisis:

 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline - call or text 988

 Crisis Text Line - text HOME to 741741

 Emergency Department - go to your nearest ED or call 911

 NAMI Helpline - 1-800-950-NAMI (6264)

These resources are available 24/7.

Final Note For Families

You do not have to do everything right.

You only need to help keep the ground steady while recovery rebuilds layer by layer.

From the RSS Family Support Program - Joshua Moss, MD

Part of the Relational Systems Stabilization clinical manual, First Edition.

www.reconnectpsychiatry.com
